Now that we have discussed linearized gravity in flat spacetime, let us next consider a general background. We can proceed in the same way we did in Section
2
by expanding the Einstein-Hilbert action around a background metric
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
. However, such expansion at the level of the action needs to be up to second order in the metric perturbation, and for a general background, this can become quite involved. We will take a different approach in this section to avoid an excess of mathematical details. The metric expansion will be made at the level of the equations of motion (Einstein’s equation), meaning that we will keep terms only up to the first order in the perturbation. Then, after a convenient choice of gauge, we can write an action whose variation leads to such a linearized equation. For the full action expansion up to second order around a general background, we refer the reader to Ref.
[
13
]
.

The final goal of this section is to describe the interaction of gravitons with a classical static Newtonian potential. This means that, although we will begin by considering a general background
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
, we will eventually take the Newtonian limit, where
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
is described by another small (now fixed) perturbation to Minkowski spacetime. Thus, the quantization procedure of the gravitational radiation discussed in Section
3
will remain valid for our purposes. In this sense, we will not discuss the subtleties associated with quantum fields in general curved spacetimes
[
160
,
18
]
.

Finally, a word on notation. In this section, we will need to distinguish between curvature symbols and tensors that are associated with
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
, the background metric, and those associated with
g_{\mu\nu}
, the
full
spacetime metric that includes the propagating gravitational radiation. For the latter, we will simply continue to use the same notation as we have been so far, but for the Christoffel symbols associated with the background metric, for instance, we will denote
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\rho}[\gamma]
,
{R^{\rho}}_{\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma]
for the background Riemann tensor, and so on. The covariant derivative compatible with
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
will be denoted by
D_{\mu}
.

4.1
Linearized Einstein’s equation

Suppose that
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
is a known solution to Einstein’s equation (in vacuum, for simplicity) and we wish to study small metric perturbations around it. In that case, the full metric tensor takes the form
g_{\mu\nu}=\gamma_{\mu\nu}+h_{\mu\nu}
, where
h_{\mu\nu}
denotes the perturbation we are interested in, with
\absolutevalue{h_{\mu\nu}}\ll\absolutevalue{\gamma_{\mu\nu}}
. In the same spirit as Eq. (
2.9
), we can consider a one-parameter family of solutions,

g_{\mu\nu}(\epsilon)=\gamma_{\mu\nu}+\epsilon h_{\mu\nu},

(4.1)

such that, once again,

h_{\mu\nu}=\evaluated{\dv{g_{\mu\nu}(\epsilon)}{\epsilon}}_{\epsilon=0}.

(4.2)

The one-parameter family of solutions satisfies Einstein’s equation, which is a non-linear operation of the form

\mathcal{E}[g(\epsilon)]=0.

(4.3)

Differentiating this equation with respect to
\epsilon
and setting
\epsilon=0
leads to a linear equation for
h_{\mu\nu}
, which is the desired linearized Einstein’s equation.

Einstein’s equation in vacuum reads

R_{\sigma\nu}=0.

(4.4)

The Ricci tensor depends on
\epsilon
through
g_{\mu\nu}(\epsilon)
. A linearized equation is then obtained by

\evaluated{\dv{R_{\sigma\nu}}{\epsilon}}_{\epsilon=0}=0.

(4.5)

Now, recall that the Riemann tensor is defined such that

\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}V^{\rho}-\nabla_{\nu}\nabla_{\mu}V^{\rho}={R^{\rho}}_{\sigma\mu\nu}V^{\sigma},

(4.6)

for some arbitrary vector
V^{\mu}
. The covariant derivative
\nabla_{\mu}
, associated with
g_{\mu\nu}(\epsilon)
, can be related to the covariant derivative
D_{\mu}
, associated with
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
, through the tensor field (see Appendix
7
)

{C^{\rho}}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\rho\sigma}(\epsilon)\quantity[D_\mu g_{\nu\sigma}(\epsilon)+D_\nu g_{\sigma\mu}(\epsilon)-D_\sigma g_{\mu\nu}(\epsilon)].

(4.7)

Explicitly, we find

\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}V^{\rho}-\nabla_{\nu}\nabla_{\mu}V^{\rho}=D_{\mu}D_{\nu}V^{\rho}-D_{\nu}D_{\mu}V^{\rho}+\quantity(2D_{[\mu}{C^\rho}_{\nu]\sigma}+2{C^\rho}_{\delta[\mu}{C^\delta}_{\nu]\sigma})V^{\sigma},

(4.8)

and thus

{R^{\rho}}_{\sigma\mu\nu}={R^{\rho}}_{\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma]+2D_{[\mu}{C^{\rho}}_{\nu]\sigma}+2{C^{\rho}}_{\delta[\mu}{C^{\delta}}_{\nu]\sigma}.

(4.9)

The Ricci tensor associated with
g_{\mu\nu}(\epsilon)
is given by

\displaystyle R_{\sigma\nu}=R_{\sigma\nu}[\gamma]+2D_{[\mu}{C^{\mu}}_{\nu]\sigma}+2{C^{\mu}}_{\delta[\mu}{C^{\delta}}_{\nu]\sigma}.

(4.10)

Since
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
is assumed to be an exact solution of the vacuum Einstein equation, we must have
R_{\sigma\nu}[\gamma]=0
and

R_{\sigma\nu}=-2D_{[\nu}{C^{\mu}}_{\mu]\sigma}+2{C^{\mu}}_{\delta[\mu}{C^{\delta}}_{\nu]\sigma}.

(4.11)

Plugging this result back into Eq. (
4.5
) and using Eqs. (
4.2
) and (
4.7
) leads to the linear equation for
h_{\mu\nu}
,

-\frac{1}{2}D_{\nu}D_{\sigma}h-\frac{1}{2}D^{\mu}D_{\mu}h_{\sigma\nu}+D^{\mu}D_{(\nu}h_{\sigma)\mu}=0,

(4.12)

where
h=\gamma^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu}
. Note that in the limit of flat spacetime, where
\gamma_{\mu\nu}\to\eta_{\mu\nu}
and
D_{\mu}\to\partial_{\mu}
, we recover Eq. (
2.8
).

We can rewrite

\displaystyle D^{\mu}D_{(\nu}h_{\sigma)\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}D_{\nu}D^{\mu}h_{\sigma\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\quantity( D^\mu D_\nu- D_\nu D^\mu)h_{\sigma\mu}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}D_{\sigma}D^{\mu}h_{\nu\mu}+\frac{1}{2}\quantity( D^\mu D_\sigma- D_\sigma D^\mu)h_{\nu\mu}

\displaystyle=D_{(\nu}D^{\mu}h_{\sigma)\mu}-R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma]h^{\rho\mu}.

(4.13)

Also, since
h
is a scalar,
D_{\nu}D_{\sigma}h=D_{\sigma}D_{\nu}h
, and thus

\displaystyle D_{(\nu}D^{\mu}h_{\sigma)\mu}-\frac{1}{2}D_{\nu}D_{\sigma}h

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}D_{\nu}\quantity( D^\mu h_{\sigma\mu}-\frac{1}{2} D_\sigma h)+\frac{1}{2}D_{\sigma}\quantity( D^\mu h_{\nu\mu}-\frac{1}{2} D_\nu h)

\displaystyle=D_{(\nu}D^{\mu}\bar{h}_{\sigma)\mu},

(4.14)

where

\bar{h}_{\mu\nu}=h_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{\mu\nu}h.

(4.15)

Finally, one is left with

D_{(\nu}D^{\mu}\bar{h}_{\sigma)\mu}-\frac{1}{2}D^{\mu}D_{\mu}h_{\sigma\nu}-R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma]h^{\rho\mu}=0.

(4.16)

This is the linearized Einstein’s equation in curved spacetime.

4.2
TT gauge

Let us now discuss gauge invariance in this more general case. It turns out that the equations of motion are still left invariant by the gauge transformation (
2.12
). The difference now is that
g_{\mu\nu}(0)=\gamma_{\mu\nu}
, and so the gauge transformation takes the form

h_{\mu\nu}^{\prime}=h_{\mu\nu}+D_{\mu}\varepsilon_{\nu}+D_{\nu}\varepsilon_{\mu},

(4.17)

for some arbitrary vector
\varepsilon_{\mu}
. Once again, gauge freedom allows one to simplify the equations of motion for
h_{\mu\nu}
. For instance, starting with any given
h_{\mu\nu}
such that
D^{\nu}\bar{h}_{\mu\nu}\neq 0
in general, one can perform a gauge transformation for which the new perturbation will satisfy

D^{\nu}\bar{h}^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}=D^{\nu}\bar{h}_{\mu\nu}+D^{\nu}D_{\nu}\varepsilon_{\mu},

(4.18)

where we used the fact that
R_{\mu\nu}[\gamma]=0
.
We may then choose
\varepsilon^{\mu}
to satisfy

D^{\nu}D_{\nu}\varepsilon_{\mu}=-D^{\nu}\bar{h}_{\mu\nu},

(4.19)

such that, for the perturbation in the new gauge, we now have
D^{\nu}\bar{h}^{\rm L}_{\mu\nu}=0
, which is the generalization of the Lorenz condition to curved spacetime.

In this gauge, the linearized Einstein equation (
4.16
) becomes

D^{\mu}D_{\mu}h^{\rm L}_{\sigma\nu}+2R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma]h_{\rm L}^{\rho\mu}=0,

(4.20)

with the metric perturbation satisfying the gauge condition

D^{\nu}h^{\rm L}_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}D_{\mu}h^{\rm L}=0.

(4.21)

In the limit of flat spacetime, Eq. (
4.20
) reduces to
\Box h^{\rm L}_{\sigma\nu}=0
.
In that limit
, as we saw in Section
2
, there is a residual gauge freedom; i.e., one can perform a further gauge transformation parametrized by a vector
\xi^{\nu}
as long as
\Box\xi_{\nu}=0
, such that both the gauge condition and the equation of motion remain satisfied. Then, one uses this freedom to choose
h=0
and
h_{0i}=0
, which, together with the equations of motion, implies
h_{00}=0
.

In the case of a curved background, however, this is not generally possible. We can try to perform such additional gauge transformation parametrized by a vector
\xi^{\nu}
satisfying
D^{\mu}D_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=0
(which is necessary for keeping Eq. (
4.21
) satisfied), but now the left-hand side of the equation of motion (
4.20
) transforms to

\displaystyle D^{\mu}D_{\mu}h^{\prime}_{\sigma\nu}+2R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma]h^{\prime\rho\mu}

\displaystyle=D^{\mu}D_{\mu}h^{\rm L}_{\sigma\nu}+2R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma]h_{\rm L}^{\rho\mu}

\displaystyle-\quantity{D^\mu\qty(R_{\mu\sigma\rho\nu}[\gamma]+R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma])}\xi^{\rho}.

(4.22)

We can see that the term on the last line of Eq. (
4.2
) prevents us from making such a gauge transformation while simultaneously keeping the equations of motion invariant.

Let us see what happens to this term when the background itself can be viewed as a small perturbation of Minkowski spacetime,

\gamma_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}+h^{(B)}_{\mu\nu},

(4.23)

with
|h^{(B)}_{\mu\nu}|\ll 1
as usual. Then a direct calculation yields

\displaystyle D^{\mu}\quantity{R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma]+R_{\mu\sigma\rho\nu}[\gamma]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\sigma}\Box h^{(B)}_{\rho\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\partial^{\mu}h^{(B)}_{\mu\sigma}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}\partial^{\mu}h^{(B)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}\Box h^{(B)}_{\rho\sigma}-\partial_{\rho}\Box h^{(B)}_{\nu\sigma}-\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\sigma}\partial^{\mu}h^{(B)}_{\rho\mu}

\displaystyle=0,

(4.24)

where we used the fact that, since
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
is a solution to Einstein’s equation in vacuum, the background perturbation
h^{(B)}_{\mu\nu}
must satisfy the linearized equation (
2.8
).

In other words,
when the background metric can be treated as a small perturbation of Minkowski spacetime
, the term preventing us from performing the residual gauge transformation on
h_{\mu\nu}
vanishes up to first order in
h^{(B)}_{\mu\nu}
. One is then allowed to choose
\xi^{\mu}
satisfying
D^{\mu}D_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}=0
, such that
h=0
and
h_{0i}=0
. The gauge condition then becomes

D_{\mu}h^{\mu\nu}=0.

(4.25)

Particularly, the
\nu=0
component of this equation reads

\partial_{0}h^{00}+\quantity{\Gamma_{\mu 0}^\mu[\gamma]+\Gamma_{00}^0[\gamma]}h^{00}+\Gamma_{ij}^{0}[\gamma]h^{ij}=0.

(4.26)

Explicitly, one has

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{0}[\gamma]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\quantity(\partial_\mu h^{(B)}_{\nu 0}+\partial_\nu h^{(B)}_{0\mu}-\partial_0h^{(B)}_{\mu\nu}),

(4.27a)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\mu}[\gamma]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\nu}h^{(B)}.

(4.27b)

If the metric perturbation is static
, one obtains
\Gamma_{\mu 0}^{\mu}[\gamma]=\Gamma_{00}^{0}[\gamma]=0
. Additionally,
if
h^{(B)}_{0i}=0
, we also have
\Gamma_{ij}^{0}[\gamma]=0
, and thus

\partial_{0}h^{00}=0.

(4.28)

Then, since
h_{00}
is just a constant, one can choose suitable boundary conditions such that
h_{00}=0
.

In summary,
for a background metric which is a small static perturbation to flat spacetime with
h^{(B)}_{0i}=0
, the Einstein equation for
h_{\mu\nu}
reads

D^{\mu}D_{\mu}h^{\rm TT}_{ij}+2R_{ikjl}[\gamma]h_{\rm TT}^{kl}=0,

(4.29)

with the TT gauge conditions

\displaystyle D_{\mu}h_{\rm TT}^{\mu\nu}=0,

(4.30a)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu\nu}h^{\rm TT}_{\mu\nu}=0,

(4.30b)

\displaystyle h^{\rm TT}_{0\mu}=0.

(4.30c)

Particularly, this shows that we can impose the TT gauge conditions when the background is described in the Newtonian limit, for instance.

Finally, note that the linearized vacuum equation in TT gauge can be obtained from the extremization of the action
22
22

22

The normalization factor was chosen to match the linearized Einstein-Hilbert action in the limit of flat spacetime. Furthermore, we note that this action matches the one presented in Ref.
[
13
]
(which was obtained from the second-order expansion at the level of the action from the beginning) if we consider the TT gauge and match the normalization conventions.

S_{\rm EH}=\frac{1}{32\pi}\int\differential^{4}x\,\quantity(\frac{1}{2}h^{\rm TT}_{ij}D^\mu D_\mu h_{\rm TT}^{ij}+h^{\rm TT}_{ij}R^{ikjl}[\gamma]h^{\rm TT}_{kl}).

(4.31)

Setting
\delta S_{\rm EH}=0
immediately leads to Eq. (
4.29
).

4.3
Newtonian limit

The Newtonian limit of general relativity is described by Eq. (
4.23
), for which case we have

h^{(B)}_{\mu\nu}=-2\phi\delta_{\mu\nu},

(4.32)

with
\phi=\phi(\mathbf{x})
being the time-independent Newtonian potential. Then we can show that

h^{\rm TT}_{ij}R^{ikjl}[\gamma]h^{\rm TT}_{kl}=-2\phi(\partial_{k}h^{\rm TT}_{ij})(\partial^{i}h_{\rm TT}^{jk})+\textrm{boundary terms},

(4.33)

where we used the gauge conditions

0=\gamma_{\mu\nu}h_{\rm TT}^{\mu\nu}=(1-2\phi)\delta_{ij}h_{\rm TT}^{ij}\implies\delta_{ij}h_{\rm TT}^{ij}=0,

(4.34a)

and

0=\phi\,D_{\mu}h_{\rm TT}^{\mu\nu}=\phi\,\partial_{\mu}h^{\mu\nu}+O(\phi^{2})\implies\phi\,\partial_{i}h^{ij}=0.

(4.34b)

Additionally, for the background metric given by Eq. (
4.23
), we have, to first order in
h^{(B)}_{\mu\nu}
,

\displaystyle h^{\rm TT}_{ij}D_{\mu}D^{\mu}h_{\rm TT}^{ij}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\mu\nu}h^{\rm TT}_{ij}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}h_{\rm TT}^{ij}+2h^{\rm TT}_{ij}(\partial^{\mu}\Gamma_{\mu k}^{i}[\gamma])h_{\rm TT}^{kj}

\displaystyle-h^{\rm TT}_{ij}\eta^{\mu\nu}\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}[\gamma]\partial_{\lambda}h_{\rm TT}^{ij}+4h^{\rm TT}_{ij}\Gamma_{\mu k}^{i}[\gamma]\partial^{\mu}h_{\rm TT}^{kj}.

(4.35)

In the Newtonian limit, the only non-vanishing Christoffel symbols are

\displaystyle\Gamma_{00}^{i}[\gamma]

\displaystyle=\partial^{i}\phi,

(4.36a)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0i}^{0}[\gamma]=\Gamma_{i0}^{0}[\gamma]

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\phi,

(4.36b)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{ij}^{k}[\gamma]

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}\partial^{k}\phi-\delta_{j}^{k}\partial_{i}\phi-\delta_{i}^{k}\partial_{j}\phi.

(4.36c)

Then, after some manipulations involving integration by parts and the gauge conditions, while keeping only terms up to the first order in
\phi
, we find

\displaystyle S_{\rm EH}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{64\pi}\int\differential^{4}x\,\gamma^{\mu\nu}h^{\rm TT}_{ij}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}h_{\rm TT}^{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{64\pi}\int\differential^{4}x\,\quantity(h^{\rm TT}_{ij}\Box h_{\rm TT}^{ij}+2\phi\,h^{\rm TT}_{ij}\delta_{\mu\nu}\partial^\mu\partial^\nu h_{\rm TT}^{ij}).

(4.37)

The action Eq. (
4.3
) can be thought of as describing gravitational radiation in flat spacetime interacting with a static Newtonian potential. At the quantum level, the quantization procedure follows as described in Section
3
. This action then describes graviton scattering by a Newtonian potential. In Appendix
8
we compute the differential cross section for a graviton scattered by a Newtonian source behaving like
\phi(r)=-M_{N}/r
, with
r^{2}=\delta_{ij}x^{i}x^{j}
and
M_{N}
being the mass of the source. The result is
23
23

23

Restoring the universal constants once again.

[
164
,
140
]

\derivative{\sigma}{\Omega}=\frac{G^{2}M_{N}^{2}}{c^{4}\sin^{4}\frac{\theta}{2}}\quantity(\cos^8\frac{\theta}{2}+\sin^8\frac{\theta}{2}),

(4.38)

with
\theta
being the scattering angle. The behavior of the differential cross section can be seen from Figure
5
to be strongly dominated by regions in which
\theta\ll 1
, going as

\derivative{\sigma}{\Omega}\sim\frac{G^{2}M_{N}^{2}}{c^{4}}\frac{1}{\theta^{4}}.

(4.39)

Such small probability for higher values of the scattering angle reflects the weakness of the gravitational interaction.

Figure 5:
Differential cross section as a function of the scattering angle.

Chapter 2
Environment-induced decoherence

The concept of a closed system is a useful but sometimes oversimplified idealization in physics, since it is based on the idea that one can isolate an object of interest from any interaction with its surroundings. In practice, one tries to minimize these unwanted influences in order to study such
approximately
closed systems. Now, while classical systems are subject to momentum and energy exchanges with the environment, which can be neglected in most cases (as, for instance, in the collisions between dust particles and a tennis ball), quantum systems, in addition to these kinds of influences, can also become entangled with the environment.

Entanglement is a strong correlation that has no classical analogue. If two quantum systems are found in an entangled state, then measurements performed on one of the subsystems will reveal some amount of information about the other, even if they are spatially separated over large distances. Now, consider a quantum system that is initially prepared in a superposition of mutually orthogonal states. Assuming that the quantum description of nature is universal, the system of interest will, in general, become entangled after interacting with its (quantum) environment. The environmental degrees of freedom (DoFs) can then be thought of as probing the quantum system while acquiring some measure of "which-path information" (or "which-state information", more generally). There is no fundamental principle that prevents us from measuring these environmental DoFs and acquiring such information, even though one typically does not do so, either by choice or due to some practical limitation (in fact, this is often the criterion used to determine which system is the system of interest and which one is the environment). As a consequence, measurements performed on the system will be less and less able to detect interference terms between the components of the superposition (the coherences) as the environment acquires more and more which-state information. In the limiting case, all coherences are somewhat "destroyed"
24
24

24

To be more precise, the interaction with the environment does not destroy the initial quantum superposition but rather enlarges it to include the environment itself. Decoherence happens when such superposition becomes unobservable at the level of the system of interest alone, giving the impression that it has been destroyed (although it is still there).
and the system can only be found in definite "semi-classical" states. This process is called
environment-induced decoherence
, or simply
decoherence

[
96
,
167
,
168
,
88
,
169
,
170
,
146
,
24
]
.

Environment-induced decoherence refers to the phenomenon of the irreversible loss of quantum coherence from a system that is coupled to an environment, as well as the dynamical selection of the observable properties of the system. It is a purely quantum phenomenon, and it can occur alongside some "classical" aspects of environmental interactions, such as dissipation. Now, since a quantum state can be expressed as different superpositions of different bases of the Hilbert space, decoherence is a basis-dependent phenomenon. In general, it is the way the system interacts with a given environment that dictates which basis will be more susceptible to the loss of quantum coherence. For instance, if the interaction Hamiltonian couples environment operators with the system position operator, then we can think of the environment as probing the position of the system at each time instant, and decoherence will eventually occur in the position basis, thereby localizing the system. This selection of observable properties is the aspect of decoherence that is most related to the quantum-to-classical transition, the dynamical selection of a few "classical" observable quantities like energy, position, and momentum. For discussions concerning this connection, and further connections with different interpretations of quantum mechanics, see Ref.
[
147
]
and references therein.

In this chapter, we present a somewhat general overview of the decoherence program in order to establish the physics behind the phenomenon before we specialize in the case of a gravitational environment for the remainder of this work. We begin by exploring the basic ideas behind decoherence in Section
5
, making our discussion more precise and explicitly showing the consequences of the system-environment entanglement. Then, in Section
6
, we introduce the influence functional formalism to open quantum systems. Since more common techniques involve the so-called master equations, we show the equivalence of the two approaches in Section
7
, where we also explore the physical interpretation of each term encompassing the influence of the environment on the open quantum system. We do this by considering the canonical model in which the system of interest is described by continuous phase-space variables, while the environment is described by a set of non-interacting harmonic oscillators. This model is usually referred to as
quantum Brownian motion
, and it bears many resemblances to the problem of a quantum particle in a bath of gravitons that we wish to consider in this work. In Section
8
we explore how the localization of the system due to decoherence can lead to the system being described by trajectories that are obtained as the solution of the Langevin equation, a Newtonian-like equation of motion governed by a stochastic force variable entering as noise from the environment. Finally, the physical reality of such trajectories is discussed in Section
9
in light of the decoherent histories approach to quantum mechanics. This will be crucial for when we define work for open quantum systems in Chapter
6
.

5
Basics of decoherence

Let us consider a quantum system
\mathcal{S}
coupled with an environment
\mathcal{E}
. The Hilbert space of the total system
\mathcal{S}+\mathcal{E}
is given by the tensor product
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{E}}
, where
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
(
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{E}}
) denotes the Hilbert space of the system (environment) alone. This separation between the system and the environment requires us to distinguish between the variables that describe the system of interest and the other (usually infinitely many) variables that can affect the system, but whose detailed dynamics are of somewhat less relevance or may even be intractable from a practical point of view. From now on, let us assume that this choice has been made in a reasonable manner. Moreover, let us assume that one can also introduce some coarse-graining measures that characterize the environment (as, for instance, when we describe a bath by its thermodynamic variables).

Suppose that the total Hamiltonian can be written as

\hat{H}=\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\otimes\hat{I}_{\mathcal{E}}+\hat{I}_{\mathcal{S}}\otimes\hat{H}_{\mathcal{E}}+\hat{H}_{\mathcal{I}},

(5.1)

where
\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
(
\hat{H}_{\mathcal{E}}
) is the free Hamiltonian of the system (environment), while
\hat{I}_{\mathcal{S}}
(
\hat{I}_{\mathcal{E}}
) denotes the identity on the system (environment) subspace. The interaction between them is described by
\hat{H}_{\mathcal{I}}
. All the operators are written in the Schrödinger picture, and we have assumed that the Hamiltonian contains no explicit time dependence. The interaction Hamiltonian can be written as

\hat{H}_{\mathcal{I}}=\sum_{\alpha}\hat{S}_{\alpha}\otimes\hat{E}_{\alpha},

(5.2)

which is the most general form, namely a diagonal decomposition of the system and environment Hermitian operators
\hat{S}_{\alpha}
and
\hat{E}_{\alpha}
. This interaction Hamiltonian singles out a specific set of orthonormal basis vectors of the system,
\quantity{\ket{s_n}}
, which satisfy

\hat{S}_{\alpha}\ket{s_n}=s_{n}^{(\alpha)}\ket{s_n}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for all $n$ and $\alpha$}.

(5.3)

For simplicity, let us assume that
[\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}},\hat{S}_{\alpha}]=0
. In the interaction picture, we have

\hat{H}_{\mathcal{I}}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}_{0}t}\hat{H}_{\mathcal{I}}e^{-i\hat{H}_{0}t}=\sum_{\alpha}\hat{S}_{\alpha}\otimes\hat{E}_{\alpha}(t),

(5.4)

where
\hat{H}_{0}=\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}\otimes\hat{I}_{\mathcal{E}}+\hat{I}_{\mathcal{S}}\otimes\hat{H}_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\hat{E}_{\alpha}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}_{\mathcal{E}}t}\hat{E}_{\alpha}e^{-i\hat{H}_{\mathcal{E}}t}
. The interaction picture time-evolution operator for the total system is then

\hat{U}(t)=\textrm{T}\exp\quantity[-i\int_0^t\dd t'\,\sum_\alpha\hat{S}_\alpha\otimes\hat{E}_\alpha(t')],

(5.5)

with T denoting the time ordering operation, which takes any product of time dependent operators and changes the order such that later times are on the left.

Now consider an initial state
\ket{\psi(0)}=\ket{s_n}\otimes\ket{E(0)}
, where
\ket{E(0)}
stands for the environment initial state. Since this is a product state, it means that initially there is no entanglement between the system and the environment. According to Eq. (
5.5
), this state evolves to

\displaystyle\ket{\psi(t)}

\displaystyle=\hat{U}(t)\ket{\psi(0)}

\displaystyle=\textrm{T}\exp\quantity[-i\int_0^t\dd t'\,\sum_\alpha\hat{S}_\alpha\otimes\hat{E}_\alpha(t')]\ket{s_n}\otimes\ket{E(0)}

\displaystyle=\ket{s_n}\otimes\quantity{\textrm{T}\exp\qty[-i\int_0^t\dd t'\,\sum_\alpha s_n^{(\alpha)}\hat{E}_\alpha(t')]\ket{E(0)}}.

(5.6)

Since the final state is still described by a tensor product, this means that a system initially in the state
\ket{s_n}
does not become entangled with the environment. We say it represents an environment-superselected preferred state
[
146
]
.

However, let us now consider the initial state

\ket{\Psi(0)}=\quantity(\sum_nc_n\ket{s_n})\otimes\ket{E(0)},

(5.7)

which is still a product state and therefore contains no entanglement. The difference here is that the initial state of the system alone is described by a superposition of the state vectors
\ket{s_n}
. It is not hard to see that the total state evolves to

\ket{\Psi(t)}=\sum_{n}c_{n}\ket{s_n}\otimes\ket{E_n(t)},

(5.8)

where

\ket{E_n(t)}=\textrm{T}\exp\quantity[-i\int_0^t\dd t'\,\sum_\alpha s_n^{(\alpha)}\hat{E}_\alpha(t')]\ket{E(0)}.

(5.9)

The state (
5.8
) cannot be written as a tensor product since it is a superposition of the states
\ket{s_n}\otimes\ket{E_n(t)}
, and thus it represents an entangled system-environment state. We see that the initial superposition of system states has been enlarged to include the environment, and one can no longer attribute a specific state to the system alone. However, if one wishes to perform measurements only in the system of interest, then one needs to compute expectation values of system observables by using the reduced density matrix, which is obtained from the total density matrix
\hat{\rho}(t)=\ket{\Psi(t)}\bra{\Psi(t)}
by performing a partial trace with respect to the environment variables
[
43
]
,

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}\quantity{\ket{\Psi(t)}\bra{\Psi(t)}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\bra{i}\quantity(\sum_nc_n\ket{s_n}\otimes\ket{E_n(t)})\quantity(\sum_mc_m^*\bra{s_m}\otimes\bra{E_m(t)})\ket{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}\sum_{m}\sum_{i}c_{n}c_{m}^{*}\ket{s_n}\bra{s_m}\innerproduct{i}{E_n(t)}\innerproduct{E_m(t)}{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m}c_{n}c_{m}^{*}\ket{s_n}\bra{s_m}\innerproduct{E_m(t)}{E_n(t)},

(5.10)

where
\quantity{\ket{i}}
denotes a complete set of environment orthonormal basis vectors. Since
\innerproduct{E_n(t)}{E_n(t)}=1
, the diagonal elements of
\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)
are constant over time. However, the off-diagonal elements, which are called
coherences
, do change over time. The time dependence of the matrix element
\matrixelement{s_n}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}{s_m}
is given by the overlap of the corresponding environment states
\ket{E_n(t)}
and
\ket{E_m(t)}
, which is usually written as

\absolutevalue{\braket{E_n(t)}{E_m(t)}}=\exp[-\Gamma_{nm}(t)].

(5.11)

When the quantity
\Gamma_{nm}(t)
satisfies
\Gamma_{nm}(t)\geq 0
, we call it the
decoherence function

[
24
]
. The matrix element
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}^{nm}=\matrixelement{s_n}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}}{s_m}
can then be written as

\rho_{\mathcal{S}}^{nm}(t)=\rho_{\mathcal{S}}^{nm}(0)e^{-\Gamma_{nm}(t)},

(5.12)

where
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}^{nm}(0)=c_{n}c_{m}^{*}
.

The time dependence of the decoherence function depends on many variables, such as the system-environment coupling and the total system initial state. For many physical systems of interest, the system-environment interaction leads to a rapid decrease in the overlap between the environment states
\ket{E_n(t)}
and
\ket{E_m(t)}
,
m\neq n
, which can be found to vanish after times that are long compared to a typical timescale
\tau_{\rm dec}
,

\innerproduct{E_n(t)}{E_m(t)}\to\delta_{nm}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for $t\gg\tau_{\rm dec}$}.

(5.13)

The timescale
\tau_{\rm dec}
is called the
decoherence time
, usually defined by the condition
\Gamma_{nm}(\tau_{\rm dec})=1
. For times much larger than
\tau_{\rm dec}
, the reduced system density matrix approaches

\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)\to\sum_{n}\absolutevalue{c_n}^{2}\ket{s_n}\bra{s_n}\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for $t\gg\tau_{\rm dec}$}.

(5.14)

Physically, this means that superpositions of the states
\ket{s_n}
, which were initially present, are effectively "destroyed" in the sense that they are no longer observable for any measurement performed on the system
\mathcal{S}
. The coherent superposition has transitioned to an incoherent statistical mixture, and the reduced density matrix has become diagonal in the particular set of basis states
\ket{s_n}
. This environment induced transition is the phenomenon known as
decoherence
.

In the following sections, we will discuss a specific canonical model that exhibits and illustrates environment-induced decoherence in a quantum system. To do so, we will first need to establish a formalism for describing open quantum systems.

6
The Feynman-Vernon influence functional

Having introduced the ideas behind the phenomenon of decoherence, let us next present the formalism of the Feynman–Vernon influence functional in order to study the general dynamics of an open quantum system
[
64
,
63
,
29
]
. We begin with a brief review of the path integral formulation of quantum mechanics, followed by the study of open quantum systems using the influence functional approach. Lastly, we present an example of an open system linearly coupled with an environment, which will be very relevant to this work.

6.1
Path integrals

In the Schrödinger picture of quantum mechanics, the states evolve in time according to

\ket{\psi(t)}=\hat{U}(t,t_{0})\ket{\psi(t_0)},

(6.1)

where the unitary time evolution operator from initial time
t_{0}
to time
t
reads

\hat{U}(t,t_{0})=\textrm{T}\quantity[\exp\qty(-i\int_{t_0}^t\dd t'\,\hat{H}(t'))]

(6.2)

with
\hat{H}(t)
being the Hamiltonian operator and T standing for time ordering.

For simplicity, let us consider a system described by a single degree of freedom
x
, which denotes the spectral family of the position operator
\hat{X}
,
\hat{X}\ket{x}=x\ket{x}
. The inner product
\psi(x)=\innerproduct{x}{\psi}
is what we call the wavefunction in the position representation. In terms of the wavefunction, the time evolution (
6.1
) reads

\psi(x,t)=\innerproduct{x}{\psi(t)}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x_{0}\,\matrixelement{x}{\hat{U}(t,t_0)}{x_0}\innerproduct{x_0}{\psi(t_0)},

(6.3)

where we introduced a completeness relation. We can rewrite the last equation as

\psi(x,t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x_{0}\,K(x,t;x_{0},t_{0})\psi(x_{0},t_{0}).

(6.4)

The function
K(x,t;x_{0},t_{0})=\matrixelement{x}{\hat{U}(t,t_0)}{x_0}
is called the
propagator
, which is an amplitude to move from point
x_{0}
at
t_{0}
to point
x
at
t
.

In the path integral formulation of quantum mechanics, the propagator is obtained from

K(x_{f},t_{f};x_{0},t_{0})=\int\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x(0)\,=\,x_{0}\end{subarray}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}x(t_{f})\,=\,x_{f}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}x\,e^{iS[x(t)]},

(6.5)

where the integral over the measure
\mathcal{D}x
denotes a sum over all paths
x(t)
that lead from
x_{0}
to
x_{f}
, and
S[x(t)]
is the classical action (the time integral from
t_{0}
to
t_{f}
of the system’s Lagrangian) computed for each path. One can arrive at (
6.5
) by dividing the time interval
[t_{0},t_{f}]
into
N
infinitesimal intervals of length
\delta t
, inserting a completeness relation for each intermediate point
x_{i}
,
i\in[0,N]
, into
\matrixelement{x}{\hat{U}(t,t_0)}{x_0}
, computing all matrix elements by using the expression for the time evolution operator (
6.2
), and finally taking the limit
N\to\infty
(
\delta t\to 0
)
[
149
,
151
,
129
]
. Alternatively, one can take Eq. (
6.5
) as a time evolution postulate of quantum mechanics and show that the wavefunction (
6.4
) must obey Schrödinger’s equation
[
63
]
. Both formulations of quantum mechanics are equivalent.

The Feynman path integral formalism, as described by Eq. (
6.5
), remarkably tells us that the time evolution of the quantum state
\ket{\psi}
is described by an integral over all paths from the beginning to the endpoint, weighted by the exponential of the
classical
action. However, our description of the quantum system may not be given by a single ket in Hilbert space, but rather by a given class of states
\ket{\psi_i}
for which our knowledge does not allow us to go beyond assigning a probability of occurrence
p_{i}
to each member of this class. We then describe the system using a density matrix,

\hat{\rho}=\sum_{i}p_{i}\ket{\psi_i}\bra{\psi_i}.

(6.6)

We always have
\textrm{Tr}\,\hat{\rho}=1
, while in general
\textrm{Tr}\,\hat{\rho}^{2}\leq 1
. Kets in the Hilbert space are represented by particular cases of density matrices with
\textrm{Tr}\,\hat{\rho}^{2}=1
(also called
pure states
, while states with
\textrm{Tr}\,\hat{\rho}^{2}<1
are called
mixed states
). It follows from Eqs. (
6.1
) and (
6.6
) that the time evolution of the density matrix is given by

\hat{\rho}(t)=\hat{U}(t)\hat{\rho}(0)\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t),

(6.7)

where we are taking the initial time instant as
t_{0}=0
and denoting
\hat{U}(t)\equiv\hat{U}(t,0)
.

We can obtain a path integral representation for the time evolution of the density matrix by considering the matrix element
\rho(x,x^{\prime},t)\equiv\matrixelement{x}{\hat{\rho}(t)}{x'}
. By inserting completeness relations and using the path integral representation for the propagator (
6.5
), we arrive at
25
25

25

A word on notation, as it can become quite confusing from this point on: when we have variables like
x
and
q
in a path integral measure, like
\mathcal{D}x
and
\mathcal{D}q
, or inside square brackets like in
S[x,q]
, we are referring to the functions
x(t)
,
q(t)
, and so on. Everywhere else, we are referring to the spatial points
x
or
q
, which are often the endpoints of paths
x(t)
and
q(t)
.

\displaystyle\rho(x,x^{\prime},t)

\displaystyle=\matrixelement{x}{\hat{U}(t)\hat{\rho}(0)\hat{U}^\dagger(t)}{x'}

\displaystyle=\matrixelement{x}{\hat{U}(t)\qty(\int_{-\infty}^\infty\dd x_0\ket{x_0}\bra{x_0})\hat{\rho}(0)\qty(\int_{-\infty}^\infty\dd x_0'\ket{x_0'}\bra{x_0'}) \hat{U}^\dagger(t)}{x'}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x_{0}\differential x_{0}^{\prime}\,\matrixelement{x}{\hat{U}(t)}{x_0}\matrixelement{x_0'}{\hat{U}^\dagger(t)}{x'}\matrixelement{x_0}{\hat{\rho}(0)}{x_0'}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x(0)\differential x^{\prime}(0)\,\int\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x(0)\,=\,x_{0}\\
x^{\prime}(0)\,=\,x_{0}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}x(t)\,=\,x\\
x^{\prime}(t)\,=\,x^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}x\mathcal{D}x^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S[x]-S[x'])}\rho(x(0),x^{\prime}(0),0).

(6.8)

As we can see, the time evolution of the density matrix involves two histories of the system, rather than a single one, as in the time evolution of the wave function (
6.4
).

6.2
The influence functional

Let us again consider an open quantum system
\mathcal{S}
coupled with a quantum environment
\mathcal{E}
. While the dynamics of the combined (closed) total system
\mathcal{S}+\mathcal{E}
are unitary, the state of the system
\mathcal{S}
, a subsystem of the combined one, will evolve according to its internal dynamics as well as to its interactions with the surroundings. These interactions lead to system-environment correlations such that the dynamics of the system
\mathcal{S}
will not be unitary in general. The dynamics of the system
\mathcal{S}
are called the
reduced system dynamics
, and the system
\mathcal{S}
itself is referred to as the
reduced system

[
24
]
.

The action of the total system is usually a sum of three terms: one describing the system alone, another for the dynamics of the environment, and the interaction between them. Let us then consider a system
\mathcal{S}
described by a set of variables
x=\quantity{x_n}
that interacts with an environment
\mathcal{E}
described by variables
q=\quantity{q_n}
. The classical action takes the form

S[x,q]=S_{\mathcal{S}}[x]+S_{\mathcal{E}}[q]+S_{\mathcal{I}}[x,q],

(6.9)

where the action
S_{\mathcal{I}}[x,q]
describes the interaction between the system and the environment. The quantum state of the total system is described by the density matrix
\rho(xq,x^{\prime}q^{\prime},t)
depending on both system and environment variables. It evolves unitarily under the total Hamiltonian, according to
[
29
]

\rho(xq,x^{\prime}q^{\prime},t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x_{0}\differential q_{0}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x_{0}^{\prime}\differential q_{0}^{\prime}\,\mathcal{J}(xq,x^{\prime}q^{\prime},t|x_{0}q_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}q_{0}^{\prime},0)\,\rho(x_{0}q_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}q_{0}^{\prime},0),

(6.10)

where
\rho(xq,x^{\prime}q^{\prime},t)\equiv\matrixelement{xq}{\hat{\rho}(t)}{x'q'}
, and
\mathcal{J}
is seen as a time evolution kernel for the total combined system, given by

\mathcal{J}(xq,x^{\prime}q^{\prime},t|x_{0}q_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}q_{0}^{\prime},0)=\int\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x(0)\,=\,x_{0}\\
x^{\prime}(0)\,=\,x_{0}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}x(t)\,=\,x\\
x^{\prime}(t)\,=\,x^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}x\mathcal{D}x^{\prime}\int\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}q(0)\,=\,q_{0}\\
q^{\prime}(0)\,=\,q_{0}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}q(t)\,=\,q\\
q^{\prime}(t)\,=\,q^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}q\mathcal{D}q^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S[x,q]-S[x',q'])}.

(6.11)

However, when dealing with open quantum system dynamics, one is usually interested in computing expectation values of system observables while taking no interest in the environment dynamics. The expectation value of such observables is computed with the reduced density matrix
\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}
, which is obtained from the total density matrix by a partial trace over the environment variables,

\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x,x^{\prime},t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q\,\rho(xq,x^{\prime}q,t).

(6.12)

Assuming that at
t=0
the system and environment are uncorrelated,

\rho(x_{0}q_{0},x_{0}^{\prime}q_{0}^{\prime},0)=\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},0)\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(q_{0},q_{0}^{\prime},0),

(6.13)

we can rearrange the order of integration to write the reduced density matrix as

\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x,x^{\prime},t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x_{0}\differential x_{0}^{\prime}\,\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{S}}(x,x^{\prime},t|x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},0)\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},0),

(6.14)

where the time evolution kernel for the reduced system reads

\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{S}}(x,x^{\prime},t|x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},0)\equiv\int\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x(0)\,=\,x_{0}\\
x^{\prime}(0)\,=\,x_{0}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}x(t)\,=\,x\\
x^{\prime}(t)\,=\,x^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}x\mathcal{D}x^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\mathcal{S}}[x]-S_{\mathcal{S}}[x'])}\mathcal{F}[x,x^{\prime}].

(6.15)

The functional
\mathcal{F}[x,x^{\prime}]
is called the
Feynman-Vernon influence functional
, and it is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[x,x^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=e^{iS_{\rm IF}[x,x^{\prime},t]}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q\differential q_{0}\differential q_{0}^{\prime}\,\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(q_{0},q_{0}^{\prime},0)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}q(0)\,=\,q_{0}\\
q^{\prime}(0)\,=\,q_{0}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{q(t)\,=\,q^{\prime}(t)\,=\,q}\mathcal{D}q\mathcal{D}q^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\mathcal{E}}[q]+S_{\mathcal{I}}[x,q]-S_{\mathcal{E}}[q']-S_{\mathcal{I}}[x',q'])}.

(6.16)

Here,
S_{\rm IF}
is called the
influence action
, and it encodes all influence from the environment on the system. Its presence in the time evolution (
6.14
) induces an interaction between the two histories
x
and
x^{\prime}
, being responsible for the non-unitary evolution.

We can list some general properties of the influence functional
[
64
,
63
,
29
]
:

1.

In terms of the time evolution operators
\hat{\mathcal{U}}(t)
and
\hat{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}(t)
for
S_{\mathcal{E}}[q]+S_{\mathcal{I}}[x,q]
and
S_{\mathcal{E}}[q]+S_{\mathcal{I}}[x^{\prime},q]
, respectively, the influence functional can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[x,x^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q\differential q_{0}\differential q_{0}^{\prime}\,\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(q_{0},q_{0}^{\prime},0)\,\matrixelement{q}{\hat{\mathcal{U}}(t)}{q_0}\matrixelement{q_0'}{\hat{\mathcal{U}}'^\dagger(t)}{q}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q\differential q_{0}\differential q_{0}^{\prime}\,\matrixelement{q_0'}{\hat{\mathcal{U}}'^\dagger(t)}{q}\matrixelement{q}{\hat{\mathcal{U}}(t)}{q_0}\matrixelement{q_0}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{E}}(0)}{q_0'}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}\quantity{\hat{\mathcal{U}}(t)\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{E}}(0)\hat{\mathcal{U}}'^\dagger(t)}=\expectationvalue{\hat{\mathcal{U}}'^\dagger(t)\hat{\mathcal{U}}(t)}_{\mathcal{E}},

(6.17)

where
\expectationvalue{\cdot}_{\mathcal{E}}
denotes the average with respect to the environment initial state
\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{E}}(0)
.

2.

The influence functional satisfies

\mathcal{F}[x,x^{\prime}]=\mathcal{F}^{*}[x^{\prime},x],

(6.18)

which follows immediately from Eq. (
1
).

3.

The influence action satisfies

S_{\rm IF}[x,x,t]=0,

(6.19)

which can be easily seen from Eq. (
1
) by recalling that
\textrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}\,\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{E}}(0)=1
.

4.

If a number of statistically and dynamically independent environments act on the system at the same time, and if
\mathcal{F}_{k}
denotes the influence functional of the
k
th environment alone, the total influence is given by the product of the individual
\mathcal{F}_{k}
,

\mathcal{F}=\prod_{k}\mathcal{F}_{k}.

(6.20)

The path integral representation (
6.2
) involves two histories
q
and
q^{\prime}
that may be described as an integral over a single history defined on a closed time path (CTP) due to the boundary condition
q(t)=q^{\prime}(t)
. Throughout the remainder of this section, we shall follow Ref.
[
29
]
and denote
x(t)=x^{1}(t)
,
x^{\prime}(t)=x^{2}(t)
such that
x^{a}
,
a=1,2
, can be thought of as a single doublet field defined on a single time path. We also define a metric tensor
c_{ab}=\textrm{diag}\quantity(1,-1)
, which, together with its inverse
c^{ab}=\textrm{diag}\quantity(1,-1)
, may be used to raise and/or lower indices, as in
x_{1}=c_{1a}x^{a}=c_{11}x^{1}=x^{1}=x
and
x_{2}=c_{2a}x^{a}=c_{22}x^{2}=-x^{2}=-x^{\prime}
. In this CTP notation, the kinetic terms in the system Lagrangian, for instance, will be written as
c_{ab}\dot{x}^{a}\dot{x}^{b}=\dot{x}_{a}\dot{x}^{a}=\dot{x}^{2}-\dot{x}^{\prime 2}
. We refer to the
CTP action

S[x^{a}]\equiv S[x]-S[x^{\prime}]
when considering the contributions from both branches of the closed time path. In CTP notation, the influence functional (
6.2
) is written as

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}[x^{a}]&=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q^{1}(t)\differential q^{1}(0)\differential q^{2}(0)\,\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(q^{1}(0),q^{2}(0),0)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int\displaylimits_{q^{1}(t)\,=\,q^{2}(t)}\mathcal{D}q^{a}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\mathcal{E}}[q^a]+S_{\mathcal{I}}[x^a,q^a])}.\end{split}

(6.21)

Having discussed the general properties of the influence functional, let us now consider a specific example of an environment that is linearly coupled to the system.

6.3
Linear coupling model

The linear coupling model is described by the assumptions that the environmental action is quadratic in the
q
variables, the initial environmental density matrix is Gaussian, and the interaction term is bilinear (linear in the system and environmental variables). This is the case for the model of quantum Brownian motion, for instance, in which the environmental action describes harmonic oscillators, and it will also be the case for the problem of a quantum particle in a bath of gravitons, as we will see in Chapter
4
.

Let us then work under all these assumptions and take an interaction of the form
26
26

26

A more precise notation would be
S_{\mathcal{I}}[x,q,t]=\int_{0}^{t}\differential t^{\prime}\,x^{a}(t^{\prime})Q_{a}[q(t^{\prime})]
.

S_{\mathcal{I}}[x,q]=\int\differential t\,x^{a}(t)Q_{a}[q(t)],

(6.22)

where the
Q
’s are linear combinations of the
q
’s. In that case, the influence functional (
6.2
) becomes a functional Fourier transform of a Gaussian functional of histories
Q(t)
and
Q^{\prime}(t)
. Since the Fourier transform of a Gaussian is another Gaussian, we conclude that, under all these assumptions, the influence action must also be quadratic in
x
and
x^{\prime}

[
29
]
. Therefore, we write

S_{\textrm{IF}}[x^{a}]=\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,x^{a}(t)G_{ab}(t,t^{\prime})x^{b}(t^{\prime}),

(6.23)

where

G_{ab}(t,t^{\prime})=-i\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta x^{a}(t)\delta x^{b}(t^{\prime})}\evaluated{e^{iS_{\rm IF}[x^a]}}_{x_a\,=\,0},

(6.24)

with the
\delta
notation denoting functional derivatives. A direct variation from Eq. (
6.21
) with
S_{\mathcal{I}}
given by Eq. (
6.22
) yields

\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta x^{a}(t)\delta x^{b}(t^{\prime})}\evaluated{e^{iS_{\rm IF}[x^a]}}_{x_a\,=\,0}=-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q^{1}(t_{f})\differential q^{1}(0)\differential q^{2}(0)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\int\displaylimits_{q^{1}(t_{f})\,=\,q^{2}(t_{f})}\mathcal{D}q^{a}\,e^{iS_{\mathcal{E}}[q^{a}]}Q_{a}(t)Q_{b}(t^{\prime})\,\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(q^{1}(0),q^{2}(0),0).

(6.25)

Explicitly,

\begin{split}G_{11}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})&=i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q\differential q(0)\differential q^{\prime}(0)\,\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(q(0),q^{\prime}(0),0)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int\displaylimits_{q(t)\,=\,q^{\prime}(t)\,=\,q}\mathcal{D}q\mathcal{D}q^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\mathcal{E}}[q]-S_{\mathcal{E}}[q'])}Q(\tau)Q(\tau^{\prime}),\end{split}

(6.26a)

\begin{split}G_{12}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})&=-i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q\differential q(0)\differential q^{\prime}(0)\,\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(q(0),q^{\prime}(0),0)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int\displaylimits_{q(t)\,=\,q^{\prime}(t)\,=\,q}\mathcal{D}q\mathcal{D}q^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\mathcal{E}}[q]-S_{\mathcal{E}}[q'])}Q(\tau)Q^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime}),\end{split}

(6.26b)

\begin{split}G_{21}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})&=-i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q\differential q(0)\differential q^{\prime}(0)\,\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(q(0),q^{\prime}(0),0)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int\displaylimits_{q(t)\,=\,q^{\prime}(t)\,=\,q}\mathcal{D}q\mathcal{D}q^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\mathcal{E}}[q]-S_{\mathcal{E}}[q'])}Q^{\prime}(\tau)Q(\tau^{\prime}),\end{split}

(6.26c)

\begin{split}G_{22}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})&=i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q\differential q(0)\differential q^{\prime}(0)\,\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(q(0),q^{\prime}(0),0)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int\displaylimits_{q(t)\,=\,q^{\prime}(t)\,=\,q}\mathcal{D}q\mathcal{D}q^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\mathcal{E}}[q]-S_{\mathcal{E}}[q'])}Q^{\prime}(\tau)Q^{\prime}(\tau^{\prime}).\end{split}

(6.26d)

In order to compute these in canonical form, let us use the fact that the propagator (
6.5
) satisfies
[
63
]

K(x_{b},t_{b};x_{a},t_{a})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x_{c}\,K(x_{b},t_{b};x_{c},t_{c})K(x_{c},t_{c};x_{a},t_{a}),

(6.27)

where
x_{c}=x(t_{c})
and
t_{a}<t_{c}<t_{b}
. Then, by assuming
\tau>\tau^{\prime}
, we may write

\displaystyle\int\displaylimits_{q(t)\,=\,q^{\prime}(t)}\mathcal{D}q\mathcal{D}q^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\mathcal{E}}[q]-S_{\mathcal{E}}[q'])}Q(\tau)Q(\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q(\tau^{\prime})\differential q(\tau)\,\int\displaylimits_{0\,\leq\,t^{\prime}\,\leq\,\tau^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}q(t^{\prime})\,e^{iS_{\mathcal{E}}[q(t^{\prime})]}Q(\tau^{\prime})\int\displaylimits_{\tau^{\prime}\,\leq\,t^{\prime}\,\leq\,\tau}\mathcal{D}q(t^{\prime})\,e^{iS_{\mathcal{E}}[q(t^{\prime})]}Q(\tau)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int\displaylimits_{\tau\,\leq\,t^{\prime}\,\leq\,t}\mathcal{D}q(t^{\prime})\,e^{iS_{\mathcal{E}}[q(t^{\prime})]}\int\displaylimits_{0\,\leq\,t^{\prime}\,\leq\,t}\mathcal{D}q^{\prime}(t^{\prime})e^{-iS_{\mathcal{E}}[q^{\prime}(t^{\prime})]},

(6.28)

where
q(t^{\prime})
and
q^{\prime}(t^{\prime})
denote the integration (path) variables, and the subscripts on the right-hand side of the equation indicate the time interval in which the path integral is being computed. We may now identify each path integral as a matrix element for the environment free time evolution operator
\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(t)=e^{-i\hat{H}_{\mathcal{E}}t}
and write

\displaystyle-iG_{11}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q(t)\differential q(0)\differential q^{\prime}(0)\differential q(\tau^{\prime})\differential q(\tau)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\matrixelement{q(t)}{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(t,\tau)}{q(\tau)}\,Q(\tau)\,\matrixelement{q(\tau)}{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau,\tau')}{q(\tau')}\,Q(\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\matrixelement{q(\tau')}{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau',0)}{q(0)}\matrixelement{q(0)}{\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(0)}{q'(0)}\matrixelement{q'(0)}{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(0,t)}{q(t)}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q(t)\differential q(\tau^{\prime})\differential q(\tau)\,\matrixelement{q(t)}{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(t,\tau)\hat{Q}}{q(\tau)}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\matrixelement{q(\tau)}{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau,\tau')\hat{Q}}{q(\tau')}\matrixelement{q(\tau')}{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau',0)\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(0)\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(0,t)}{q(t)}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q(t)\,\matrixelement{q(t)}{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(t,\tau)\hat{Q}\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau,\tau')\hat{Q}\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau',0)\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(0)\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(0,t)}{q(t)}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}\quantity{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(t,\tau)\hat{Q}\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau,\tau')\hat{Q}\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau',0)\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(0)\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(0,t)}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}\quantity{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(0,t)\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(t,\tau)\hat{Q}\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau,\tau')\hat{Q}\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau',0)\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(0)}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}\quantity{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(0,\tau)\hat{Q}\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau,0)\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(0,\tau')\hat{Q}\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau',0)\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(0)}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}\quantity{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}^\dagger(\tau)\hat{Q}\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau)\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}^\dagger(\tau')\hat{Q}\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau')\rho_{\mathcal{E}}(0)}

\displaystyle=\expectationvalue{\hat{Q}(\tau)\hat{Q}(\tau')}_{\mathcal{E}},\hskip 28.45274pt(\tau>\tau^{\prime})

(6.29)

where
\hat{Q}(\tau)
is an operator in the interaction picture,
\hat{Q}(\tau)=\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}^{\dagger}(\tau)\hat{Q}\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(\tau)
. In the expressions above, we used the composition rule
\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(t,t^{\prime})\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(t^{\prime},t_{0})=\hat{U}_{\mathcal{E}}(t,t_{0})
. It is interesting to note that if we had not specified the temporal relation between
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
, the path integral would have automatically set the latest time to the left. This means that we can write the general result

G_{11}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})=i\expectationvalue{\textrm{T}\qty[\hat{Q}(\tau)\hat{Q}(\tau')]}_{\mathcal{E}}.

(6.30)

By completely analogous procedures, we can write the other components (
6.26
) in canonical form and obtain

\mathbb{G}(t,t^{\prime})=i\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\expval{\textrm{T}\qty[\hat{Q}(t)\hat{Q}(t')]}_{\mathcal{E}} & -\expval{\hat{Q}(t')\hat{Q}(t)}_{\mathcal{E}} \\ -\expval{\hat{Q}(t)\hat{Q}(t')}_{\mathcal{E}} & \expval{\Tilde{\textrm{T}}\qty[\hat{Q}(t)\hat{Q}(t')]}_{\mathcal{E}}\endlx@physics@matrix),

(6.31)

where
\tilde{\textrm{T}}
denotes
anti-time
ordering, which takes any product of time dependent operators and changes the order such that later times are on the
right
. Also
\mathbb{G}
denotes the matrix whose elements are the
G_{ab}
’s.

The influence action (
6.23
) can then be written as

\displaystyle S_{\rm IF}[x,x^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\left\{\expectationvalue{\textrm{T}\qty[\hat{Q}(t)\hat{Q}(t')]}_{\mathcal{E}}x(t)x(t^{\prime})-\expectationvalue{\hat{Q}(t')\hat{Q}(t)}_{\mathcal{E}}x(t)x^{\prime}(t^{\prime})\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\left.-\expectationvalue{\hat{Q}(t)\hat{Q}(t')}_{\mathcal{E}}x^{\prime}(t)x(t^{\prime})+\expectationvalue{\Tilde{\textrm{T}}\qty[\hat{Q}(t)\hat{Q}(t')]}_{\mathcal{E}}x^{\prime}(t)x^{\prime}(t^{\prime})\right\}.

(6.32)

We can write it in a more compact form by introducing the variables
u=x-x^{\prime}
and
X=(x+x^{\prime})/2
. Additionally, we use the property
\theta(-x)=1-\theta(x)
of the step function to write

\textrm{T}\quantity[\hat{Q}(t)\hat{Q}(t')]=\theta(t-t^{\prime})\commutator{\hat{Q}(t)}{\hat{Q}(t')}+\hat{Q}(t^{\prime})\hat{Q}(t),

(6.33a)

and

\tilde{\textrm{T}}\quantity[\hat{Q}(t)\hat{Q}(t')]=-\theta(t-t^{\prime})\commutator{\hat{Q}(t)}{\hat{Q}(t')}+\hat{Q}(t)\hat{Q}(t^{\prime}),

(6.33b)

with
\commutator{\cdot}{\cdot}
denoting the commutator between operators. Then, after a fair amount of algebraic manipulations, we arrive at

S_{\rm IF}[x,x^{\prime}]=\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\quantity[u(t)D(t,t')X(t')+\frac{i}{2}u(t)N(t,t')u(t')],

(6.34)

where we have defined the
dissipation
and
noise kernels

D(t,t^{\prime})=i\expectationvalue{\comm{\hat{Q}(t)}{\hat{Q}(t')}}_{\mathcal{E}}\theta(t-t^{\prime}),

(6.35a)

N(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\expectationvalue{\acomm{\hat{Q}(t)}{\hat{Q}(t')}}_{\mathcal{E}},

(6.35b)

respectively. Here
\anticommutator{\cdot}{\cdot}
denotes the anti-commutator between operators.

The physical interpretation of these kernels, as well as the reason for their nomenclature, will become more evident once we derive a master equation for the reduced density matrix in the next section.

7
The master equation

For closed quantum systems, the time evolution can be described entirely in terms of the density matrix by the
Liouville-von Neumann equation
,

\derivative{t}\hat{\rho}(t)=-i\commutator{\hat{H}}{\hat{\rho}(t)},

(7.1)

where
\hat{H}
is the total Hamiltonian of the system. This time evolution is unitary, and the same cannot be said when the system is open. In that case, the Liouville-von Neumann equation is replaced by the so-called master equations. For most cases of interest, the time evolution of the reduced density matrix for a system coupled with an environment takes the form
[
146
,
24
]

\derivative{t}\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)=-i\commutator{\hat{H}'_{\mathcal{S}}}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}+\hat{\mathcal{D}}\quantity[\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)].

(7.2)

Here
\hat{H}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the part of the Hamiltonian that acts only on the system of interest. In general,
\hat{H}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{S}}\neq\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
, since the interaction with the environment can lead to a renormalization of the energy levels of the system, an effect usually referred to as the
Lamb-shift
contribution
[
146
]
. Additionally, the presence of the environment introduces the second term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
7.2
), which is responsible for the non-unitary time evolution of the reduced system. The super-operator
27
27

27

A super-operator refers to an operator that acts on another operator.

\hat{\mathcal{D}}
is sometimes referred to as the dissipator, and it is responsible for the effects of dissipation and decoherence.

A master equation like Eq. (
7.2
) is usually obtained from the Liouville-von Neumann equation for the total combined system by taking the partial trace over the environment variables, assuming an initial product state (as we have done in the previous section), and further imposing some other approximations, like the Born and Markov approximations
28
28

28

The Born approximation assumes that the interaction between the system and the environment is sufficiently weak, and the environment is sufficiently large in comparison with the size of the system, such that the total state remains approximately a product state throughout the time evolution. The Markov approximation assumes that the environmental correlation functions drop to zero much faster than the characteristic timescale over which the reduced density matrix of the system changes appreciably. It basically neglects the memory effects of the environment and usually transforms the integro-differential master equation into a much simpler differential one.
(see Refs.
[
146
,
24
]
for the details). Here we will simply show how we can obtain a master equation for the reduced density matrix from the influence functional approach.

7.1
Deriving the master equation

We begin by writing an expression for
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{f},x^{\prime}_{f},t+\Delta t)
in terms of
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x,x^{\prime},t)
,

\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{f},x^{\prime}_{f},t+\Delta t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x\differential x^{\prime}\,\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{f},x^{\prime}_{f},t+\Delta t|x,x^{\prime},t)\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x,x^{\prime},t),

(7.3)

where

\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{f},x^{\prime}_{f},t+\Delta t|x,x^{\prime},t)=\int\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}x(t)\,=\,x\\
x^{\prime}(t)\,=\,x^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}x\quantity(t+\Delta t)\,=\,x_{f}\\
x^{\prime}(t+\Delta t)\,=\,x_{f}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}x\mathcal{D}x^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(\Delta S_{\mathcal{S}}[x]-\Delta S_{\mathcal{S}}[x'])}e^{i\Delta S_{\rm IF}[x,x^{\prime}]},

(7.4)

with
\Delta S_{\mathcal{S}}[x]\equiv S_{\mathcal{S}}[x,t+\Delta t]-S_{\mathcal{S}}[x,t]
and the same holds for
\Delta S_{\rm IF}[x,x^{\prime}]
.

Since our goal is to obtain a first order differential equation for the time evolution of the reduced density matrix, we want to keep terms up to first order in
\Delta t
. Now,
\Delta S_{\rm IF}[x,x^{\prime}]\propto\Delta t
and thus

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{f},x^{\prime}_{f},t+\Delta t|x,x^{\prime},t)

\displaystyle\simeq K_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{f},t+\Delta t|x,t)K_{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(x_{f}^{\prime},t+\Delta t|x^{\prime},t)

\displaystyle+i\int\mathcal{D}x\mathcal{D}x^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(\Delta S_{\mathcal{S}}[x]-\Delta S_{\mathcal{S}}[x'])}\Delta S_{\rm IF}[x,x^{\prime}],

(7.5)

where

\displaystyle K_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{f},t+\Delta t|x,t)

\displaystyle=\int\displaylimits_{x(t)\,=\,x}^{x\quantity(t+\Delta t)\,=\,x_{f}}\mathcal{D}x\,e^{i\Delta S_{\mathcal{S}}[x]}=\matrixelement{x_f}{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{S}}(t+\Delta t,t)}{x}

\displaystyle\simeq\delta(x_{f}-x)-i\Delta t\matrixelement{x_f}{\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}}{x}.

(7.6)

If we work with the linear coupling model, the influence action is given by Eq. (
6.34
), and we find

\Delta S_{\rm IF}[x,x^{\prime}]=\Delta t\,u(t)\int_{0}^{t}\differential t^{\prime}\,\quantity[D(t,t')X(t')+iN(t,t')u(t')],

(7.7)

where we used the fact that
D(t,t^{\prime})\propto\theta(t-t^{\prime})
, i.e.,
D(t,t^{\prime})
is explicitly causal.

By plugging Eqs. (
7.1
) and (
7.7
) into Eq. (
7.3
), using Eq. (
6.14
) in order to write the time evolution in terms of the initial system density matrix, and then taking the limit where
\Delta t\to 0
while letting
x_{f}\to x
and
x_{f}^{\prime}\to x^{\prime}
, we finally arrive at

\displaystyle\partialderivative{t}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x,x^{\prime},t)=-i\matrixelement{x}{\comm{\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}}{x'}

\displaystyle-(x-x^{\prime})\int_{0}^{t}\differential t^{\prime}\,\quantity[N(t,t')\qty(\mathbf{X}-\mathbf{X}')(x,x',t')-\frac{i}{2}D(t,t')\qty(\mathbf{X}+\mathbf{X}')(x,x',t')],

(7.8)

with

\mathbf{X}(x,x^{\prime},t^{\prime})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x_{0}\differential x_{0}^{\prime}\,\int\mathcal{D}x\mathcal{D}x^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\mathcal{S}}[x]-S_{\mathcal{S}}[x']+S_{\rm IF}[x,x'])}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},0)x(t^{\prime}).

(7.9)

Note that the evolution of
\mathbf{X}(x,x^{\prime},t^{\prime})
also depends on the interaction with the environment through the influence action. However, for sufficiently weak interactions, we can take advantage of the fact that both the noise and dissipation kernels are, in general, of second order in the operator
\hat{Q}
that couples with the system. Therefore, we can neglect the influence action in the exponential since this would lead to higher order contributions and write
[
29
]

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}(x,x^{\prime},t^{\prime})

\displaystyle\simeq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x_{0}\differential x_{0}^{\prime}\,\int\mathcal{D}x\mathcal{D}x^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\mathcal{S}}[x]-S_{\mathcal{S}}[x'])}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},0)x(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x_{0}\differential x_{0}^{\prime}\differential x(t^{\prime})\matrixelement{x(t')}{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{S}}(t',0)}{x_0}\matrixelement{x}{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{S}}(t,t')\hat{X}}{x(t')}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\matrixelement{x_0}{\hat{U}_{\mathcal{S}}(0,t)}{x'}\matrixelement{x_0}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(0)}{x_0'}

\displaystyle=\matrixelement{x}{e^{-i\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}(t-t')}\hat{X}e^{-i\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}t'}\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(0)e^{i\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}t}}{x'}

\displaystyle=\matrixelement{x}{\hat{X}(-\tau)\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}{x'},

(7.10)

where
\tau=t-t^{\prime}
and
\hat{X}(t)=e^{i\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}t}\hat{X}e^{-i\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}t}
is the interaction picture position operator. Similarly, one can show that
\mathbf{X}^{\prime}(x,x^{\prime},t^{\prime})
is the
(x,x^{\prime})
matrix element of the operator
\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)\hat{X}(-\tau)
.

At last, putting everything together leads to the master equation

\displaystyle\derivative{t}\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)=-i\commutator{\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau\,\left\{N(t,t-\tau)\commutator{\hat{X}}{\comm{\hat{X}(-\tau)}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{i}{2}D(t,t-\tau)\commutator{\hat{X}}{\acomm{\hat{X}(-\tau)}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}}\right\}.

(7.11)

This is of the form presented in Eq. (
7.2
), with the dissipation and noise kernels encoding the non-unitary time evolution. To further explore the physical meaning behind these terms, let us apply the master equation for the case of a well-known model of the system of interest, namely a harmonic oscillator.

7.2
Dissipation, diffusion and decoherence

Suppose now that our system of interest is a harmonic oscillator with mass
M
and frequency
\Omega
, such that its free Hamiltonian takes the form

\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}=\frac{\hat{P}^{2}}{2M}+\frac{1}{2}M\Omega^{2}\hat{X}^{2},

(7.12)

with
\hat{X}
and
\hat{P}
being the (Schrödinger picture) position and momentum operators of the system satisfying the canonical commutation relation
[\hat{X},\hat{P}]=i
. The interaction picture position operator (which is the same as the Heisenberg picture operator when the system evolves only according to its self-Hamiltonian
\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
) reads
[
144
]

\hat{X}(t)=\hat{X}\cos(\Omega t)+\frac{1}{M\Omega}\hat{P}\sin(\Omega t).

(7.13)

Using this result in the master equation (
7.1
) yields

\displaystyle\derivative{t}\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)

\displaystyle=-i\commutator{\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}+\frac{1}{2}M\delta\Omega^2(t)\hat{X}^2}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}-i\gamma(t)\commutator{\hat{X}}{\acomm{\hat{P}}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}}

\displaystyle-\sigma^{2}(t)\commutator{\hat{X}}{\comm{\hat{X}}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}}-\Sigma^{2}(t)\commutator{\hat{X}}{\comm{\hat{P}}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}},

(7.14)

where

\displaystyle\delta\Omega^{2}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{M}\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau\,D(t,t-\tau)\cos(\Omega\tau),

(7.15a)

\displaystyle\gamma(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2M\Omega}\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau\,D(t,t-\tau)\sin(\Omega\tau),

(7.15b)

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau\,N(t,t-\tau)\cos(\Omega\tau),

(7.15c)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{2}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{M\Omega}\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau\,N(t,t-\tau)\sin(\Omega\tau).

(7.15d)

In the position representation, the master equation (
7.2
) reads

\displaystyle\partialderivative{t}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x,x^{\prime},t)=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{i}{2M}\quantity(\pdv[2]{x}-\pdv[2]{{x'}})-\frac{i}{2}M\quantity(\Omega^2+\delta\Omega^2(t))\quantity(x^2-x'^2)\right.

\displaystyle-\gamma(t)\quantity(x-x')\quantity(\pdv{x}-\pdv{{x'}})-\sigma^{2}(t)\quantity(x-x')^{2}

\displaystyle\left.+i\Sigma^{2}(t)\quantity(x-x')\quantity(\pdv{x}+\pdv{{x'}})\right]\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x,x^{\prime},t).

(7.16)

It is worth mentioning that an exact master equation for quantum Brownian motion can be obtained without imposing the weak coupling approximation, as we have done in Eq. (
7.1
). In that case, the expressions for the coefficients (
7.15
) become much more complicated, although the master equation maintains the same form (
7.2
) (see Refs.
[
80
,
29
]
). Remarkably, the exact master equation is found to be local in time even though it exhibits non-Markovian effects.

Let us now discuss the physical interpretation of each of the coefficients in Eq. (
7.15
). First, let us note that the coefficient
\delta\Omega^{2}(t)
, given by Eq. (
7.15a
), amounts to a renormalization of the natural frequency of the system (the Lamb-shift contribution). The unitary part of the time evolution is then that of a quantum harmonic oscillator with physical frequency
\quantity(\Omega^2+\delta\Omega^2(t))^{1/2}
.

The coefficient
\gamma(t)
, given by Eq. (
7.15b
), describes dissipation through momentum damping. To see this, let us consider the time evolution of the expectation value
\langle\hat{P}\rangle(t)=\textrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{S}}[\hat{P}\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)]
. Using the master equation (
7.2
) and working out the details using the canonical commutation relation, one is led to

\displaystyle\derivative{t}\langle\hat{P}\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{S}}\quantity[\hat{P}\dv{t}\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)]

\displaystyle=-M\quantity(\Omega^2+\delta\Omega^2(t))\langle\hat{X}\rangle(t)-2\gamma(t)\langle\hat{P}\rangle(t).

(7.17)

While the first term on the right-hand side describes the usual unitary evolution of the oscillator, the second term describes momentum damping. This is more evident when we take the full Markovian approximation and extend the integral upper limit to infinity, rendering the coefficient (
7.15b
) constant
[
146
]
. In that case, we have
\langle\hat{P}\rangle(t)\propto e^{-2\gamma t}\langle\hat{P}\rangle(0)
. Note that
\gamma(t)
is completely determined by the kernel
D(t,t^{\prime})
, which is why we call it the
dissipation
kernel.

The coefficient
\sigma^{2}(t)
describes decoherence in the position basis, as can be seen from the position representation of the master equation (
7.2
). Not only does it amount to environmental monitoring of the system position operator, but it also, as a consequence, describes diffusion
29
29

29

Since normal diffusion is observed in classical Brownian motion
[
97
]
, it is this similarity that rendered this canonical model the nomenclature of ”quantum Brownian motion”
[
146
]
.
in momentum. This can be seen from the time evolution of
\langle\hat{P}^{2}\rangle(t)

[
146
]
,

\derivative{t}\langle\hat{P}^{2}\rangle(t)=-M\quantity(\Omega^2+\delta\Omega^2(t))\langle\hat{X}\hat{P}+\hat{P}\hat{X}\rangle(t)-4\gamma(t)\langle\hat{P}^{2}\rangle(t)+2\sigma^{2}(t),

(7.18)

and, therefore,
\langle\hat{P}^{2}\rangle(t)\propto\sigma^{2}t
in the Markovian limit, when this coefficient becomes constant over time. For this reason,
\sigma^{2}(t)
is sometimes called the (normal) diffusion coefficient. It is also possible to show that the Wigner function for such a system obeys a diffusion equation, with the diffusion coefficient given by Eq. (
7.15c
)
[
146
]
.

Lastly, the coefficient
\Sigma^{2}(t)
is also related to decoherence and diffusion. However, in the Wigner representation of the master equation, this coefficient is tied to a double derivative, each with respect to a different variable (position and momentum) instead of a single one. For this reason, it is called the anomalous diffusion coefficient, and it leads, in many physical situations of interest, to a negligible influence compared to the one induced by the normal diffusion coefficient
[
146
]
.

Now, if we wish to gain a more insightful physical interpretation of the kernels
D(t,t^{\prime})
and
N(t,t^{\prime})
, we will need to specify the model for the environment. In quantum Brownian motion, the environment is described by a set of harmonic oscillators in thermal equilibrium. This model will allow us to derive an important theorem relating the dissipation and noise kernels, namely the fluctuation-dissipation theorem.

7.3
The fluctuation-dissipation theorem

Our derivation of the influence action (
6.34
) only assumes that the free Hamiltonian of the environment is quadratic, the initial state is Gaussian, and the coupling is linear, with no further specification. Now let us choose this environment to represent a set of harmonic oscillators with masses
m_{i}
and frequencies
\omega_{i}
such that the environment Hamiltonian reads

\hat{H}_{\mathcal{E}}=\sum_{i}\quantity(\frac{\hat{p}_i^2}{2m_i}+\frac{1}{2}m_i\omega_i^2\hat{q}_i^2),

(7.19)

with
\hat{q}_{i}
(
\hat{p}_{i}
) being the position (momentum) operator of the
i
th oscillator. Suppose now that the
i
th mode couples with the system with a coupling constant
c_{i}
, so that the environment operator
\hat{Q}
is given by
\hat{Q}=\sum_{i}c_{i}\hat{q}_{i}
. For the initial environment state, let us assume that the modes are in thermal equilibrium at a temperature
\beta^{-1}
such that
\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{E}}(0)=e^{-\beta\hat{H}_{\mathcal{E}}}/Z
, where
Z=\textrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{E}}(e^{-\beta\hat{H}_{\mathcal{E}}})
is the canonical partition function.

Now, since the modes do not directly interact with each other, we have, for
i\neq j
,

\expectationvalue{q_i(t)q_j(t')}_{\mathcal{E}}=\expectationvalue{q_i(t)}_{\mathcal{E}}\expectationvalue{q_j(t')}_{\mathcal{E}}=0,

(7.20)

and the dissipation and noise kernel can be written as

D(t,t^{\prime})=i\sum_{i}c_{i}^{2}\expectationvalue{\comm{\hat{q}_i(t)}{\hat{q}_i(t')}}_{\mathcal{E}}\theta(t-t^{\prime}),

(7.21a)

N(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}c_{i}^{2}\expectationvalue{\acomm{\hat{q}_i(t)}{\hat{q}_i(t')}}_{\mathcal{E}}.

(7.21b)

The interaction picture position operators are given by
[
144
]

\hat{q}_{i}(t)=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2m_{i}\omega_{i}}}\quantity(\hat{a}_ie^{-i\omega_it}+\hat{a}_i^\dagger e^{i\omega_it}),

(7.22)

where the
\hat{a}
’s (
\hat{a}^{\dagger}
’s) are annihilation (creation) operators satisfying the commutation relations

\begin{split}\commutator{\hat{a}_i}{\hat{a}_j}&=\commutator{\hat{a}_i^\dagger}{\hat{a}_j^\dagger}=0,\\
\commutator{\hat{a}_i}{\hat{a}_j^\dagger}&=\delta_{ij}.\end{split}

(7.23)

A direct calculation then yields

\expectationvalue{\comm{\hat{q}_i(t)}{\hat{q}_i(t')}}_{\mathcal{E}}=-\frac{i}{m_{i}\omega_{i}}\sin[\omega_{i}(t-t^{\prime})],

(7.24a)

\displaystyle\expectationvalue{\acomm{\hat{q}_i(t)}{\hat{q}_i(t')}}_{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{m_{i}\omega_{i}^{2}}\langle\hat{H}_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}\,\cos[\omega_{i}(t-t^{\prime})]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{m_{i}\omega_{i}}\quantity[\langle\hat{a}_i^2\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}\,e^{-i\omega_i(t+t')}+\langle(\hat{a}^\dagger_i)^2\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}\,e^{i\omega_i(t+t')}],

(7.24b)

where
\hat{H}_{i}=\omega_{i}\quantity(\hat{a}_i^\dagger\hat{a}_i+\frac{1}{2})=\frac{\omega_{i}}{2}\{\hat{a}_{i},\hat{a}_{i}^{\dagger}\}

is the free Hamiltonian operator of the harmonic oscillator with frequency
\omega_{i}
. Note that, in this model, the dissipation kernel is independent of the initial environmental state.

Since the environment is initially in a thermal state, we have
\langle\hat{a}_{i}^{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}=\langle(\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{i})^{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}=0
. Furthermore, the partition function for the
i
th mode reads

Z_{i}=\sum_{n_{i}=0}^{\infty}\exp\quantity[-\omega_i\beta\qty(n_i+\frac{1}{2})]=\frac{e^{-\omega_{i}\beta/2}}{1-e^{-\omega_{i}\beta}},

(7.25)

from which we find
[
123
]

\langle\hat{H}_{i}\rangle_{\mathcal{E}}=-\partialderivative{\beta}\ln Z_{i}=\frac{\omega_{i}}{2}\coth\quantity(\frac{\omega_i\beta}{2}).

(7.26)

Now, putting everything together into the dissipation and noise kernels leads to

D(\tau)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\omega\,J(\omega)\sin(\omega\tau)\,\theta(\tau),

(7.27a)

N(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\omega\,J(\omega)\coth\quantity(\frac{\omega\beta}{2})\cos(\omega\tau),

(7.27b)

where
\tau=t-t^{\prime}
and we have introduced the
spectral density

[
146
,
24
]

J(\omega)\equiv\sum_{i}\frac{c_{i}^{2}}{m_{i}\omega_{i}}\delta(\omega-\omega_{i}).

(7.28)

Note that the effects of thermal fluctuations are completely encoded in the kernel
N(\tau)
, which is why we called it the
noise
kernel. However, although the dissipation kernel is completely determined by the spectral density and does not contain any explicit dependence on the environmental temperature, these kernels are connected through an important relation. To see this, first note that
J(\omega)
is simply the sine Fourier transform of the dissipation kernel,

J(\omega)=\tilde{D}_{S}(\omega)=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,D(\tau)\sin(\omega\tau).

(7.29a)

Similarly,

\frac{1}{2}J(\omega)\coth\quantity(\frac{\omega\beta}{2})=\tilde{N}_{C}(\omega)=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,N(\tau)\cos(\omega\tau),

(7.29b)

with
\tilde{N}_{C}(\omega)
being the cosine Fourier transform of the noise kernel. Both equations together imply that

\tilde{N}_{C}(\omega)=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{D}_{S}(\omega)\coth\quantity(\frac{\omega\beta}{2}).

(7.30)

This expression is known as the
fluctuation-dissipation theorem

[
97
,
100
,
28
]
, as it essentially relates the thermal fluctuations of a system, encoded in the noise kernel, to dissipation.

The fluctuation-dissipation theorem arises from the fact that both effects depend on the spectral density
J(\omega)
. Typically, instead of working with the definition given by Eq. (
7.28
), one usually replaces the discrete sum with a continuous function of the environmental frequencies, often following some phenomenological motivation
[
146
,
29
]
. Let us see an example before closing this section.

7.4
The Caldeira-Leggett master equation

Usually, one takes a power-law expression for the spectral density,
J(\omega)\propto\omega^{\alpha}
, for some constant
\alpha
. The case
\alpha=1
is referred to as an
Ohmic spectral density
. For instance, we can take

J(\omega)=\frac{4M\gamma_{0}}{\pi}\omega\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}+\omega^{2}},

(7.31)

where
\gamma_{0}
is an effective coupling constant measuring the interaction between the system and the environment. The factor
\Lambda^{2}/(\Lambda^{2}+\omega^{2})
was added to avoid the spectral density from growing without bound, since this would be non-physical. One then introduces a frequency cutoff
\Lambda
, usually much greater than the natural frequencies of the system,
\Lambda\gg\Omega
. There are multiple ways to do that, and the prescription in Eq. (
7.31
) is called the Lorentz-Drude form
[
146
]
.

For the Ohmic spectral density (
7.31
), we find

D(\tau)=2M\gamma_{0}\Lambda^{2}e^{-\Lambda\tau},

(7.32a)

N(\tau)\simeq 2M\gamma_{0}\frac{\Lambda}{\beta}e^{-\Lambda\tau},

(7.32b)

where we took the high temperature limit for simplicity,
\beta\omega\ll 1
. As a further simplification, let us take the Markovian approximation versions of the coefficients (
7.15
),

\displaystyle\delta\Omega^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{M}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,D(\tau)\cos(\Omega\tau)=-2\gamma_{0}\frac{\Lambda^{3}}{\Lambda^{2}+\Omega^{2}},

(7.33a)

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2M\Omega}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,D(\tau)\sin(\Omega\tau)=\gamma_{0}\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}+\Omega^{2}},

(7.33b)

\displaystyle\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,N(\tau)\cos(\Omega\tau)=\frac{2M\gamma_{0}}{\beta}\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{\Lambda^{2}+\Omega^{2}},

(7.33c)

\displaystyle\Sigma^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{M\Omega}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\tau\,N(\tau)\sin(\Omega\tau)=-\frac{2\gamma_{0}}{\beta}\frac{\Lambda}{\Lambda^{2}+\Omega^{2}}.

(7.33d)

We can obtain even simpler expressions by recalling that
\Lambda\gg\Omega
. Particularly, we find that the anomalous diffusion coefficient behaves as
\Sigma^{2}=-\sigma^{2}/M\Lambda
, leading to an effect that is much smaller than normal diffusion.

Finally, the master equation (
7.2
) can be written as

\derivative{t}\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)=-i\commutator{\hat{H}'_{\mathcal{S}}}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}-i\gamma_{0}\commutator{\hat{X}}{\acomm{\hat{P}}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}}-\frac{2M\gamma_{0}}{\beta}\commutator{\hat{X}}{\comm{\hat{X}}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}},

(7.34)

where
\hat{H}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the harmonic oscillator Hamiltonian with its frequency shifted by an amount
-2\gamma_{0}\Lambda
. Eq. (
7.34
) is known as the
Caldeira-Leggett master equation

[
28
]
, and it describes the interaction of a system (here represented by a harmonic oscillator) with a Markovian Ohmic bath of non-interacting modes in thermal equilibrium at high temperature
\beta^{-1}
.

Note that our choice of spectral density implies that the diffusion coefficient is given by
\sigma^{2}=\gamma_{0}/\lambda_{\rm B}^{2}
, with
\lambda_{\rm B}=1/\sqrt{2M\beta^{-1}}
being the thermal de Broglie wavelength. Since the decoherence rate is proportional to
\sigma^{2}
, and macroscopic systems have an extremely small thermal de Broglie wavelength, we have shown a way to
describe
how decoherence occurs very quickly for such a system, which is compatible with the observation that one does not usually detect macroscopic objects in quantum superpositions
[
146
]
.

8
The Langevin equation

The localization of a quantum system due to decoherence raises interesting questions: if we no longer observe superpositions in the position basis, can we then make sense of the concept of "trajectory" for the quantum system? If yes, how can one obtain these trajectories? We will address the former question in Section
9
. In this section, we show how to obtain such trajectories from the so-called Langevin equation.

Regardless of the possibility of making sense of quantum trajectories, it is possible to take the classical limit of the system under consideration
30
30

30

Although mathematically the solutions to the Langevin equation fall under both cases of a ”quantum trajectory” (to be discussed in Section
9
