) and the trajectory in the classical limit, the physical meaning is distinct for each of them. While in the latter case one imposes the classical limit by hand, in the former case this notion arises only due to the decoherence mechanism, without any further assumption other than quantum theory itself
[
155
]
.
. In the path integral formulation, this is done by the method of stationary phase, which is based on the fact that the classical paths are the ones that render the total action stationary
[
63
]
. Let us now see how to obtain the equation of motion for the classical limit of a reduced quantum system coupled with an environment.

Recall from Eq. (
6.14
) that the reduced density matrix evolves in time according to

\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x,x^{\prime},t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x_{0}\differential x_{0}^{\prime}\,\int\mathcal{D}x\mathcal{D}x^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\mathcal{S}}[x]-S_{\mathcal{S}}[x']+S_{\rm IF}[x,x'])}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},0),

(8.1)

where all environmental influence is encoded in the influence action
S_{\rm IF}[x,x^{\prime}]
. Particularly, if we consider the linear coupling model, the influence action is given by Eq. (
6.34
) in terms of the dissipation and noise kernels. Assuming that this is the case, let us note that we can rewrite the term containing the noise kernel as
[
29
]

e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,u(t)N(t,t^{\prime})u(t^{\prime})}=\mathcal{C}\int\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\mathcal{N}(t)N^{-1}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{N}(t^{\prime})}e^{i\int\differential t\mathcal{N}(t)u(t)},

(8.2)

where
\mathcal{C}
is a normalization constant and
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}
denotes the path integral measure for the stochastic variable
\mathcal{N}(t)
. This simple functional identity allows us to rewrite the noise kernel influence as a stochastic average with Gaussian probability density given by

\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}]=\mathcal{C}\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\mathcal{N}(t)N^{-1}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{N}(t^{\prime})}.

(8.3)

For example, the one point and two point correlation functions are

\expectationvalue{\mathcal{N}(t)}_{\rm sto}=\int\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}\,\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}]\mathcal{N}(t)=0,

(8.4a)

\expectationvalue{\mathcal{N}(t)\mathcal{N}(t')}_{\rm sto}=\int\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}\,\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}]\mathcal{N}(t)\mathcal{N}(t^{\prime})=N(t,t^{\prime}).

(8.4b)

The time evolution of the reduced density matrix can then be written as

\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x,x^{\prime},t)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential x_{0}\differential x_{0}^{\prime}\,\int\mathcal{D}x\mathcal{D}x^{\prime}\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}\,\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(x_{0},x_{0}^{\prime},0)e^{iS_{\rm SEA}[x,x^{\prime},\mathcal{N}]},

(8.5)

with the stochastic effective action

\displaystyle S_{\rm SEA}[x,x^{\prime},\mathcal{N}]

\displaystyle=S_{\mathcal{S}}[x]-S_{\mathcal{S}}[x^{\prime}]+\int\differential t\,\mathcal{N}(t)\quantity[x(t)-x'(t)]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\quantity[x(t)-x'(t)]D(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[x(t')+x'(t')].

(8.6)

The equation of motion is obtained by setting
\delta S_{\rm SEA}=0
as usual, resulting in

\derivative{t}\quantity(\pdv{L_{\mathcal{S}}}{\dot{x}})-\partialderivative{L_{\mathcal{S}}}{x}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\differential t^{\prime}\,D(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[x(t')+x'(t')]=\mathcal{N}(t),

(8.7)

where
L_{\mathcal{S}}
is the system’s Lagrangian. Eq. (
8.7
) is the
Langevin equation
. If the system of interest is a harmonic oscillator, for instance, we have

M\ddot{x}(t)+M\Omega^{2}x(t)-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\differential t^{\prime}\,D(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[x(t')+x'(t')]=\mathcal{N}(t).

(8.8)

Note that the
D
term generates non-local, memory-dependent dissipation, while the
N
term acts as a stochastic force on the system. Once again, the name
noise kernel
for the two-point function
N(t,t^{\prime})
proves to be quite fitting.

Finally, before we close this chapter, let us address the question of the observability of individual solutions to the Langevin equation for
quantum systems
, i.e., when the classical limit is not imposed by hand, yet the trajectory description is still fitting. To do so, we shall turn to the
decoherent
(or
consistent
)
histories
approach to quantum mechanics.

9
Decoherent histories

Let us now present the decoherent histories formalism, which was developed primarily by Griffiths, Omnès, Gell-Mann, and Hartle
[
72
,
116
,
117
,
68
]
(see also Ref.
[
57
]
for a review). We begin by introducing the concept of history in quantum mechanics and then define the so-called decoherence functional while addressing its role in the formalism. Finally, we discuss the physical conditions for making sense of trajectories of quantum systems.

9.1
Histories in quantum mechanics

A quantum mechanical history is a sequence of quantum mechanical events at successive moments in time, thus characterized by a sequence of successive projection operators. Consider a closed quantum system, for instance, which at an initial time
t_{0}
is in the state described by the density matrix
\hat{\rho}_{0}
. At time
t_{1}>t_{0}
, the system will be described by

\hat{\rho}(t_{1})=\hat{U}(t_{1},t_{0})\hat{\rho}_{0}\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t_{1},t_{0}),

(9.1)

where
\hat{U}(t_{i},t_{j})
is the unitary time-evolution operator from time
t_{j}
to time
t_{i}
. We may now wonder whether the event corresponding to a set of mutually orthogonal projection operators
\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}}
occurs at
t_{1}
. The probability of such an occurrence is given by

\displaystyle p(\alpha_{1}t_{1})

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}\hat{\rho}(t_1)]=\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}\hat{U}(t_1,t_0)\hat{\rho}_0\hat{U}^\dagger(t_1,t_0)]

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{U}^\dagger(t_1,t_0)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}\hat{U}(t_1,t_0)\hat{\rho}_0]

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{\rho}_0],

(9.2)

where we have defined
\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})\equiv\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t_{1},t_{0})\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}}\hat{U}(t_{1},t_{0})
and the trace is taken over an orthonormal basis of the system’s Hilbert space. Once the projective measurement has been made, the system undergoes the usual non-unitary evolution,

\hat{\rho}(t_{1})\to\hat{\rho}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})=\frac{\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}}\hat{\rho}(t_{1})\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}}}{\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}\hat{\rho}(t_1)]}.

(9.3)

Now suppose we let the system evolve further to time
t_{2}>t_{1}
, where we ask about the event corresponding to projectors
\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{2}}
. The probability of this additional occurrence is

\displaystyle p(\alpha_{2}t_{2}|\alpha_{1}t_{1})

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}\hat{U}(t_2,t_1)\hat{\rho}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{U}^\dagger(t_2,t_1)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p(\alpha_{1}t_{1})}\textrm{Tr}[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{2}}\hat{U}(t_{2},t_{1})\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}}\hat{U}(t_{1},t_{0})\hat{\rho}_{0}\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t_{1},t_{0})\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}}\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t_{2},t_{1})]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p(\alpha_{1}t_{1})}\textrm{Tr}\left[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{2}}\hat{U}(t_{2},t_{1})\hat{U}(t_{1},t_{0})\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t_{1},t_{0})\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}}\hat{U}(t_{1},t_{0})\hat{\rho}_{0}\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t_{1},t_{0})\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\left.\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}}\hat{U}(t_{1},t_{0})\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t_{1},t_{0})\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t_{2},t_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p(\alpha_{1}t_{1})}\textrm{Tr}\left[\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t_{2},t_{0})\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{2}}\hat{U}(t_{2},t_{0})\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t_{1},t_{0})\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}}\hat{U}(t_{1},t_{0})\hat{\rho}_{0}]\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\left.\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t_{1},t_{0})\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}}\hat{U}(t_{1},t_{0})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p(\alpha_{1}t_{1})}\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{\rho}_0\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{p(\alpha_{1}t_{1})}\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{\rho}_0\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)],

(9.4)

where we used the unitarity of the time-evolution operators, as well as their composition rule, the cyclic property of the trace, and the idempotence of the projection operators.

The probability

\displaystyle p(\alpha_{2}t_{2},\alpha_{1}t_{1})

\displaystyle=p(\alpha_{2}t_{2}|\alpha_{1}t_{1})p(\alpha_{1}t_{1})

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{\rho}_0\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)]

(9.5)

is what we call the probability of the history
\quantity(\rho_0,t_0)\to\quantity(\alpha_1,t_1)\to\quantity(\alpha_2,t_2)
. Strictly speaking, this is an abuse of nomenclature since the quantity in Eq. (
9.1
) does not satisfy Kolmogorov’s third axiom of probability theory, the probability sum rule. For instance, let us consider another history in which no projection is made at time
t_{1}
, that is, the history
\quantity(\rho_0,t_0)\to\quantity(\alpha_2,t_2)
. In complete analogy with Eq. (
9.1
), the probability of
\alpha_{2}
occurrence at
t_{2}
is now given by

p(\alpha_{2}t_{2})=\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)\hat{\rho}_0].

(9.6)

We can use the identity
\sum_{\alpha_{1}}\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{1}}(t_{1})=1
to write this as

\displaystyle p(\alpha_{2}t_{2})

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)\hat{\rho}_0\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)]

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)\sum_{\alpha_1}\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{\rho}_0\sum_{\alpha_1'}\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1'}(t_1)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha_{1}}\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{\rho}_0\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\sum\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1},\alpha_{1}^{\prime}\\
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{1}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{\rho}_0\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1'}(t_1)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)].

(9.7)

By comparing this result with Eq. (
9.1
), we conclude that, in general,

p(\alpha_{2}t_{2})\neq\sum_{\alpha_{1}}p(\alpha_{2}t_{2},\alpha_{1}t_{1})

(9.8)

due to the term

\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{\rho}_0\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1'}(t_1)\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_2}(t_2)],

(9.9)

which is generally non-zero and represents interference between different quantum-mechanical histories. This is a key feature of quantum mechanics, namely the presence of interference terms that prevent probabilities from being assigned to quantum histories. For some applications, however, we may attempt to identify sets of histories that suffer negligible interference with each other and, therefore, to which probabilities can be assigned. These may be found by studying the quantity in Eq. (
9.9
).

9.2
Decoherence functional

For a pair of histories
[\alpha]
,
[\beta]
, where
[\alpha]
denotes the sequence of events
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{2}
,
\dots
, and
\alpha_{n}
at times
t_{1}<t_{2}<\dots<t_{n}
, and analogously for
[\beta]
, we define the
decoherence functional
as

\mathcal{D}\quantity[\alpha,\beta]=\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_n}(t_n)\dots\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_1}(t_1)\hat{\rho}_0\hat{\Pi}_{\beta_1}(t_1)\dots\hat{\Pi}_{\beta_n}(t_n)],

(9.10)

where

\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{k}}(t_{k})=\hat{U}^{\dagger}(t_{k},t_{0})\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{k}}\hat{U}(t_{k},t_{0}).

(9.11)

The decoherence functional may also be written in a more compact form as
[
29
]

\mathcal{D}\quantity[\alpha,\beta]=\textrm{Tr}\quantity{\Tilde{\textrm{T}}\qty[\prod_{j=1}^n\hat{\Pi}_{\beta_j}(t_j)]\textrm{T}\qty[\prod_{i=1}^n\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_i}(t_i)]\hat{\rho}_0}.

(9.12)

It is then straightforward to show that this functional satisfies the following properties:

1.

\mathcal{D}\quantity[\alpha,\beta]=\mathcal{D}^{*}\quantity[\beta,\alpha]
,

2.

\sum_{[\alpha],[\beta]}\mathcal{D}\quantity[\alpha,\beta]=1
,

3.

\mathcal{D}\quantity[\alpha,\alpha]\geq 0
,

4.

\sum_{[\alpha]}\mathcal{D}\quantity[\alpha,\alpha]=1
,

where we are denoting
\sum_{[\alpha]}\equiv\sum_{\alpha_{1}}\sum_{\alpha_{2}}\dots\sum_{\alpha_{n}}
.

The last two properties suggest that we identify the diagonal elements
\mathcal{D}[\alpha,\alpha]
as the probability for the history
(\rho_{0},t_{0})\to\quantity(\alpha_1,t_1)\to\dots\to\quantity(\alpha_n,t_n)
, namely
p(\alpha)=\mathcal{D}[\alpha,\alpha]
. This does not satisfy the probability sum rule, however, and, in general, we have
[
57
]

\displaystyle p(\alpha\vee\beta)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}[\alpha,\alpha]+\mathcal{D}[\beta,\beta]+2\textrm{Re}\mathcal{D}[\alpha,\beta]

\displaystyle=p(\alpha)+p(\beta)+2\textrm{Re}\mathcal{D}[\alpha,\beta].

(9.13)

As we discussed before, this violation prevents us from making sense of the notion of trajectory in quantum mechanics. On the other hand, this notion acquires meaning when there is strong decoherence,
\mathcal{D}[\alpha,\beta]=0
. One may then write the fundamental formula for the quantum mechanics of history as

\textrm{Re}\quantity{\mathcal{D}[\alpha,\beta]}=p(\alpha)\delta_{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}\dots\delta_{\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}.

(9.14)

This equation expresses the necessary and sufficient condition for us to assign probabilities to individual histories; at the same time, it tells us what those probabilities are. For most applications of the formalism, one may be satisfied to claim that a pair of mutually exclusive histories is consistent when

\textrm{Re}\quantity{\mathcal{D}[\alpha,\beta]}\ll\mathcal{D}[\alpha,\alpha],\,\mathcal{D}[\beta,\beta]\hskip 14.22636pt\textrm{for}\hskip 14.22636pt\alpha\neq\beta.

(9.15)

From now on, let us consider projections in the position basis, which is a kind of history implemented naturally in the path integral formalism. The projectors are represented by window functions
\hat{\Pi}_{\alpha_{k}}(t_{k})\to w_{\alpha}[x(t_{k})]
, which, for example, can be unity if the configuration at
t_{k}
satisfies the requirement of history
\alpha
and zero otherwise
[
155
,
29
]
. Eq. (
9.12
) can then be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\alpha,\beta]

\displaystyle=\int\differential y(0)\differential y^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle\times\int\mathcal{D}y\mathcal{D}y^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S[y]-S[y'])}\rho\quantity(y(0),y'(0),0)\quantity{\prod_iw_\alpha\qty[y(t_i)]}\quantity{\prod_jw_\beta\qty[y'(t_j)]}.

(9.16)

Here
S
is the action for the total system described by the generic coordinate
y
. Let us now study the decoherence functional for an open quantum system in order to establish under what conditions one can actually observe the quasi-classical trajectories arising from the Langevin equation.

9.3
Consistent histories for open quantum systems

Let us consider the open quantum system in the linear coupling model that we treated in previous sections. Let us also assume that the system of interest is a harmonic oscillator for illustration purposes. In that case, we are interested in histories where the system variable
x(t)
follows a trajectory
\chi(t)
, which is obtained by solving the Langevin equation with a given accuracy
\sigma_{\chi}(t)
. Now take the window functions to be Gaussians so that we can make the replacement
[
29
,
155
]

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathcal{S}}\quantity(x(0),x'(0),0)

\displaystyle\quantity{\prod_iw_\alpha\qty[x(t_i)]}\quantity{\prod_jw_\beta\qty[x'(t_j)]}\to

\displaystyle\to\exp\quantity{-\int\dd t\frac{1}{2\sigma^2_\chi(t)}\qty[(x-\chi)^2+(x'-\chi')^2]}

(9.17)

in the decoherence functional of two histories represented by trajectories
\chi(t)
and
\chi^{\prime}(t)
. The integration over the environmental variables is done as outlined in Section
6
, leading to

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\chi,\chi^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}x\mathcal{D}x^{\prime}\,\exp\left\{\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\quantity[u(t)iL(t,t')X(t')-\frac{1}{2}u(t)N(t,t')u(t')]\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\int\differential t\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}_{\chi}(t)}\quantity[(x-\chi)^2+(x'-\chi')^2]\right\},

(9.18)

where we defined
[
155
]

L(t,t^{\prime})\equiv-M\quantity(\dv[2]{t}+\Omega^2)\delta(t-t^{\prime})+D(t,t^{\prime}).

(9.19)

Also, recall that
X(t)=\quantity[x(t)+x'(t)]/2
and
u(t)=x(t)-x^{\prime}(t)
.

Now define
Y(t)=\quantity[\chi(t)+\chi'(t)]/2
and
v(t)=\chi(t)-\chi^{\prime}(t)
and change the path integral variables
(x,x^{\prime})\to(X,u)
so that the decoherence functional can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\chi,\chi^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\exp{-\int\dd t\frac{1}{2\sigma^2_\chi(t)}\qty[2Y^2(t)+\frac{1}{2}v^2(t)]}

\displaystyle\times\int\mathcal{D}X\mathcal{D}u\,\exp\quantity[-\frac{1}{2}\int\dd t\dd t'\,\mqty(X(t) & u(t))\mathbb{M}(t,t')\mqty(X(t') \\ u(t'))]

\displaystyle\times\exp\quantity{\int\dd t\,\qty[\mathbb{J}(t)]^T\mqty(X(t) \\ u(t))},

(9.20)

where we defined

\mathbb{M}(t,t^{\prime})=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\frac{2}{\sigma^2_\chi(t)}\delta(t-t') & -iL(t,t') \\ -iL(t,t') & \Tilde{N}(t,t')\endlx@physics@matrix),

(9.21a)

with
\tilde{N}(t,t^{\prime})=N(t,t^{\prime})+[2\sigma_{\chi}^{2}(t)]^{-1}\delta(t-t^{\prime})
, and also

\mathbb{J}(t)=\frac{1}{2\sigma_{\chi}^{2}(t)}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 4Y(t) \\ v(t)\endlx@physics@matrix).

(9.21b)

The Gaussian path integral in Eq. (
9.3
) is a traditional one in field theory with the well known result
[
149
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\chi,\chi^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\propto\exp{-\int\dd t\frac{1}{2\sigma^2_\chi(t)}\qty[2Y^2(t)+\frac{1}{2}v^2(t)]}

\displaystyle\times\exp\quantity[\frac{1}{2}\int\dd t\dd t'\,\qty[\mathbb{J}(t)]^T\mathbb{M}^{-1}(t,t')\mathbb{J}(t)].

(9.22)

Explicitly, we have

\mathbb{M}^{-1}(t,t^{\prime})=\quantity[\det\mathbb{M}(t,t')]^{-1}\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix\Tilde{N}(t,t') & iL(t,t') \\ iL(t,t') & \frac{2}{\sigma^2_\chi(t)}\delta(t-t')\endlx@physics@matrix)

(9.23)

with
\det\mathbb{M}(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}_{\chi}(t)}\delta(t-t^{\prime})\tilde{N}(t,t^{\prime})+L^{2}(t,t^{\prime})
. Then we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\chi,\chi^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\propto\exp\left\{-\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\frac{Y(t)}{\sigma_{\chi}^{2}(t)}\quantity[\delta(t-t')-\frac{2}{\sigma_\chi^2(t')}\qty[\det\mathbb{M}(t,t')]^{-1}\Tilde{N}(t,t')]Y(t^{\prime})\right.

\displaystyle+\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\frac{Y(t)}{\sigma_{\chi}^{2}(t)\sigma_{\chi}^{2}(t^{\prime})}\quantity[\det\mathbb{M}(t,t')]^{-1}iL(t,t^{\prime})v(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle\left.-\int\differential t\frac{v(t)}{4\sigma_{\chi}^{2}(t)}\quantity[1-\frac{1}{\sigma_\chi^4(t)}\qty[\det\mathbb{M}(t,t)]^{-1}]v(t)\right\}.

(9.24)

For simplicity, let us assume that the (dissipative) dynamics is much smaller than the noise,
L(t,t^{\prime})\ll\tilde{N}(t,t^{\prime})

[
29
]
, such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}[\chi,\chi^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\sim\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\,\quantity[N(t)+\qty(2\sigma_\chi^2(t))^{-1}]^{-1}\quantity[L(t)Y(t)]^{2}\right.

\displaystyle+\int\differential t\,\frac{i}{2\sigma_{\chi}^{2}(t)}Y(t)\quantity[N(t)+\qty(2\sigma_\chi^2(t))^{-1}]^{-1}L(t)v(t)

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\,N(t)\quantity[2\sigma_\chi^2(t)N(t)+1]^{-1}v^{2}(t)\right\}.

(9.25)

The probability of history
\chi
is obtained by setting
v=0
in Eq. (
9.3
),

\mathcal{D}[\chi,\chi]\sim\exp\quantity{-\frac{1}{2}\int\dd t\,\qty[N(t)+\qty(2\sigma_\chi^2(t))^{-1}]^{-1}\qty[L(t)\chi(t)]^2}.

(9.26)

Thus, the highest probabilities are associated with those trajectories that satisfy the equation of motion (without the noise),
L\chi=0
, while the deviations from determinism are measured by the factor
N+\quantity(2\sigma_\chi^2)^{-1}
.

Consistency between the two histories
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
follows from the behavior of Eq. (
9.3
) for increasing values of
v
. Particularly, two histories are approximately consistent when
v^{2}\geq 2\sigma_{\chi}^{2}+N^{-1}
. Now, if the noise is weak,
N^{-1}\gg\sigma_{\chi}^{2}
, then
v\gtrsim N^{-1}\gg\sigma_{\chi}^{2}
, which means that histories of accuracy
\sim\sigma_{\chi}
do not decohere. On the other hand, if we have strong noise,
N^{-1}\ll\sigma_{\chi}^{2}
, then
v\gtrsim\sigma_{\chi}
, which means that any history whose accuracy can be probed is consistent and, therefore, the solutions to the Langevin equation represent trajectories that actually describe physical reality. Of course, strong noise leads to less predictability, and one can find balance by saying that this "physical reality aspect" of the Langevin trajectories arises when we choose to follow these paths with accuracy given by
\sigma_{\chi}^{2}\sim N^{-1}

[
29
,
155
]
.

Chapter 3
Gravitational decoherence

We saw in Chapter
2
how decoherence is linked to the quantum-to-classical transition, which can explain why one does not usually detect macroscopic systems in quantum superpositions. Decoherence occurs due to interactions with an environment, such as photons, dust particles, and sometimes even the system’s own internal degrees of freedom. If this process is related to gravitational interactions, either directly or indirectly, we call it
gravitational decoherence
.

As we discussed in Chapter
1
, gravity is the weakest of the four known interactions, and, usually, other decoherence sources are much stronger than gravitational ones. This raises an immediate challenge for observing the effects of gravity in quantum superpositions: all other competing decoherence processes must be suppressed (see Ref.
[
131
]
for a proposal of a universal test of gravitational decoherence). From the theoretical side, the interest comes from the fact that gravity is universal and, therefore, cannot be shielded.

Gravitational decoherence is a broad term that encompasses any loss of coherence in a quantum system, either directly caused by the coupling with a (classical or quantum) gravitational field or related to gravitational effects in some way (see Refs.
[
14
,
79
]
for a review). It can refer, for instance, to collapse models in which the wavefunction collapse is understood as a physical process that occurs due to gravity. Quantum mechanics, as opposed to classical physics, seems to assign a special role to measurement processes, which are not described by the dynamical Schrödinger equation. Collapse models are modifications of quantum mechanics that typically introduce stochastic terms in the Schrödinger equation to account for the measurement postulate, or what is sometimes referred to as the wavefunction collapse. This is the viewpoint that the collapse is indeed a physical process. Since such processes are expected to be universal and more significant for macroscopic systems, some authors have proposed that gravity is behind the wavefunction collapse since it is also universal and scales with the mass of the quantum system
[
91
,
50
,
51
,
67
,
126
,
52
,
53
,
2
]
. Recently, a different viewpoint was proposed in which quantum and classical mechanics belong to different regimes, both emerging from some yet unknown physics at the Planck scale (as opposed to the viewpoint that classical physics emerges from its quantum counterpart), with a gravitational self-decoherence model that describes decoherence of quantum systems as they approach a Heisenberg cut
M_{\rm C}\sim M_{\rm P}
while maintaining the coherence of microscopic systems for which
m\ll M_{\rm C}

[
3
]
.

In this work, we consider a different type of gravitational decoherence. We take the viewpoint that, to the best of our knowledge, the quantum description is, in principle, valid for all systems in nature, including the gravitational dynamical degrees of freedom. We work within the limits of weak gravitational fields, and so its quantum description falls within the framework of perturbative quantum gravity as described in Chapter
1
. Our interest will be in investigating the decoherence of quantum and non-relativistic systems due to quantum fluctuations of spacetime (of course, one can also study decoherence induced by stochastic
classical
spacetime fluctuations; see Refs.
[
102
,
153
,
27
,
139
,
142
,
99
,
22
]
).

This chapter is not supposed to be an extensive review of the literature on gravitational decoherence, nor a complete description of selected works in the field. In Section
10
we outline the findings of Blencowe
[
19
]
, and Anastopoulos and Hu
[
6
]
, in which the authors obtained a Markovian master equation describing the decoherence of a non-relativistic system induced by a weak quantum gravitational field. In Section
11
we introduce another approach taken by Kanno et al.
[
90
]
that describes spatial localization induced by gravitons using the Feynman-Vernon influence functional. Finally, in Section
12
we describe a different kind of gravitational decoherence mechanism proposed by Pikovski et al.
[
133
,
134
]
. Here, decoherence occurs due to the coupling of the center-of-mass coordinate of a quantum system to its internal degrees of freedom induced by time dilation caused by a classical static gravitational potential. This is different from decoherence due to spacetime fluctuations, but it will serve as motivation for the problem of gravitational decoherence of a composite particle that we will study in Part
II
of this thesis.

10
Master equation for gravitational decoherence

We introduce the topic of gravitational decoherence by outlining the approach of Blencowe
[
19
]
, and also mentioning the equivalent findings of Anastopoulos and Hu
[
6
]
.

Blencowe
[
19
]
considers quantum matter as described by a massive scalar field
\varphi(x)
, with a mass parameter
m
. The metric field is expanded as in Eq. (
3.8
), i.e., the author considers the weak field limit where the dynamical degrees of freedom are a perturbation of Minkowski spacetime,
g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}+\kappa_{\rm g}h_{\mu\nu}
. The total action is then given by

S[\varphi,h_{\mu\nu}]=S_{\mathcal{S}}[\varphi]+S_{\mathcal{E}}[h_{\mu\nu}]+S_{\mathcal{I}}[\varphi,h_{\mu\nu}],

(10.1)

where
S_{\mathcal{S}}[\varphi]
is the free Klein-Gordon action,

S_{\mathcal{S}}[\varphi]=-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{4}x\quantity(\partial_\mu\varphi\partial^\mu\varphi+m^2\varphi^2),

(10.2)

S_{\mathcal{E}}[h_{\mu\nu}]
is the linearized Einstein-Hilbert action, Eq. (
3.10
), and the interaction between the scalar and the tensor fields is described by
31
31

31

Note that the author also works with second-order terms in the metric expansion in the interaction term.

S_{\mathcal{I}}[\varphi,h_{\mu\nu}]=\int\differential^{4}x\quantity[\frac{\kappa_{\rm g}}{2}T^{\mu\nu}(\varphi)h_{\mu\nu}+\frac{\kappa_{\rm g}^2}{4}U^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\varphi)h_{\mu\nu}h_{\rho\sigma}],

(10.3)

with

T_{\mu\nu}(\varphi)=\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial_{\nu}\varphi-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\rho}\varphi\partial^{\rho}\varphi-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}m^{2}\varphi^{2}

(10.4a)

being the scalar field energy-momentum tensor and
[
9
]

\displaystyle U^{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=-2\eta^{\sigma\mu}\partial^{\rho}\varphi\partial^{\nu}\varphi+\eta^{\rho\sigma}\partial^{\mu}\varphi\partial^{\nu}\varphi

\displaystyle+\quantity(\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\rho\mu}\eta^{\sigma\nu}-\frac{1}{4}\eta^{\rho\sigma}\eta^{\mu\nu})\quantity(\partial^\lambda\varphi\partial_\lambda\varphi+m^2\varphi^2).

(10.4b)

The author then proceeds to quantize both the scalar field and the metric perturbation using the closed time path integral approach while integrating over the gravitational variables, thereby treating the gravitons as the environment. As usual, one considers the initial state to be separable,
\hat{\rho}(0)=\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(0)\otimes\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{E}}(0)
, while the gravitons are considered to be initially in a thermal state with temperature
T_{\rm g}
(we will discuss the interpretation of this parameter near the end of this section). The result is the time evolution of the reduced density matrix
\rho_{\mathcal{S}}[\varphi,\varphi^{\prime},t]
in terms of the influence functional, in complete analogy with
32
32

32

See Ref.
[
29
]
for the influence functional approach applied to quantum fields.
Eq. (
6.14
). Then, by evaluating the influence action to lowest order in
\kappa_{\rm g}
and introducing a Lorenz gauge fixing term in the linearized Einstein-Hilbert action, Blencowe obtains a Born-approximated master equation for the scalar field given by

\displaystyle\derivative{t}\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)

\displaystyle=-i\commutator{\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\differential\tau\int\differential^{3}r\differential^{3}r^{\prime}\left\{N(\tau,\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime})\left(2\commutator{\hat{T}_{\mu\nu}(\vb{r})}{\comm{\hat{T}^{\mu\nu}(-\tau,\vb{r}')}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\commutator{{\hat{T}_\mu}\,^\mu(\vb{r})}{\comm{{\hat{T}_\nu}\,^\nu(-\tau,\vb{r}')}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}}\right)

\displaystyle-iD(\tau,\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime})\left(2\commutator{\hat{T}_{\mu\nu}(\vb{r})}{\acomm{\hat{T}^{\mu\nu}(-\tau,\vb{r}')}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.-\commutator{{\hat{T}_\mu}\,^\mu(\vb{r})}{\acomm{{\hat{T}_\nu}\,^\nu(-\tau,\vb{r}')}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}}\right)\right\}.

(10.5)

where
\hat{H}_{\mathcal{S}}
is the free scalar field Hamiltonian and

D(t,\mathbf{r})=\quantity(\frac{\kappa_{\rm g}}{4})^{2}\int\frac{\differential^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sin(\omega_kt)\frac{e^{i\mathbf{k}\dotproduct\mathbf{r}}}{\omega_{k}},

(10.6a)

N(t,\mathbf{r})=\quantity(\frac{\kappa_{\rm g}}{4})^{2}\int\frac{\differential^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\cos(\omega_kt)\quantity[1+2n(\omega_k)]\frac{e^{i\mathbf{k}\dotproduct\mathbf{r}}}{\omega_{k}},

(10.6b)

are the dissipation and noise kernels, with
\omega_{k}=\absolutevalue{\vb{k}}
and
n(\omega_{k})
being the Bose-Einstein distribution at temperature
T_{\rm g}
.

For the system, Blencowe proceeds to consider a specific class of coherent states that model stationary material objects as Gaussian matter "balls"
[
19
]
. Then, in the non-relativistic limit in which the dominant contribution comes from
T_{00}\simeq m^{2}\varphi^{2}/2
, and within the Markovian approximation and high temperature limit, the author finds

\partialderivative{t}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}[\varphi,\varphi^{\prime},t]=-\frac{T_{\rm g}}{2\pi}\quantity(\frac{\kappa_{\rm g}}{4})^{2}\quantity{\int\dd^3r\qty[\frac{1}{2}m^2\varphi^2(\vb{r})-\frac{1}{2}m^2{\varphi'}^2(\vb{r})]}^{2}\rho_{\mathcal{S}}[\varphi,\varphi^{\prime},t]+\dots,

(10.7)

where we only wrote explicitly the terms that are relevant to decoherence. For two matter "ball" states with energies
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, this Markovian master equation describes the decay of the off-diagonal density matrix elements with decoherence time

t_{\rm dec}=\frac{\hbar}{k_{B}T_{\rm g}}\quantity(\frac{E_{\rm P}}{\Delta E})^{2},

(10.8)

with
E_{\rm P}
being the Planck energy,
\Delta E=E_{1}-E_{2}
and we restored the universal constants.

Blencowe
[
19
]
argues that this result is "sufficiently basic" such that one can expect it to hold for more general cases than the scalar field model. In fact, this result was used to constrain quantum spacetime induced oscillation damping in (ultra-relativistic) neutrinos experiments in Refs.
[
59
,
54
]
, for instance.

Note from Eq. (
10.8
) that decoherence does not occur for superposition states with
\Delta E\ll E_{\rm P}
. Additionally,
gravitational decoherence occurs in the energy basis
, meaning that a spatial superposition of states
\ket{\vb{r}_1}
and
\ket{\vb{r}_2}
will not decohere
if they have the same energy
, for example.

By taking a slightly different path, Anastopoulos and Hu
[
6
]
arrive at the same conclusions. The authors also model the system as a massive scalar field, which interacts with the graviton thermal bath through a
3+1
decomposition of the total action. After obtaining the master equation, the authors restrict the analysis to the one-particle Hilbert subspace and take the non-relativistic limit, which leads to (within the Born-Markov approximation and in the high temperature limit)

\derivative{t}\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)=-\frac{i}{2m_{R}}\commutator{\hat{P}^2}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}-\frac{4\pi T_{\rm g}}{9m_{R}^{2}}\commutator{\hat{P}^2}{\comm{\hat{P}^2}{\hat{\rho}_{\mathcal{S}}(t)}},

(10.9)

for motion in one spatial dimension. Here
m_{R}
is the renormalized mass of the system and
\hat{P}
is the one-particle momentum operator.

If the initial state is a quantum superposition of two states that are localized in
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, or equivalently with velocities
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, decoherence will take place after a time

t_{\rm dec}=\frac{\hbar^{2}c^{5}}{Gk_{B}T_{\rm g}m_{R}^{2}V^{2}(\Delta v)^{2}},

(10.10)

where
V=(v_{1}+v_{2})/2
and
\Delta v=v_{1}-v_{2}
. By using the fact that
m_{R}^{2}V^{2}(\Delta v)^{2}=(\Delta E)^{2}
, this decoherence time can be easily seen to be equivalent to the one obtained by Blencowe
[
19
]
, Eq. (
10.8
).

Now, let us turn to the interpretation of the parameter
T_{\rm g}
, which we loosely referred to as the temperature of the graviton bath. As Anastopoulos and Hu
[
6
]
point out, the thermalization of the graviton environment over typical timescales cannot be assumed, since they interact very weakly. Instead, the parameter
T_{\rm g}
is to be thought of as a phenomenological one that simply characterizes the power spectral density of the gravitational noise, sometimes called
noise temperature
. It serves the same role as the temperature of usual thermal baths, but without necessarily being associated with any precise thermodynamic definition. Nevertheless, the observation of gravitational decoherence, as characterized by the parameter
T_{\rm g}
, would offer valuable information concerning the initial graviton state, which Anastopoulos and Hu
[
6
]
call the "textures of spacetime".

Naturally, the literature on master equations for gravitational decoherence is not limited to the works we discussed here. The ones we outlined in this section were obtained within the Born-Markov approximations in the non-relativistic limit, but they can also be applied to relativistic systems, such as photons, for instance
[
98
]
. Additionally, one can also obtain master equations without restricting to scalar fields as the matter system
[
75
,
118
]
. Furthermore, although we did not address the issue of renormalization in the master equation in this section, there is a discussion regarding which part of the derivation renormalization must take place
[
61
]
. Finally, for a non-Markovian master equation for gravitational decoherence that is valid for arbitrary temperatures of the graviton bath, we refer the reader to Ref.
[
40
]
.

11
Graviton-induced spatial localization

In the previous section, we saw that gravitational decoherence occurs in the energy basis. Nevertheless, we can analyze how gravitons may lead to spatial decoherence for a system initially in a superposition of position states, as long as they have different energies. This is precisely the decoherence analysis that was conducted by Kanno et al.
[
90
]
.

The approach of Kanno et al.
[
90
]
is closer in spirit to our own, which is why we will simply outline some steps towards their conclusions while leaving the details for Part
II
of this thesis. It differs from the approaches discussed in Section
10
in two main aspects. First, Kanno et al.
[
90
]
describe the quantum system as a non-relativistic point particle from the beginning. From the action for a free particle in curved spacetime, the interaction with gravitons arises from the introduction of Fermi normal coordinates with respect to the geodesic of another test particle and the usual metric expansion around the Minkowski background. Now, the use of this coordinate system works as long as the incoming gravitational radiation has wavelengths that are not smaller than some characteristic separation length, which is why we need to introduce an energy cutoff
\Lambda
(we will make this clearer in Chapter
4
). Second, the decoherence rate is obtained directly from the influence functional without the necessity of deriving a master equation.

By considering a case in which the system can move only along two classically distinguishable paths
\xi^{(1)}(t)
and
\xi^{(2)}(t)
, and the gravitons to be initially in the vacuum state, Kanno et al.
[
90
]
obtained the decoherence function
33
33

33

The authors work in natural units in which
\hbar=c=1
, but
G
is held explicit.

\Gamma(t)\sim\frac{m^{2}\Lambda^{6}\Xi^{2}}{M_{\rm P}^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\differential t_{1}\differential t_{2}\,\Delta\xi(t_{1})\Delta\xi(t_{2})F_{5}[\Lambda(t_{1}-t_{2})],

(11.1)

where
m
is the mass of the particle,
M_{\rm P}
is the Planck mass,
\Xi=(\xi^{(1)}+\xi^{(2)})/2
(assumed to be approximately time independent) and
\Delta\xi(t)=\xi^{(1)}(t)-\xi^{(2)}(t)
. The function
F
comes from the evaluation of the noise kernel for the initial graviton state, being defined by

F_{n}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{x^{n+1}}\int_{0}^{x}\differential y\,y^{n}\cos y.

(11.2)

For the specific configuration of the superposition state, Kanno et al.
[
90
]
(see also Ref.
[
23
]
) take

\Delta\xi(t^{\prime})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}2vt^{\prime}&\textrm{for}\hskip 5.69046pt0<t^{\prime}\leq t/2\\
2v(t-t^{\prime})&\textrm{for}\hskip 5.69046ptt/2<t^{\prime}<t\end{array}\right.,

(11.3)

for some constant velocity
v
. One then finds

\Gamma(t)\sim\frac{m^{2}v^{2}}{M_{\rm P}^{2}}f_{\rm v}^{(I)}(\Lambda t),

(11.4)

where
34
34

34

The subscript and the superscript will become clear in Chapter
5
.

f_{\rm v}^{(I)}(x)=1+\frac{2}{3x}\quantity[\sin x-8\sin\qty(\frac{x}{2})]+\frac{1}{x^{2}}\quantity[\frac{2}{3}\cos x-\frac{32}{3}\cos\qty(\frac{x}{2})+10].

(11.5)

Note that
f_{\rm v}^{(I)}(x)\sim O(1)
, and thus
\Gamma(t)\ll 1
holds as long as
mv\ll M_{\rm P}
, for which case decoherence does not occur. The off-diagonal density matrix elements, whose time evolution goes as
e^{-\Gamma(t)}
, will decay only for systems with momentum greater than the Planck mass, of order
M_{\rm P}\sim 10^{-8}
kg. However, the most massive quantum systems to have ever been put into spatial superposition are molecules with a mass of the order of
m\sim 10^{-22}
kg
[
69
,
62
,
124
]
, which illustrates the immense challenge of observing such gravitational decoherence. Nevertheless, as pointed out by Kanno et al.
[
90
]
, the decoherence rate can be enhanced by considering other configurations and also initial squeezed graviton states.

Kanno et al.
[
90
]
considered the quantum system to be a point particle. In Part
II
of this thesis, we will show how the internal degrees of freedom of a quantum system actually enhance such a mechanism of gravitational decoherence due to the universal character of gravity. For this reason, we will discuss another kind of gravitational decoherence, which occurs due to the coupling between internal and external variables induced by gravitational time dilation.

12
Decoherence due to gravitational time dilation

Contrary to the works we discussed in the previous sections, gravitational decoherence, as analyzed by Pikovski et al.
[
133
,
134
]
, is not due to spacetime fluctuations and occurs even in static gravitational fields. The idea is to consider a
composite
quantum system, by which we mean a point-like particle described by a single center-of-mass coordinate that contains dynamical internal degrees of freedom. For instance, a system with internal oscillatory motion (which can be used to track time) models what we understand as a clock. In general, a composite system is described by the state

\ket{\Psi}=\ket{\psi_{\rm ext}}\otimes\ket{\psi_{\rm int}},

(12.1)

where
\ket{\psi_{\rm ext}}
describes the quantum state of the center-of-mass variable, while
\ket{\psi_{\rm int}}
describes the internal degrees of freedom.

According to non-relativistic quantum mechanics, in the absence of interactions between the external and internal variables, the time evolution of the composite system is described by the Hamiltonian

\hat{H}=\hat{H}_{\rm ext}\otimes\hat{I}_{\rm int}+\hat{I}_{\rm ext}\otimes\hat{H}_{\rm int},

(12.2)

where
\hat{H}_{\rm ext}
(
\hat{H}_{\rm int}
) dictates the free evolution of the external (internal) degrees of freedom, and
\hat{I}_{\rm ext}
(
\hat{I}_{\rm int}
) is the identity operator in the external (internal) Hilbert space. The total time evolution is simply

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi(t)}

\displaystyle=e^{-i\hat{H}t}\ket{\Psi}

\displaystyle=e^{-i\hat{H}_{\rm ext}t}\ket{\psi_{\rm ext}}\otimes e^{-i\hat{H}_{\rm int}t}\ket{\psi_{\rm int}}.

(12.3)

The final state is still a product state.

The situation is different in a relativistic scenario (both special and general). Let us now consider the composite system in a general background spacetime and choose coordinates
x^{\mu}=(t,\mathbf{x})
to describe its worldline with respect to some laboratory frame. The system will evolve according to its proper time
\tau
. Now, as long as its constituents are enclosed in a spacetime region that is small enough such that one can neglect metric variations along the extension of the composite particle
35
35

35

This condition needs to be satisfied in order to have a precise definition of center-of-mass in the first place
[
171
]
.
, the total action will be given by

S=\int L_{\rm rest}\differential\tau,

(12.4)

where
L_{\rm rest}=-m+\mathscr{L}
, with
\mathscr{L}
denoting the Lagrangian describing the internal DoFs, and

\differential\tau=\differential t\sqrt{-g_{\mu\nu}\derivative{x^\mu}{t}\derivative{x^\nu}{t}}.

(12.5)

Note that gravity couples with the total rest energy of the system, which is compatible with the equivalence principle (see Ref.
[
171
]
for the precise definition of gravitational mass for composite systems).

Pikovski et al.
[
133
,
134
]
proceed to consider the Newtonian limit,

g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}-2\phi\delta_{\mu\nu},

(12.6)

with
\phi
being the static Newtonian potential, while also employing a
c^{-1}-
expansion. This allows us to avoid the full machinery of quantum field theory (in curved spacetime). Up to
O(c^{-2})
we have

\differential\tau\simeq\differential t\quantity[1+\phi(x)-\frac{v^2}{2}],

(12.7)

with
v
denoting the velocity of the composite particle in the laboratory frame. The extra terms inside brackets are responsible for the phenomenon of time dilation. Within this limit, the total Hamiltonian describing a composite system of mass
m
is found to be given by

\hat{H}=\hat{H}_{\rm ext}\otimes\hat{I}_{\rm int}+\hat{I}_{\rm ext}\otimes\hat{H}_{\rm int}+\quantity[\phi(\hat{X})-\frac{\Hat{P}^2}{2m^2}]\otimes\hat{H}_{\rm int}.

(12.8)

For a free particle, for instance, we have

\hat{H}_{\rm ext}=m+\frac{\hat{P}^{2}}{2m^{2}}+m\phi(\hat{X}).

(12.9)

Note that, in this scenario, the time evolution induces a coupling between internal and external degrees of freedom.

If we are interested in the superposition of external variables, such as spatial superposition, then the center-of-mass coordinate is the system of interest while the internal degrees of freedom act as an environment. By integrating out the latter while considering a stationary center-of-mass (in order to neglect momentum terms) and within the Born-Markov approximation, Pikovski et al.
[
133
,
134
]
obtain the decoherence time for an initial superposition of two different heights
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
given by

t_{\rm dec}\simeq\frac{\sqrt{2}\hbar}{\Delta E}\frac{c^{2}}{\Delta\phi},

(12.10)

where
\Delta E=\sqrt{\expectationvalue{\hat{H}_{\rm int}^2}-\expectationvalue{\hat{H}_{\rm int}}^{2}}
,
\Delta\phi=\phi(x_{2})-\phi(x_{1})
, and we restored the universal constants. For
N
degrees of freedom in thermal equilibrium at temperature
T_{\rm int}
, the three-dimensional Einstein solid model gives
\Delta E=\sqrt{N}k_{B}T_{\rm int}
, for instance
[
134
]
, and decoherence increases with the number of internal degrees of freedom and with internal temperature. As is the case with other models that receive the name of gravitational decoherence, the experimental realization of this time dilation induced effect faces the problem of controlling competing sources of decoherence (see Ref.
[
31
]
for a discussion).

The approach of Pikovski et al.
[
133
,
134
]
shows that gravity can induce decoherence in quantum systems even through indirect manifestations, such as coupling its center-of-mass coordinate with dynamical internal degrees of freedom, without needing to exhibit (classical or quantum) fluctuations itself. But we saw in previous sections that such fluctuations also lead to decoherence. Additionally, these can couple internal and external variables of a quantum composite system in the same way as the static Newtonian potential, and one may wonder how this contributes even further to the loss of quantum coherence in such systems. This will be the subject of Part
II
of this thesis, together with the consequences of gravitational decoherence on entropy production in quantum systems.

Part II
Graviton-induced decoherence and entropy production

Chapter 4
Quantum system interacting with a graviton environment

Let us start by considering an open quantum system interacting with both a Newtonian gravitational potential and a bath of gravitons. To be specific, here we assume a non-relativistic quantum particle described by external and internal degrees of freedom (DoFs). This could represent a molecule for which the external DoFs are its center-of-mass coordinate, while the internal DoFs could be its vibrational modes, for instance. We will simply refer to it as a composite particle. Our main goal in this chapter is to obtain an expression for the reduced density matrix describing the external DoFs only. In order to do that, we start with the classical action describing the simultaneous interaction of a composite system with gravitational radiation and a static Newtonian potential in Section
13
. Then, in Section
14
, we proceed to integrate over the gravitational variables, treating them as an environment. Finally, we integrate over the internal DoFs of the system in Section
15
and obtain the desired reduced density matrix at time
t
. This will serve as the starting point for analyzing decoherence and entropy production in subsequent chapters.

13
The classical action

We could begin by writing down the Lagrangian of a single composite particle coupled with a classical gravitational wave. However, as discussed in Chapter
1
, a single particle is not enough to probe the effects of a gravitational field, since a particle at rest will remain at rest at all times in the TT frame. Let us start then by writing the classical action of a weak gravitational field coupled to a pair of free-falling massive particles. The total action takes the form

S=S_{\rm matter}+S_{\rm EH},

(13.1)

where the first term describes the pair of freely falling test masses, while the second term is the Einstein-Hilbert action, which describes the dynamics of the metric field
g_{\mu\nu}
.

13.1
The matter action

By denoting the spacetime coordinates of each particle by
\zeta^{\mu}
and
\xi^{\mu}
, the matter action can be written as
[
171
]

S_{\rm matter}=-M\int\differential t\,\sqrt{-g_{\mu\nu}\dot{\zeta}^{\mu}\dot{\zeta}^{\nu}}+\int\differential t\,L_{\rm rest}\sqrt{-g_{\mu\nu}\dot{\xi}^{\mu}\dot{\xi}^{\nu}},

(13.2)

where
M
is the mass of the first particle (with coordinates
\zeta^{\mu}
), and
L_{\rm rest}
denotes the rest Lagrangian of the second particle (with coordinates
\xi^{\mu}
). In writing down the action (
13.2
), we assumed that the first particle has no (dynamical) internal DoFs. For the second particle, the rest Lagrangian is of the form

L_{\rm rest}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\,\bar{t})=-m+\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\,\bar{t}).

(13.3)

In this equation,
m
is the mass of the second particle, while
\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\,\bar{t})
describes its internal degrees of freedom with coordinate
\varrho
(relative to the center-of-mass coordinate) and generalized velocity
\dot{\varrho}=\derivative*{\varrho}{t}
. We have also defined
\bar{t}=\derivative*{t}{\tau}
, with
\tau
being the particle’s proper time.

Now, let us take the first particle to be at rest at the origin of our coordinate system,
\zeta^{\mu}(t)=t\,\delta_{0}^{\mu}
, such that the coordinate time
t
is interpreted as its proper time, and let us assume that
M\gg L_{\rm rest}
. Under these assumptions, the first term in the action (
13.2
) essentially has no dynamics. Since our interest relies on the composite particle, we will simply refer to it as the system from now on.

In the context we described above, it becomes appropriate to think of
(t,\xi^{i})
as the Fermi normal coordinates defined with respect to the worldline of the heavier particle (see Appendix
7
). In these coordinates, we can write the metric components as [Eqs. (
7.K.37
)]

\displaystyle g_{00}(t,\xi^{i})

\displaystyle=-1-R_{i0j0}(t,0)\xi^{i}\xi^{j}+O(\xi^{3}),

(13.4a)

\displaystyle g_{0i}(t,\xi^{i})

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}R_{0jik}(t,0)\xi^{j}\xi^{k}+O(\xi^{3}),

(13.4b)

\displaystyle g_{ij}(t,\xi^{i})

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}-\frac{1}{3}R_{ikjl}(t,0)\xi^{k}\xi^{l}+O(\xi^{3}),

(13.4c)

where
R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
is the Riemann curvature tensor. Physically, the use of Fermi normal coordinates allows us to interpret the coordinates
\xi^{i}
as not simply describing a single particle in an arbitrary coordinate system, but rather as the geodesic deviation between two test masses (Figure
6
). Following the discussion at the end of Appendix
7
, we emphasize that this expansion of the metric components holds as long as
\xi\ll R_{0}
, with
R_{0}
being the scale over which the metric changes appreciably.

Figure 6:
Two test masses
M
and
m
, with
M\gg m
, and their geodesic deviation in Fermi normal coordinates, represented by the vector
\boldsymbol{\xi}
. The mass
m
is also described by internal degrees of freedom, represented by the curly red lines.

In our parametrization,
\xi^{0}(t)=t
, thus resulting

\displaystyle\sqrt{-g_{\mu\nu}(\xi)\dot{\xi}^{\mu}\dot{\xi}^{\nu}}

\displaystyle\simeq\sqrt{1+R_{i0j0}(t,0)\xi^{i}\xi^{j}-\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^{i}\dot{\xi}^{j}}

\displaystyle\simeq 1-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^{i}\dot{\xi}^{j}+\frac{1}{2}R_{i0j0}(t,0)\xi^{i}\xi^{j}.

(13.5)

Next, let us specify our metric field to describe small perturbations
h_{\mu\nu}
around some background spacetime
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
as in Eq. (
4.1
), namely
g_{\mu\nu}=\gamma_{\mu\nu}+h_{\mu\nu}
, with
\absolutevalue{h_{\mu\nu}}\ll\absolutevalue{\gamma_{\mu\nu}}
. Recall from Eq. (
4.9
) that the Riemann tensor associated with
g_{\mu\nu}
is given by

{R^{\rho}}_{\sigma\mu\nu}={R^{\rho}}_{\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma]+2D_{[\mu}{C^{\rho}}_{\nu]\sigma}+2{C^{\rho}}_{\delta[\mu}{C^{\delta}}_{\nu]\sigma}.

(13.6)

where
D_{\mu}
is the covariant derivative compatible with
\gamma_{\mu\nu}
and whose commutator defines
{R^{\rho}}\,_{\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma]
. The tensor
{C^{\rho}}_{\mu\nu}
reads, up to second order in the metric perturbation,

\displaystyle{C^{\rho}}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}g^{\rho\sigma}\quantity(D_\mu g_{\nu\sigma}+D_\nu g_{\sigma\mu}-D_\sigma g_{\mu\nu})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{\rho\sigma}\quantity(D_\mu h_{\nu\sigma}+D_\nu h_{\sigma\mu}-D_\sigma h_{\mu\nu}),

(13.7)

which follows from metric compatibility. Then, an explicit calculation yields

\displaystyle R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma]+\frac{1}{2}\quantity({R^\lambda}\,_{\sigma\mu\nu}[\gamma]h_{\lambda\rho}-{R^\lambda}\,_{\rho\mu\nu}[\gamma]h_{\sigma\lambda})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\quantity(D_\mu D_\sigma h_{\rho\nu}-D_\mu D_\rho h_{\nu\sigma}-D_\nu D_\sigma h_{\rho\mu}+D_\nu D_\rho h_{\mu\sigma}),

(13.8)

where
R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}=g_{\rho\eta}{R^{\eta}}_{\sigma\mu\nu}
as usual. The interaction between the system variables and the spacetime curvature then takes the form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}R_{i0j0}(t,0)\xi^{i}\xi^{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}R_{i0j0}[\gamma](t,0)\xi^{i}\xi^{j}+\frac{1}{4}\evaluated{\qty({R^\lambda}\,_{0i0}[\gamma]h_{\lambda j}-{R^\lambda}\,_{ij0}[\gamma]h_{0\lambda})}_{\xi=0}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}\evaluated{\qty(D_iD_0h_{j0}+D_0D_ih_{j0}-D_iD_jh_{00}-D_0D_0h_{ij})}_{\xi=0}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}.

(13.9)

Up to this point, everything works for a general background. Ultimately, we are interested in considering the background metric in the Newtonian limit, for which
\gamma_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}-2\phi\delta_{\mu\nu}
, with
\phi(x)
being the time-independent gravitational potential. In that case, as we saw in Chapter
1
, the metric perturbation
h_{\mu\nu}
can be chosen to satisfy the TT gauge conditions
36
36

36

Since we will only work within the TT gauge, we will not use the superscript ’TT’. From this point on, the metric perturbation field
h_{ij}
is understood to satisfy the TT gauge conditions, Eq. (
4.30
).
(
4.30
), and we find

R_{i0j0}[\gamma](\xi)=\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\phi(\xi).

(13.10)

Putting everything back together in Eq. (
13.1
) yields

\frac{1}{2}R_{i0j0}(t,0)\xi^{i}\xi^{j}=\evaluated{\qty[\frac{1}{2}\partial_i\partial_j\phi+\frac{1}{4}\qty(\partial^k\partial_i\phi)h_{kj}-\frac{1}{4}D_0D_0h_{ij}]}_{\xi=0}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}.

(13.11)

The covariant derivatives in the last term differ from partial derivatives by the Christoffel symbols. Since those are evaluated along the geodesic, they vanish by Fermi’s second condition [Eq. (
7.K.18
)] and, finally, the interaction term can be written as

\frac{1}{2}R_{i0j0}(t,0)\xi^{i}\xi^{j}=-\quantity[\frac{1}{2}\Phi_{ij}+\frac{1}{4}{\Phi^k}_ih_{kj}(t,0)+\frac{1}{4}\Ddot{h}_{ij}(t,0)]\xi^{i}\xi^{j},

(13.12)

where we introduced the
tidal tensor

\Phi_{ij}=-\evaluated{\qty(\partial_i\partial_j\phi)}_{\xi=0}

[
76
,
39
]
. Specifically, for two test masses close to a spherically symmetric (even if only approximately) Newtonian source, such as Earth, the much lighter one is under the influence of the gravitational potential given by

\phi(\boldsymbol{\xi})=-\frac{M_{N}}{\absolutevalue{\boldsymbol{\xi}-\vb{R}}},

(13.13)

with
M_{N}
being the source’s mass and
\mathbf{R}
being the radius vector that points from the mass
M
(the origin of our coordinate system) to the center of the Newtonian source, such that
\absolutevalue{\vb{R}}\simeq R_{N}
, which represents its radius (Figure
7
). From Eq. (
13.13
) one can easily show that the tidal tensor takes the form

\displaystyle\Phi_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\evaluated{\qty(\partial_i\partial_j\phi)}_{\xi=0}=\frac{M_{N}}{R^{3}}\quantity(\frac{3R_iR_j}{R^2}-\delta_{ij})

\displaystyle=\frac{M_{N}}{R_{N}^{3}}(3\delta_{i3}\delta_{j3}-\delta_{ij}).

(13.14)

Figure 7:
The two test masses
M
and
m
are in the vicinity of a much bigger and much more massive spherical mass
M_{N}
, with radius
R_{N}
, located at
\mathbf{R}\simeq R_{N}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}
with respect to the mass
M
.

Note from Eq. (
13.12
) that the validity of the metric expansion in Fermi normal coordinates holds as long as
\xi^{2}\ll R_{N}^{3}/M_{N}
, and also
\xi^{2}\ll\omega^{-2}
, with
\omega
denoting the angular frequency of the gravitational waves. Hence, we need to introduce an energy cutoff for the incident gravitational radiation of the order
\Lambda\sim L_{0}^{-1}
, where
L_{0}
is some typical geodesic separation, which we sometimes refer to as the "detector size".

At last, using Eqs. (
13.1
) and (
13.12
), the matter action becomes

\displaystyle S_{\rm matter}

\displaystyle=\int\differential t\,\quantity[\frac{1}{2}m\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j+\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\Bar{t})-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\Bar{t})\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\,L_{\rm rest}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\bar{t})\Phi_{ij}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\int\differential t\,h_{ij}(t,0)\quantity{L_{\rm rest}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\Bar{t}){\Phi^i}_k\xi^k\xi^j+\dv[2]{t}\qty[L_{\rm rest}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\Bar{t})\xi^i\xi^j]},

(13.15)

where we dropped non-dynamical terms and integrated by parts while dropping boundary contributions.

13.2
The gravitational action

Now, for the gravitational field, we saw in Chapter
1
that the Einstein-Hilbert action for the metric expansion around a Newtonian background, and with the perturbation in the TT gauge, is given by Eq. (
4.3
), which we repeat here for convenience:

S_{\rm EH}=\frac{1}{64\pi}\int\differential^{4}x\,\quantity(h_{ij}\Box h^{ij}+2\phi\,h_{ij}\delta_{\mu\nu}\partial^\mu\partial^\nu h^{ij}).

(13.16)

Note that the interaction between the gravitational radiation and the classical Newtonian potential is of order
O(\phi h^{2})
, while in Eq. (
13.1
) they simultaneously couple with the system through an interaction of order
O(\phi h)
, which is therefore the dominant one in perturbation theory. The interaction term in Eq. (
13.16
) leads to coupling between graviton modes. In fact, we showed in Appendix
8
that this term is physically associated with graviton scattering, leading to a differential cross section that behaves as
\derivative*{\sigma}{\Omega}\sim M_{N}^{2}\theta^{-4}
, with
\theta\ll 1
being the scattering angle. Since this angle is very small, we will neglect this less dominant interaction
37
37

37

This interaction term is also neglected in Ref.
[
36
]
where the authors compute quantum gravity corrections to the fall of test masses, for instance.
and consider only the
O(\phi h)
contribution in Eq. (
13.1
).

Hence, we consider the gravitons to be described by their usual flat-spacetime Lagrangian,

S_{\rm EH}=-\frac{1}{64\pi}\int\differential^{4}x\,\partial_{\mu}h_{ij}\partial^{\mu}h^{ij},

(13.17)

from which we immediately obtain the wave equation
\Box h_{ij}=0
. The general solution takes the form

h_{ij}(t,\mathbf{x})=\int\differential^{3}k\,\sum_{s=+,\crossproduct}\epsilon_{ij}^{s}(\mathbf{k})q_{s}(t,\mathbf{k})e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}},

(13.18)

with
\epsilon_{ij}^{s}
denoting the polarization tensor that satisfies the transversality and traceless conditions, Eqs. (
2.27
), as well as the normalization condition (
2.31
). Additionally, the reality of
h_{ij}
implies that

\epsilon_{ij}^{*s}(\mathbf{k})q_{s}^{*}(t,\mathbf{k})=\epsilon_{ij}^{s}(-\mathbf{k})q_{s}(t,-\mathbf{k}).

(13.19)

By plugging Eq. (
13.18
) into Eq. (
13.17
), we obtain

\displaystyle S_{\rm EH}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{64\pi}\int\differential^{4}x\,\int\differential^{3}k\,\differential^{3}k^{\prime}\,\sum_{s,s^{\prime}}\epsilon_{ij}^{s}(\mathbf{k})\epsilon^{ij}_{s^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\quantity[-\dot{q}_s(t,\vb{k})\dot{q}_{s'}(t,\vb{k}')-\vb{k}\cdot\vb{k}'q_s(t,\vb{k})q_{s'}(t,\vb{k}')]e^{i\quantity(\vb{k}+\vb{k}')\cdot\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(2\pi)^{3}}{64\pi}\int\differential t\int\differential^{3}k\,\sum_{s,s^{\prime}}\epsilon_{ij}^{s}(\mathbf{k})\epsilon^{ij}_{s^{\prime}}(-\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\quantity[-\dot{q}_s(t,\vb{k})\dot{q}_{s'}(t,-\vb{k})+\vb{k}^2q_s(t,\vb{k})q_{s'}(t,-\vb{k})]

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}\int\differential t\int\differential^{3}k\,\sum_{s}\quantity[\abs{\dot{q}_s(t,\vb{k})}^2-\vb{k}^2\abs{q_s(t,\vb{k})}^2]

\displaystyle=\int\differential t\int\differential^{3}k\,\sum_{s}\frac{1}{2}\quantity[m_{\rm g}\abs{\dot{q}_s(t,\vb{k})}^2-m_{\rm g}\vb{k}^2\abs{q_s(t,\vb{k})}^2],

(13.20)

where we defined
m_{\rm g}=\pi^{2}/2
and used Eq. (
13.19
), as well as the normalization condition (
2.31
), and the identity
\int\differential^{3}x\,e^{i\quantity(\vb{k}+\vb{k}')\cdot\mathbf{x}}=(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{3}(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{k}^{\prime})
.

Let us note that the action (
13.2
) has the form
S_{\rm EH}=\int\differential t\int\differential^{3}k\,\sum_{s}L_{s}(t,\mathbf{k})
, with the Lagrangian
L_{s}(t,\mathbf{k})
for each mode describing a harmonic oscillator with mass
m_{\rm g}
and frequency
\omega=\absolutevalue{\vb{k}}
. The associated Hamiltonian takes the usual form

H_{s}(t,\mathbf{k})=\frac{\absolutevalue{p_s(t,\vb{k})}^{2}}{2m_{\rm g}}+\frac{1}{2}m_{\rm g}\mathbf{k}^{2}\absolutevalue{q_s(t,\vb{k})}^{2},

(13.21)

with
p_{s}(t,\mathbf{k})=\partialderivative{L_s(t,\vb{k})}{\dot{q}_s(t,\vb{k})}=m_{\rm g}\dot{q}_{s}(t,\mathbf{k})
.

13.3
The total action

At last, by plugging the general solution (
13.18
) into the matter action (
13.1
), the total action (
13.1
) takes the form

S=S_{\rm sys}+S_{\rm grav}+S_{\textrm{s}+\textrm{g}},

(13.22)

where

S_{\rm sys}=\int\differential t\,\quantity[\frac{1}{2}m\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j+\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\Bar{t})-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\Bar{t})\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j-\frac{1}{2}L_{\rm rest}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\Bar{t})\Phi_{ij}\xi^i\xi^j],

(13.23a)

S_{\rm grav}=\int\differential t\int\differential^{3}k\,\sum_{s}\frac{1}{2}\quantity[m_{\rm g}\abs{\dot{q}_s(t,\vb{k})}^2-m_{\rm g}\vb{k}^2\abs{q_s(t,\vb{k})}^2],

(13.23b)

S_{\textrm{s}+\textrm{g}}=\int\differential t\int\differential^{3}k\sum_{s}q_{s}(t,\mathbf{k})X^{s}(t,\mathbf{k}),

(13.23c)

and we have defined

X^{s}(t,\mathbf{k})=-\frac{1}{4}\epsilon_{ij}^{s}(\mathbf{k})\quantity{L_{\rm rest}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\Bar{t}){\Phi^i}_k\xi^k\xi^j+\dv[2]{t}\qty[L_{\rm rest}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\Bar{t})\xi^i\xi^j]}.

(13.24)

The action (
13.22
) describes the interaction of a system, characterized by both external and internal DoFs, with classical gravitational radiation in a Newtonian background. This will be our starting point for describing the interaction of such a system with gravitons. The quantization of the metric perturbation field, which we discussed in Chapter
1
, can be achieved by promoting the field amplitudes to operators in Hilbert space in the usual way. Since our ultimate goal is to describe the system variables alone, the weak quantized gravitational field shall be treated as an environment; for this case, we will follow the Feynman-Vernon influence functional approach to open quantum systems, as described in Chapter
2
. In fact, one can anticipate the resemblance to quantum Brownian motion from the total action (
13.22
).

14
Gravitational influence functional

Let us now quantize both the system and the gravitational degrees of freedom. Suppose that, at initial time
t=0
, the system and the gravitons are uncorrelated, and the total density matrix can be written as

\hat{\rho}(0)=\hat{\rho}_{\rm sys}(0)\otimes\hat{\rho}_{\rm grav}(0),

(14.1)

with
\hat{\rho}_{\rm sys}(t)
(
\hat{\rho}_{\rm grav}(t)
) denoting the reduced density matrix used to compute local system (gravitational) observables. This is obtained by taking a partial trace of the total density matrix with respect to the gravitational (system) variables.

For
t>0
, the system interacts with the gravitational field, and the total state becomes entangled in general. Since we are not interested in the gravitons final state, we must integrate over the gravitational variables such that the system becomes effectively open, and the weak quantum gravitational field is viewed as an environment. This description is accomplished by using the Feynman-Vernon influence functional (Chapter
2
), which is written as

\mathcal{F}[X,X^{\prime}]=e^{iS_{\rm IF}[X,X^{\prime},t]},

(14.2)

where
S_{\rm IF}
is the influence action and
X
and
X^{\prime}
denote two different histories of the system variables. In terms of the influence functional, the time evolution of the system reduced density matrix is given by

\rho_{\rm sys}(X,X^{\prime},t)=\int\differential X_{0}\differential X_{0}^{\prime}\,\mathcal{J}_{\rm sys}(X,X^{\prime},t|X_{0},X_{0}^{\prime},0)\rho_{\rm sys}(X_{0},X_{0}^{\prime},0),

(14.3)

where
\rho_{\rm sys}(X,X^{\prime},t)=\matrixelement{X}{\hat{\rho}_{\rm sys}(t)}{X'}
and the evolution operator for the reduced density matrix reads

\mathcal{J}_{\rm sys}(X,X^{\prime},t|X_{0},X_{0}^{\prime},0)\equiv\int\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}X(0)\,=\,X_{0}\\
X^{\prime}(0)\,=\,X_{0}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{\begin{subarray}{c}X(t)\,=\,X\\
X^{\prime}(t)\,=\,X^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}X\mathcal{D}X^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\rm sys}[X]-S_{\rm sys}[X'])}\mathcal{F}[X,X^{\prime}],

(14.4)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[X,X^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\differential q\differential q_{0}\differential q_{0}^{\prime}\,\rho_{\rm grav}(q_{0},q_{0}^{\prime},0)

\displaystyle\times\int\displaylimits_{\begin{subarray}{c}q(0)\,=\,q_{0}\\
q^{\prime}(0)\,=\,q_{0}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}^{q(t)\,=\,q^{\prime}(t)\,=\,q}\mathcal{D}q\mathcal{D}q^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\rm grav}[q]+S_{\textrm{s}+\textrm{g}}[X,q]-S_{\rm grav}[q']-S_{\textrm{s}+\textrm{g}}[X',q'])},

(14.5)

with
q
and
q^{\prime}
denoting two different histories of the gravitational environment variables.

The influence action encodes all influence of the environment on the system. In our case, the system is coupled with the infinite graviton modes. Since each mode (and polarization) is independent of all the others, they can be treated separately in such a way that the total influence action is the sum of the action corresponding to each mode
38
38

38

Note that this would no longer be true if we had kept the scattering term in the graviton action.
(and polarization). This is because the total influence functional for a system coupled with statistically and dynamically independent environments is simply the product of each individual influence functional [Eq. (
6.20
)]. Furthermore, we note that the environment action (the Einstein-Hilbert action) is quadratic in the field amplitudes, and the coupling with the system variable
X^{s}(t,\mathbf{k})
is linear. This is then a special case of the linear coupling model, described in full detail in Section
6.3
, for which the path integrals can be computed analytically. Translating the results of that section in terms of the variables described by Eq. (
13.22
) leads to the influence action

\displaystyle S_{\rm IF}[X,X^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\sum_{s}\int\differential^{3}k\,\quantity[X^s(t,\vb{k})-{X'}^s(t,\vb{k})]d_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\quantity[X^s(t',\vb{k})+{X'}^s(t',\vb{k})]

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{2}\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\sum_{s}\int\differential^{3}k\,\quantity[X^s(t,\vb{k})-{X'}^s(t,\vb{k})]n_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\quantity[X^s(t',\vb{k})-{X'}^s(t',\vb{k})],

(14.6)

where

d_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})=i\expectationvalue{\comm{\hat{q}_s(t,\vb{k})}{\hat{q}_s(t',\vb{k})}}_{\rm g}\theta(t-t^{\prime})

(14.7a)

n_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})=\frac{1}{2}\expectationvalue{\acomm{\hat{q}_s(t,\vb{k})}{\hat{q}_s(t',\vb{k})}}_{\rm g}

(14.7b)

are the
dissipation
and
noise kernels
.

In Eqs. (
14.7
), the
\hat{q}
’s stand for position operators in the Heisenberg picture, and
\theta(x)
is the Heaviside step function. The expectation values with the subscript ’g’ are computed with respect to the initial state of the gravitons.

Both terms in the influence action (
14
) have the generic form

\displaystyle I(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\sum_{s}\int\differential^{3}k\,\quantity[X^s(t,\vb{k})-{X'}^s(t,\vb{k})]j_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\quantity[X^s(t',\vb{k})\pm{X'}^s(t',\vb{k})],

(14.8)

with
j_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})
denoting some kernel (either dissipation or noise) and
X^{s}(t,\mathbf{k})
defined in Eq. (
13.24
). After some algebra, integrating by parts on the time variables and dropping second order terms on the Newtonian potential, this can be rewritten as

I(t,t^{\prime})=\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\quantity[x_{ij}(t)-x'_{ij}(t)]J^{ijkl}(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[x_{kl}(t')\pm x'_{kl}(t')],

(14.9)

where

x_{ij}(t)=L_{\rm rest}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\bar{t})\xi_{i}(t)\xi_{j}(t)

(14.10)

and

\displaystyle J_{ijkl}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{s}\int\differential^{3}k\,\left[\epsilon_{ij}(\mathbf{k})\epsilon_{kl}(\mathbf{k})\derivative[2]{t}\derivative[2]{{t'}}j_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\epsilon_{ij}(\mathbf{k})\epsilon_{nl}(\mathbf{k}){\Phi^{n}}_{k}\derivative[2]{t}j_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})+\epsilon_{nj}(\mathbf{k})\epsilon_{kl}(\mathbf{k}){\Phi^{n}}_{i}\derivative[2]{{t'}}j_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})\right].

(14.11)

Then, after putting everything together, we find the influence action to be given by

\begin{split}S_{\rm IF}[x,x^{\prime}]=\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\left\{\frac{1}{2}\quantity[x_{ij}(t)-x'_{ij}(t)]D^{ijkl}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[x_{kl}(t')+x'_{kl}(t')]\right.\\
\left.+\frac{i}{2}\quantity[x_{ij}(t)-x'_{ij}(t)]N^{ijkl}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[x_{kl}(t')-x'_{kl}(t')]\right\},\end{split}

(14.12)

where we have defined the
gravitational dissipation
and
noise kernels
,

\begin{split}D_{\rm g}^{ijkl}(t,t^{\prime})&=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{s}\int\differential^{3}k\,\left[\epsilon^{ij}(\mathbf{k})\epsilon^{kl}(\mathbf{k})\derivative[2]{t}\derivative[2]{{t'}}d_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})\right.\\
&\hskip 8.5359pt\left.+\epsilon^{ij}(\mathbf{k})\epsilon^{nl}(\mathbf{k}){\Phi_{n}}^{k}\derivative[2]{t}d_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})+\epsilon^{nj}(\mathbf{k})\epsilon^{kl}(\mathbf{k}){\Phi_{n}}^{i}\derivative[2]{{t'}}d_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})\right],\end{split}

(14.13a)

\begin{split}N_{\rm g}^{ijkl}(t,t^{\prime})&=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{s}\int\differential^{3}k\,\left[\epsilon^{ij}(\mathbf{k})\epsilon^{kl}(\mathbf{k})\derivative[2]{t}\derivative[2]{{t'}}n_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})\right.\\
&\hskip 8.5359pt\left.+\epsilon^{ij}(\mathbf{k})\epsilon^{nl}(\mathbf{k}){\Phi_{n}}^{k}\derivative[2]{t}n_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})+\epsilon^{nj}(\mathbf{k})\epsilon^{kl}(\mathbf{k}){\Phi_{n}}^{i}\derivative[2]{{t'}}n_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})\right],\end{split}

(14.13b)

with
d_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})
and
n_{s}(t,t^{\prime},\mathbf{k})
defined in Eqs. (
14.7
).

Now, in the same way we proceeded in Section
8
, it will be useful to express the noise kernel contribution in terms of a stochastic variable
\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t)
using the Gaussian functional identity
[
38
]

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\quantity[x_{ij}(t)-x'_{ij}(t)]N^{ijkl}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[x_{kl}(t')-x'_{kl}(t')]}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\mathcal{C}\int\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}\,e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t)(N_{g}^{-1})^{ijkl}(t,t^{\prime})\mathcal{N}_{kl}(t^{\prime})}e^{i\int\differential t\,\mathcal{N}^{ij}(t)\quantity[x_{ij}(t)-x'_{ij}(t)]},

(14.14)

where
\mathcal{C}
is a normalization constant, and
\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}
denotes the path integral measure for the stochastic variable
\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t)
. Stochastic averages are then computed using a Gaussian probability density
\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}]
, for which we have

\expectationvalue{\mathcal{N}^{ij}(t)}_{\rm sto}=\int\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}\,\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}]\mathcal{N}^{ij}(t)=0,

(14.15a)

\expectationvalue{\mathcal{N}^{ij}(t)\mathcal{N}^{kl}(t')}_{\rm sto}=\int\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}\,\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}]\mathcal{N}^{ij}(t)\mathcal{N}^{kl}(t^{\prime})=N_{\rm g}^{ijkl}(t,t^{\prime}).

(14.15b)

The gravitational influence functional then becomes

\displaystyle e^{iS_{\rm IF}[x,x^{\prime}]}

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}\,\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}]\,e^{i\int\differential t\,\mathcal{N}^{ij}(t)\quantity[x_{ij}(t)-x'_{ij}(t)]}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\times e^{\frac{i}{2}\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\quantity[x_{ij}(t)-x'_{ij}(t)]D^{ijkl}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[x_{kl}(t')+x'_{kl}(t')]}.

(14.16)

Let us now note that the term involving the dissipation kernel in Eq. (
14
) is of order
O(\xi^{4})
since
x_{ij}
is already of order
O(\xi^{2})
. Thus, the leading order contribution comes from the term involving the noise kernel, and we may approximate Eq. (
14
) as

e^{iS_{\rm IF}[x,x^{\prime}]}\simeq\int\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}\,\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}]\,e^{i\int\differential t\,\mathcal{N}^{ij}(t)\quantity[x_{ij}(t)-x'_{ij}(t)]}.

(14.17)

This means that we are only considering the influence of the gravitational field encoded in the noise kernel, which is responsible for describing decoherence, as we saw in Chapter
2
, and whose explicit form depends on the initial state of the gravitons. In Appendix
9
, we obtain explicit expressions for the gravitational noise kernel by considering four different possible initial states: vacuum, thermal, coherent, and squeezed states.

15
The external DoFs density matrix

The total density matrix of the particle (including external and internal degrees of freedom) at time
t
is given by

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm sys}(\xi,\varrho,\xi^{\prime},\varrho,t)=\int\differential\xi(0)\differential\xi^{\prime}(0)\differential\varrho(0)\differential\varrho^{\prime}(0)\,\rho_{\rm sys}(\xi(0),\varrho(0),\xi^{\prime}(0),\varrho^{\prime}(0),0)

\displaystyle\times\int\mathcal{D}\xi\mathcal{D}\xi^{\prime}\mathcal{D}\varrho\mathcal{D}\varrho^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\rm sys}[\xi,\varrho]-S_{\rm sys}[\xi',\varrho'])}e^{iS_{\rm IF}[\xi,\varrho,\xi^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime}]},

(15.1)

with
e^{iS_{\rm IF}}
given in Eq. (
14
) in terms of the variable
x_{ij}(t)
, defined in Eq. (
14.10
), and

\displaystyle S_{\rm sys}[\xi,\varrho]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}m\int\differential t\,\quantity(\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j+\Phi_{ij}\xi^i\xi^j)+\int\differential t\,\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\bar{t})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\,\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\bar{t})\quantity(\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j+\Phi_{ij}\xi^i\xi^j).

(15.2)

The path integral over
\xi(t)
is taken from
\xi(0)
to
\xi
, and similarly for the others.

Now, within the approximation (
14.17
), we find

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm sys}(\xi,\varrho,\xi^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime},t)=\int\differential\xi(0)\differential\xi^{\prime}(0)\differential\varrho(0)\differential\varrho^{\prime}(0)\,\rho_{\rm sys}(\xi(0),\varrho(0),\xi^{\prime}(0),\varrho^{\prime}(0),0)

\displaystyle\times\int\mathcal{D}\xi\mathcal{D}\xi^{\prime}\mathcal{D}\varrho\mathcal{D}\varrho^{\prime}\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}\,\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}]\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\rm eff}[\xi,\varrho,\mathcal{N}]-S_{\rm eff}[\xi',\varrho',\mathcal{N}])},

(15.3)

where

\displaystyle S_{\rm eff}[\xi,\varrho,\mathcal{N}]

\displaystyle=\int\differential t\,\quantity[\frac{1}{2}m\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j+m\qty(\frac{1}{2}\Phi_{ij}-\mathcal{N}_{ij})\xi^i\xi^j]+\int\differential t\,\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\bar{t})

\displaystyle-\int\differential t\,\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\bar{t})\quantity[\frac{1}{2}\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j+\qty(\frac{1}{2}\Phi_{ij}-\mathcal{N}_{ij})\xi^i\xi^j],

(15.4)

with
\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t)
being the Gaussian stochastic variable satisfying Eqs. (
14.15
). Let us now note that the tidal tensor can be absorbed into the stochastic variable by making
\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t)\to\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t)-\frac{1}{2}\Phi_{ij}
in Eq. (
15
). Since this is a linear transformation, the new variable is still Gaussian with stochastic averages

\expectationvalue{\mathcal{N}^{ij}(t)}_{\rm sto}=-\frac{1}{2}\Phi_{ij},

(15.5a)

\expectationvalue{\mathcal{N}^{ij}(t)\mathcal{N}^{kl}(t')}_{\rm sto}=N_{\rm g}^{ijkl}(t,t^{\prime})+O(\phi^{2}).

(15.5b)

The effective system action (
15
) then becomes

\displaystyle S_{\rm eff}[\xi,\varrho,\mathcal{N}]

\displaystyle=\int\differential t\,\quantity(\frac{1}{2}m\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j-m\mathcal{N}_{ij}\xi^i\xi^j)+\int\differential t\,\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\bar{t})

\displaystyle-\int\differential t\,\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\bar{t})\quantity(\frac{1}{2}\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j-\mathcal{N}_{ij}\xi^i\xi^j).

(15.6)

We now proceed by considering only the external degrees of freedom of our system, as we are interested in the effects of the noise coming from both the internal DoFs and the quantum fluctuations of the gravitational field. In order to do this, we have to compute the reduced density matrix of the relevant degrees of freedom while tracing out all the others. Let us assume that initially the external and internal DoFs of our system are also uncorrelated, thus implying that

\rho_{\rm sys}(\xi(0),\varrho(0),\xi^{\prime}(0),\varrho^{\prime}(0),0)=\rho_{\rm ext}(\xi(0),\xi^{\prime}(0),0)\rho_{\rm int}(\varrho(0),\varrho^{\prime}(0),0),

(15.7)

where
\hat{\rho}_{\rm ext}
(
\hat{\rho}_{\rm int}
) stands for the external (internal) DoFs density matrix. The time evolution couples the external and internal variables, and we are left with the total density matrix (
15
). The reduced external DoFs density matrix is obtained by taking the partial trace

\rho_{\rm ext}(\xi,\xi^{\prime},t)=\int\differential\varrho\,\rho_{\rm sys}(\xi,\varrho,\xi^{\prime},\varrho,t),

(15.8)

resulting in

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm ext}(\xi,\xi^{\prime},t)

\displaystyle=\int\differential\xi(0)\differential\xi^{\prime}(0)\,\rho_{\rm ext}(\xi(0),\xi^{\prime}(0),0)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\times\int\mathcal{D}\xi\mathcal{D}\xi^{\prime}\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}\,\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}]\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\rm eff}^{(1)}[\xi,\mathcal{N}]-S_{\rm eff}^{(1)}[\xi',\mathcal{N}])}e^{S_{\rm IF}^{(\textrm{int})}[\xi,\xi^{\prime},\mathcal{N}]},

(15.9)

where we have defined the new influence functional

\displaystyle e^{iS_{\rm IF}^{(\textrm{int})}[\xi,\xi^{\prime},\mathcal{N}]}

\displaystyle=\int\differential\varrho\differential\varrho(0)\differential\varrho^{\prime}(0)\,\rho_{\rm int}(\varrho(0),\varrho^{\prime}(0),0)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int\mathcal{D}\varrho\mathcal{D}\varrho^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\rm eff}^{(2)}[\xi,\varrho,\mathcal{N}]-S_{\rm eff}^{(2)}[\xi',\varrho',\mathcal{N}])},

(15.10)

with

S_{\rm eff}^{(1)}[\xi,\mathcal{N}]=\int\differential t\,\quantity(\frac{1}{2}m\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j-m\mathcal{N}_{ij}\xi^i\xi^j),

(15.11a)

and

S_{\rm eff}^{(2)}[\xi,\varrho,\mathcal{N}]=\int\differential t\,\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\bar{t})-\int\differential t\,\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\bar{t})\quantity(\frac{1}{2}\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j-\mathcal{N}_{ij}\xi^i\xi^j).

(15.11b)

Thus, we essentially have a similar problem to the one treated in Section
14
, namely a system interacting with a quantum environment. Therefore, we shall compute the Feynman-Vernon influence functional once again. It is worth remarking that the total influence functional (gravitons plus internal DoFs) is not simply the product of the individual functionals, since the gravitational field couples with all variables describing the system. When considering the system of interest to be the external degrees of freedom, we effectively end up with two environments that interact with the system and with each other. In such a case, the additive property of the influence action for multiple environments does not hold.

In order to proceed, let us assume that the Lagrangian describing the internal degrees of freedom is of the form

\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\,\bar{t})=\sum_{\alpha}\quantity[\frac{1}{2}\mu_\alpha\qty(\dot{\varrho}_\alpha\,\bar{t})^2-\mathcal{V}(\varrho_\alpha)],

(15.12)

with
\mu_{\alpha}
representing the reduced masses of the system and
\mathcal{V}
being a function of the coordinates. Since we want to keep terms only up to second order in the position and velocity coordinates, we may write
