\mathscr{L}(\varrho,\dot{\varrho}\,\bar{t})\simeq\sum_{\alpha}\quantity(\frac{1}{2}\mu_\alpha\dot{\varrho}_\alpha^2-\vartheta_\alpha\varrho_\alpha-\frac{1}{2}\mu_\alpha\varpi_\alpha^2\varrho_\alpha^2),

(15.13)

where
\vartheta_{\alpha}
and
\varpi_{\alpha}
are constants. Then, Eq. (
15.11b
) becomes

S_{\rm eff}^{(2)}=\sum_{\alpha}\left[\int\differential t\quantity(\frac{1}{2}\mu_\alpha\dot{\varrho}_\alpha^2-\frac{1}{2}\mu_\alpha\varpi_\alpha^2\varrho_\alpha^2)+\vartheta_{\alpha}\int\differential t\,Y(t)\varrho_{\alpha}(t)\right],

(15.14)

with

Y(t)=\frac{1}{2}\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^{i}\dot{\xi}^{j}-\mathcal{N}_{ij}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}-1.

(15.15)

Note that we are essentially describing the internal degrees of freedom as a set of independent harmonic oscillators that couple linearly with the external ones. In this case, the internal degrees of freedom influence functional (
15
) is Gaussian, implying that we can write the influence action as

\displaystyle S^{(\textrm{int})}_{\rm IF}[Y,Y^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\left\{\frac{1}{2}\quantity[Y(t)-Y'(t)]D_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[Y(t')+Y'(t')]\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\left.+\frac{i}{2}\quantity[Y(t)-Y'(t)]N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[Y(t')-Y'(t')]\right\},

(15.16)

with

D_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})=i\sum_{\alpha}\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}\expectationvalue{\comm{\hat{\varrho}_\alpha(t)}{\hat{\varrho}_\alpha(t')}}_{\rm int}\theta(t-t^{\prime})

(15.17a)

and

N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha}\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}\expectationvalue{\acomm{\hat{\varrho}_\alpha(t)}{\hat{\varrho}_\alpha(t')}}_{\rm int}

(15.17b)

being the internal DoFs dissipation and noise kernels. Now the
\hat{\varrho}(t)
’s are operators in the Heisenberg picture, and expectation values with the subscript ’int’ are computed with respect to the initial state of the internal DoFs.

Similarly to what we did for the term containing the noise kernel for the gravitational influence functional, we can express the noise term in
S_{\rm IF}^{(\textrm{int})}
in terms of a stochastic variable using the same Gaussian functional identity. Then, this term in the internal DoFs influence functional will lead to a Gaussian probability density and a linear term in the
Y
variable. Therefore, just like in the gravitational case, the leading order contributions come from the noise term, and we may take

e^{iS_{\rm IF}^{(\textrm{int})}[Y,Y^{\prime}]}\simeq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int dtdt^{\prime}\,\quantity[Y(t)-Y'(t)]N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[Y(t')-Y'(t')]},

(15.18)

resulting in

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm ext}(\xi,\xi^{\prime},t)=\int\differential\xi(0)\differential\xi^{\prime}(0)\,\rho_{\rm ext}(\xi(0),\xi^{\prime}(0),0)\int\mathcal{D}\xi\mathcal{D}\xi^{\prime}\,e^{\frac{i}{2}m\delta^{ij}\int\differential t\,\quantity(\dot{\xi}_i\dot{\xi}_j-\dot{\xi}_i'\dot{\xi}_j')}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\times\int\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}\,\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}]\,e^{-im\int\differential t\,\mathcal{N}^{ij}\quantity(\xi_i\xi_j-\xi_i'\xi_j')}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\quantity[Y(t)-Y'(t)]N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[Y(t')-Y'(t')]}.

(15.19)

The stochastic averages shown in Eqs. (
15.5
) can now be employed, provided we work in a perturbative regime (dropping higher order terms on
\xi
and
\phi
). From this, we obtain the external degrees of freedom density matrix as

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm ext}(\xi,\xi^{\prime},t)=\int\differential\xi(0)\differential\xi^{\prime}(0)\,\rho_{\rm ext}(\xi(0),\xi^{\prime}(0),0)\int\mathcal{D}\xi\mathcal{D}\xi^{\prime}\,e^{i\quantity(S_{\rm ext}[\xi]-S_{\rm ext}[\xi'])}

\displaystyle\times\exp\quantity{-\frac{1}{4}\int\dd t\dd t'\,\delta^{ij}\qty[\frac{1}{2}\delta^{kl}y_{ij}(t)N_{\rm int}(t,t')y_{kl}(t')+\Phi^{kl}y_{ij}(t)N_{\rm int}(t,t')w_{kl}(t')]}

\displaystyle\times\exp\quantity{-\int\dd t\dd t'\,w_{ij}(t)\qty[\frac{m^2}{2}+N_{\rm int}(t,t')]N_{\rm g}^{ijkl}(t,t')w_{kl}(t')},

(15.20)

where

S_{\rm ext}[\xi]=\frac{m}{2}\int\differential t\,\quantity(\delta^{ij}\dot{\xi}_i\dot{\xi}_j+\Phi^{ij}\xi_i\xi_j),

(15.21)

and we have defined

w_{ij}(t)=\xi_{i}(t)\xi_{j}(t)-\xi_{i}^{\prime}(t)\xi_{j}^{\prime}(t)

(15.22a)

and

y_{ij}(t)=\dot{\xi}_{i}(t)\dot{\xi}_{j}(t)-\dot{\xi}_{i}^{\prime}(t)\dot{\xi}_{j}^{\prime}(t).

(15.22b)

The reduced density matrix (
15
) is the main result of this chapter. Note that its time evolution is not unitary due to the interaction with the environments. The non-unitarity sector of the time evolution is determined by the gravitational noise kernel
N_{\rm g}^{ijkl}(t,t^{\prime})
and the internal DoFs noise kernel
N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})
, which we also compute in Appendix
9
by considering an internal thermal bath. In the next chapters, we explore the implications of this non-unitary time evolution.

Chapter 5
Graviton-induced decoherence of a composite particle

Now that we have described the interaction of a composite particle with a bath of gravitons in a Newtonian background in Chapter
4
, let us next obtain the time evolution of quantum superpositions of the center-of-mass variable. Our starting point will be the reduced density matrix (
15
) that was obtained by integrating over the gravitational and internal system degrees of freedom. Here, we will consider the system to be moving in a superposition of two classically distinguishable paths and study the decoherence arising from the interactions with both environments. In Section
16
, we define the decoherence function and compute it for two possible configurations of the superposition state. We analyze the behavior of this function and compute the decoherence time in Section
17
by considering the gravitons to be initially in the vacuum state. We extend the analysis to other possible states in Section
18
. Lastly, in Section
19
, we discuss the possibility of gravitational recoherence for long times.

16
The decoherence function

Consider a special case in which the composite particle can move only along
two classically distinguishable paths
\xi^{(1)}(t)
and
\xi^{(2)}(t)
. Then Eq. (
15
) can be written as

\begin{split}&\rho_{\rm ext}(\xi,\xi^{\prime},t)=\int\differential\xi_{0}\differential\xi_{0}^{\prime}\rho_{\rm ext}(\xi_{0},\xi_{0}^{\prime},0)\\
&\hskip 8.5359pt\times\evaluated{\sum_{m,n=1}^2e^{i\qty{S_{\rm ext}\qty[\xi^{(m)}]-S_{\rm ext}\qty[\xi^{(n)}]}}e^{-\Gamma\qty[\xi^{(m)},\xi^{(n)},t]}}_{\xi^{(m)}(0)=\xi_0,\,\,\xi^{(n)}(0)=\xi_0'}^{\xi^{(m)}(t)=\xi,\,\,\xi^{(n)}(t)=\xi'},\end{split}

(16.1)

where we have introduced the functional

\displaystyle\Gamma\quantity[\xi^{(m)},\xi^{(n)},t]=\int\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\left\{\frac{1}{8}\delta^{ij}\delta^{kl}\quantity[\dot{\xi}_i^{(m)}(t)\dot{\xi}_j^{(m)}(t)-\dot{\xi}_i^{(n)}(t)\dot{\xi}_j^{(n)}(t)]N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\quantity[\dot{\xi}_k^{(m)}(t')\dot{\xi}_l^{(m)}(t')-\dot{\xi}_k^{(n)}(t')\dot{\xi}_l^{(n)}(t')]

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt+\frac{1}{4}\delta^{ij}\Phi^{kl}\quantity[\dot{\xi}_i^{(m)}(t)\dot{\xi}_j^{(m)}(t)-\dot{\xi}_i^{(n)}(t)\dot{\xi}_j^{(n)}(t)]N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\quantity[\xi_k^{(m)}(t')\xi_l^{(m)}(t')-\xi_k^{(n)}(t')\xi_l^{(n)}(t')]

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt+\quantity[\xi_i^{(m)}(t)\xi_j^{(m)}(t)-\xi_i^{(n)}(t)\xi_j^{(n)}(t)]\quantity[\frac{m^2}{2}+N_{\rm int}(t,t')]

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\left.\times N_{\rm g}^{ijkl}(t,t^{\prime})\quantity[\xi_k^{(m)}(t')\xi_l^{(m)}(t')-\xi_k^{(n)}(t')\xi_l^{(n)}(t')]\right\}.

(16.2)

This is a functional of paths
\xi^{(1)}(t)
and
\xi^{(2)}(t)
, which satisfies

\Gamma\quantity[\xi^{(m)},\xi^{(n)},t]=\Gamma\quantity[\xi^{(n)},\xi^{(m)},t],

(16.3a)

and

\Gamma\quantity[\xi^{(m)},\xi^{(m)},t]=0.

(16.3b)

Because of these properties, we only need to consider
\Gamma\quantity[\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)},t]\equiv\Gamma(t)
. When
\Gamma(t)\geq 0
, this function describes the decay of the off-diagonal density matrix elements, which is why we call it the
decoherence function
.

For simplicity, let us take
\xi_{i}^{(m)}(t)=\xi^{(m)}(t)\delta_{i3}
, namely unidimensional paths in the
x^{3}-
direction. Now, consider a scheme in which both paths start at the same point
\xi_{0}=\xi_{0}^{\prime}
and both end at another point
\xi=\xi^{\prime}
. This is the typical scenario in which an initially localized quantum system undergoes a superposition of paths and is then recombined after time
t_{f}
in order for its interference patterns to be analyzed.

Let us define the variables

\Xi(t)\equiv\frac{1}{2}\quantity[\xi^{(1)}(t)+\xi^{(2)}(t)],\hskip 14.22636pt\Delta\xi(t)\equiv\xi^{(1)}(t)-\xi^{(2)}(t),

(16.4a)

and

V(t)\equiv\derivative{t}\Xi(t),\hskip 14.22636pt\Delta v(t)\equiv\derivative{t}\Delta\xi(t),

(16.4b)

such that we can write

\displaystyle\Gamma(t_{f})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t_{f}}\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,V(t)\Delta v(t)N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})V(t^{\prime})\Delta v(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\Phi_{zz}\int_{0}^{t_{f}}\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,V(t)\Delta v(t)N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})\Xi(t^{\prime})\Delta\xi(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle+4\int_{0}^{t_{f}}\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\Xi(t)\Delta\xi(t)\quantity[\frac{m^2}{2}+N_{\rm int}(t,t')]N_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})\Xi(t^{\prime})\Delta\xi(t^{\prime}),

(16.5)

with
N_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})\equiv N_{\rm g}^{3333}(t,t^{\prime})
.

In Appendix
9
, the noise kernel
N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})
was computed by considering the internal DoFs to represent an Ohmic bath described by the coupling constant
\eta
in thermal equilibrium at high temperature
T_{\rm int}\gg\absolutevalue{t-t'}
. Explicitly, using Eq. (
9.B.5
), we have

\displaystyle\Gamma(t_{f})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\eta\pi T_{\rm int}\int_{0}^{t_{f}}\differential t\,\quantity[V(t)\Delta v(t)]^{2}+\eta\pi T_{\rm int}\Phi_{zz}\int_{0}^{t_{f}}\differential t\,V(t)\Delta v(t)\Xi(t)\Delta\xi(t)

\displaystyle+2m^{2}\int_{0}^{t_{f}}\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\Xi(t)\Delta\xi(t)N_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})\Xi(t^{\prime})\Delta\xi(t^{\prime})

\displaystyle+4\eta\pi T_{\rm int}\int_{0}^{t_{f}}\differential t\,\quantity[\Xi(t)\Delta\xi(t)]^{2}N_{\rm g}(t),

(16.6)

where
N_{\rm g}(t)\equiv\lim_{t^{\prime}\to t}N_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})
.

The function
\Gamma(t)
depends on the specific configurations of the superposition state through the variables (
16.4
), and also on the gravitational noise kernel
N_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})
, which was computed in Appendix
9
. In this work, we will consider the same configuration path as the one in Refs.
[
90
,
23
]
in order to explore the time evolution of the superposition state. However, we will also show how the decoherence function is modified for another configuration for illustrative purposes.

16.1
Configuration 1

For the first configuration, which we refer to as
Configuration 1
, we choose

\Xi(t)=\Xi=\textrm{constant in time}

(16.7a)

and

\Delta\xi(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}2vt&\textrm{for}\hskip 5.69046pt0<t\leq t_{f}/2\\
2v(t_{f}-t)&\textrm{for}\hskip 5.69046ptt_{f}/2<t<t_{f}\end{array}\right.,

(16.7b)

for some constant velocity
v
. Note that this implies
V(t)=\derivative*{\Xi}{t}=0
.

We then have

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}(t_{f})

\displaystyle=8m^{2}\Xi^{2}v^{2}\left[\int_{0}^{t_{f}/2}\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,tt^{\prime}N_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})+\int_{t_{f}/2}^{t_{f}}\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,(t_{f}-t)(t_{f}-t^{\prime})N_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left.+2\int_{0}^{t_{f}/2}\differential t\int_{t_{f}/2}^{t_{f}}\differential t^{\prime}\,t(t_{f}-t^{\prime})N_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle+16\eta\pi T_{\rm int}\Xi^{2}v^{2}\quantity[\int_0^{t_f/2}\dd t\,t^2N_{\rm g}(t)+\int_{t_f/2}^{t_f}\dd t\,(t_f-t)^2N_{\rm g}(t)].

(16.8)

The next step is to use the various expressions for the gravitational noise kernel to explicitly compute the function
\Gamma_{1}(t)
. These can be found in Appendix
9
for gravitons initially in the vacuum, thermal, coherent, and squeezed states. Although long, this is a straightforward computation, so let us simply present the final results for the function
\Gamma_{1}(t)
.

First, let us recall from Eq. (
13.1
) that
\Phi_{zz}=2M_{N}/R_{N}^{3}
. Also, it will be interesting to return the universal constants
\hbar
,
c
,
G
, and
k_{B}
so that we present the result in SI units rather than Planck units.

Let us introduce the index
A
, which can be either v, t, c, or s, representing vacuum, thermal, coherent, and squeezed states, respectively. Then, the results for the function
\Gamma_{1}(t)
can be summarized in the following expression:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}^{(A)}(t)

\displaystyle=b_{A}(1-\delta_{A,\textrm{v}})\Gamma_{1}^{\rm(v)}(t)

\displaystyle+\frac{8\Xi^{2}v^{2}m^{2}}{5\pi E_{\rm P}^{2}}K_{1,A}\left\{\quantity(\frac{\Lambda_A}{\hbar})^{2}\quantity[f_A^{(I)}\qty(\frac{\Lambda_At}{\hbar})+\frac{\kappa_A}{E_{\rm rest}^2}f_A^{(II)}\qty(\frac{\Lambda_At}{\hbar})]\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\left.-\frac{GM_{N}}{R_{N}^{3}}\quantity[f_A^{(III)}\qty(\frac{\Lambda_At}{\hbar})+\frac{\kappa_A}{E_{\rm rest}^2}f_A^{(IV)}\qty(\frac{\Lambda_At}{\hbar})]\right\}.

(16.9)

Here,

b_{A}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\textrm{for $A\neq\textrm{s}$}\\
\cosh 2r&\textrm{for $A=\textrm{s}$}\end{array}\right.,

(16.10a)

\Lambda_{A}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Lambda&\textrm{for $A\neq\textrm{t}$}\\
\pi k_{B}T_{\rm g}&\textrm{for $A=\textrm{t}$}\end{array}\right.,

(16.10b)

and

\kappa_{A}=\eta\pi k_{B}T_{\rm int}\Lambda_{A}.

(16.10c)

Also,
E_{\rm rest}=mc^{2}
and
E_{\rm P}
are the rest and Planck energies. The constant
\Lambda
is the graviton energy cutoff,
T_{\rm g}
is the graviton temperature for the initial thermal state, and
r
is the real squeeze parameter for the initial squeezed state. The constants
K_{1,A}
are

\displaystyle K_{1,\textrm{v}}=2,

(16.11a)

\displaystyle K_{1,\textrm{t}}=\frac{4}{3},

(16.11b)

\displaystyle K_{1,\textrm{c}}=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{3},

(16.11c)

\displaystyle K_{1,\textrm{s}}=-\frac{2}{3}\sinh 2r,

(16.11d)

with
\alpha
being the displacement parameter for the initial coherent state. For all states, the functions
f_{A}^{(I)}
describe the contributions to decoherence that come from the gravitons alone, while the functions
f_{A}^{(II)}
describe the contributions coming from the interplay between the gravitons and the internal DoFs of the system. The functions
f_{A}^{(III)}
and
f_{A}^{(IV)}
, representing the contributions from gravitons and gravitons plus internal DoFs, respectively, also encode the contribution from the Newtonian potential. Note that, within this configuration, there are no contributions coming solely from the internal DoFs, and in the absence of quantum spacetime fluctuations, decoherence does not occur (for Configuration 1). The various functions
f_{A}
are listed below:

Vacuum state

f_{\rm v}^{(I)}(x)=1+\frac{2}{3x}\quantity[\sin x-8\sin\qty(\frac{x}{2})]+\frac{1}{x^{2}}\quantity[\frac{2}{3}\cos x-\frac{32}{3}\cos\qty(\frac{x}{2})+10],

(16.12a)

f_{\rm v}^{(II)}(x)=\frac{1}{108}x^{3},

(16.12b)

f_{\rm v}^{(III)}(x)=8\gamma_{E}-\frac{4}{3}\ln 4-\frac{32}{3}\textrm{Ci}\quantity(\frac{x}{2})+\frac{8}{3}\textrm{Ci}(x)+8\ln\quantity(\frac{x}{2}),

(16.12c)

f_{\rm v}^{(IV)}(x)=\frac{1}{18}x^{3}.

(16.12d)

Thermal state

f_{\rm t}^{(I)}(x)=\frac{1+16e^{x}+26e^{2x}+16e^{3x}+e^{4x}}{(e^{2x}-1)^{2}}-\frac{15}{x^{2}},

(16.13a)

f_{\rm t}^{(II)}(x)=\frac{4}{189}x^{3},

(16.13b)

f_{\rm t}^{(III)}(x)=4\ln\quantity[\frac{2(e^x-1)^3}{x^3(e^x+1)}]-4x,

(16.13c)

f_{\rm t}^{(IV)}(x)=\frac{2}{45}x^{3}.

(16.13d)

Coherent state

\displaystyle f_{\rm c}^{(I)}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{7}{2}+\frac{1}{6x}\quantity{3\sin(2x)-16\qty[9\sin\qty(\frac{x}{2})-3\sin x+\sin\qty(\frac{3x}{2})]}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{36x^{2}}\quantity[1495-1728\cos\qty(\frac{x}{2})+288\cos x-64\cos\qty(\frac{3x}{2})+9\cos(2x)],

(16.14a)

\displaystyle f_{\rm c}^{(II)}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{3}}{36}+\frac{1}{8x^{3}}\left[49+24(x^{2}-2)\cos x+(2x^{2}-1)\cos(2x)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-4x(12-2x^{2}+\cos x)\sin x\right],

(16.14b)

f_{\rm c}^{(III)}(x)=28\gamma_{E}+4\ln\quantity(\frac{81x^7}{2^{17}})-48\textrm{Ci}\quantity(\frac{x}{2})+32\textrm{Ci}(x)-16\textrm{Ci}\quantity(\frac{3x}{2})+4\textrm{Ci}(2x),

(16.14c)

f_{\rm c}^{(IV)}(x)=\frac{x^{3}}{6}+4\sin x+\frac{2}{x}(2\cos x+\cos^{2}x-3).

(16.14d)

Squeezed state

\displaystyle f_{\rm s}^{(I)}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2x}\quantity[\sin(2x)+12\sin x-16\sin\qty(\frac{x}{2})-\frac{16}{3}\sin\qty(\frac{3x}{2})]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{36x^{2}}\quantity[415-576\cos\qty(\frac{x}{2})+216\cos x-64\cos\qty(\frac{3x}{2})+9\cos(2x)],

(16.15a)

\displaystyle f_{\rm s}^{(II)}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8x^{3}}\left[49+24(x^{2}-2)\cos x+(2x^{2}-1)\cos(2x)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-4x(12-2x^{2}+\cos x)\sin x\right],

(16.15b)

f_{\rm s}^{(III)}(x)=4\gamma_{E}+4\ln\quantity(\frac{81x}{2^{9}})-16\textrm{Ci}\quantity(\frac{x}{2})+24\textrm{Ci}(x)-16\textrm{Ci}\quantity(\frac{3x}{2})+4\textrm{Ci}(2x),

(16.15c)

f_{\rm s}^{(IV)}(x)=4\sin x+\frac{2}{x}(2\cos x+\cos^{2}x-3).

(16.15d)

Here
\gamma_{E}\simeq 0.577
is the Euler-Mascheroni constant, and
\textrm{Ci}(z)=-\int_{z}^{\infty}\frac{\cos t}{t}\differential t
is the cosine integral function.

16.2
Configuration 2

We can consider a different configuration for the superposition state; for instance, one that is described by linear paths with different constant velocities,
\xi^{(m)}=v_{m}t
. In that case, which we call
Configuration 2
, we have

\Xi(t)=Vt,\hskip 14.22636pt\Delta\xi(t)=\Delta vt,

(16.16a)

and

V=\frac{v_{1}+v_{2}}{2},\hskip 14.22636pt\Delta v=v_{1}-v_{2}.

(16.16b)

For Configuration 2, one finds

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}(t_{f})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\eta\pi T_{\rm int}\quantity(v_1^2-v_2^2)^{2}\quantity[\frac{t_f}{2}+\frac{\Phi_{zz}t_f^3}{3}+4\int_0^{t_f}\dd t\,t^4N_{\rm g}(t)]

\displaystyle+\frac{m^{2}}{2}\quantity(v_1^2-v_2^2)^{2}\int_{0}^{t_{f}}\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,(tt^{\prime})^{2}N_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime}).

(16.17)

Just as we did for Configuration 1, our next task is to plug in the expressions for the noise kernel into Eq. (
16.2
) in order to obtain explicit expressions for
\Gamma_{2}(t)
. Once again, we shall only summarize the results of this long but straightforward computation, which gives (after restoring the universal constants once more)

\Gamma_{2}^{(A)}(t)=\frac{\pi\eta k_{B}T_{\rm int}}{4\hbar}\frac{\quantity(v_1^2-v_2^2)^{2}}{c^{4}}\quantity(\frac{t}{2}+\frac{2}{3}\frac{GM_N}{R_N^3}t^3)+\Gamma_{2(g)}^{(A)}(t),

(16.18a)

with

\begin{split}\Gamma_{2(g)}^{(A)}(t)&=b_{A}(1-\delta_{A,\textrm{v}})\Gamma_{2(g)}^{\rm(v)}(t)\\
&+\frac{m^{2}\quantity(v_1^2-v_2^2)^{2}}{15\pi E_{\rm P}^{2}}K_{2,A}\left\{g_{A}^{(I)}\quantity(\frac{\Lambda_At}{\hbar})+\frac{\kappa_{A}}{E_{\rm rest}^{2}}g_{A}^{(II)}\quantity(\frac{\Lambda_At}{\hbar})\right.\\
&\hskip 71.13188pt\left.-\quantity(\frac{\hbar}{\Lambda_A})^{2}\frac{GM_{E}}{R_{E}^{3}}\quantity[g_A^{(III)}\qty(\frac{\Lambda_At}{\hbar})+\frac{\kappa_A}{E_{\rm rest}^2}g_A^{(IV)}\qty(\frac{\Lambda_At}{\hbar})]\right\},\end{split}

(16.18b)

with
b_{A}
,
\Lambda_{A}
, and
\kappa_{A}
defined in Eqs. (
16.10
).
The constants
K_{2,A}
are

\displaystyle K_{2,\textrm{v}}=1,

(16.19a)

\displaystyle K_{2,\textrm{t}}=12,

(16.19b)

\displaystyle K_{2,\textrm{c}}=\alpha^{2},

(16.19c)

\displaystyle K_{2,\textrm{s}}=-\sinh 2r.

(16.19d)

The functions
g_{A}
have the same interpretations as the functions
f_{A}
with respect to the physical mechanism responsible for their contribution. However, as opposed to Configuration 1, the decoherence function for Configuration 2 exhibits contributions that come from the internal DoFs alone, in the same spirit as the gravitational decoherence mechanism proposed by Pikovski et al.
[
133
,
134
]
(Section
12
). Since our interest will be in analyzing the decoherence resulting from quantum spacetime fluctuations, we will work with Configuration 1 for the remainder of this work. Nevertheless, for illustrative purposes (and as a reference for some possible future work), the various functions
g_{A}
are listed below:

Vacuum state

g_{\rm v}^{(I)}(x)=\frac{x^{4}}{4}+8\gamma_{E}-12-8\textrm{Ci}(x)+8\ln x+4x\sin x+12\cos x,

(16.20a)

g_{\rm v}^{(II)}(x)=\frac{x^{5}}{15},

(16.20b)

g_{\rm v}^{(III)}(x)=2x^{4}-8x^{2}+16\cos x+16x\sin x-16,

(16.20c)

g_{\rm v}^{(IV)}(x)=\frac{2}{5}x^{5}.

(16.20d)

Thermal state

g_{\rm t}^{(I)}(x)=1-\frac{2x}{3}+\frac{x^{4}}{90}+\frac{2}{3}\ln\quantity(\frac{e^{2x}-1}{2x})-\frac{x}{3}\quantity[\frac{\sinh(2x)+x}{\sinh^2x}],

(16.21a)

g_{\rm t}^{(II)}(x)=\frac{8}{945}x^{5},

(16.21b)

g_{\rm t}^{(III)}(x)=\frac{x^{4}}{9}+\frac{4}{9}x^{3}+\frac{2}{3}x^{2}-\frac{4}{3}x^{2}\ln(1-e^{2x})-\frac{4}{3}x\textrm{Li}_{2}(e^{2x})+\frac{2}{3}\textrm{Li}_{3}(e^{2x})-\frac{2}{3}\zeta(3),

(16.21c)

g_{\rm t}^{(IV)}(x)=\frac{4}{225}x^{5}.

(16.21d)

Coherent state

\displaystyle g_{\rm c}^{(I)}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{4}}{8}+2\gamma_{E}-\frac{59}{16}+\frac{1}{16}(59-22x^{2})\cos(2x)-2\textrm{Ci}(2x)-2\ln\quantity(\frac{x}{2})+4\ln x

\displaystyle+\frac{x}{8}(27-2x^{2})\sin(2x),

(16.22a)

g_{\rm c}^{(II)}(x)=\frac{x^{5}}{30}+\frac{1}{8x}\quantity[15+\qty(-15+18x^2-2x^4)\cos(2x)]+(x^{2}-3)\sin(2x),

(16.22b)

g_{\rm c}^{(III)}(x)=x^{4}-\frac{7}{2}x^{2}-4+\quantity(4-\frac{9}{2}x^2)\cos(2x)-x(x^{2}-8)\sin(2x),

(16.22c)

g_{\rm c}^{(IV)}(x)=\frac{3}{2}x+\frac{x^{5}}{5}+x\quantity(\frac{9}{2}-x^2)\cos(2x)+3(x^{2}-1)\sin(2x).

(16.22d)

Squeezed state

\displaystyle g_{\rm s}^{(I)}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{37}{8}-4\gamma_{E}-12\cos x+\frac{1}{8}(59-22x^{2})\cos(2x)+8\textrm{Ci}(x)-4\textrm{Ci}(2x)

\displaystyle-4\ln\quantity(\frac{x}{2})-\frac{x}{2}\quantity[8+(2x^2-27)\cos x]\sin x,

(16.23a)

g_{\rm s}^{(II)}(x)=\frac{1}{4x}\quantity[15+\qty(-15+18x^2-2x^4)\cos(2x)]+2(x^{2}-3)\sin(2x),

(16.23b)

g_{\rm s}^{(III)}(x)=x^{2}+8-16\cos x+(8-9x^{2})\cos(2x)-4x\quantity[4+(x^2-8)\cos x]\sin x,

(16.23c)

g_{\rm s}^{(IV)}(x)=3x+x(9-2x^{2})\cos(2x)+6(x^{2}-1)\sin(2x).

(16.23d)

Here
\zeta(z)
is the Riemann zeta function, and
\textrm{Li}_{n}(z)
is the polylogarithm function.

17
Decoherence for initial vacuum state

For gravitons initially in the vacuum state, the decoherence function (for Configuration 1) reads

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{v})}(t)=\frac{16\Xi^{2}v^{2}m^{2}}{5\pi E_{\rm P}^{2}}\left\{\quantity(\frac{\Lambda}{\hbar})^{2}\quantity[f_{\rm v}^{(I)}\qty(\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar})+\frac{\kappa}{E_{\rm rest}^2}f_{\rm v}^{(II)}\qty(\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar})]\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\left.-\frac{GM_{N}}{R_{N}^{3}}\quantity[f_{\rm v}^{(III)}\qty(\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar})+\frac{\kappa}{E_{\rm rest}^2}f_{\rm v}^{(IV)}\qty(\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar})]\right\},

(17.1)

with
\kappa\equiv\kappa_{\rm v}
. The explicit expressions for the functions
f_{\rm v}(x)
were listed in Section
16
, while some of their relevant properties are shown in Table
1
(see also Figure
8
).

Vacuum state

Contribution

Function

Behavior for
x\ll 1

Behavior for
x\gg 1

G

f_{\rm v}^{(I)}(x)

x^{4}/288

1

G+I

f_{\rm v}^{(II)}(x)

x^{3}/108

x^{3}/108

G+N

f_{\rm v}^{(III)}(x)

8\gamma_{E}-\frac{32}{3}\ln 2+8\ln x

G+N+I

Table 1:
Different contributions to the decoherence function considering the gravitons to be initially in the
vacuum state
. In the "Contribution" column, "G" means gravitons, "I" means internal DoFs, and "N" stands for Newtonian potential.

Figure 8:
Different contributions for the decoherence function considering the gravitons to be initially in the
vacuum state
.

From Eq. (
17
), we see that the contribution from the interplay between the gravitons and the internal degrees of freedom of the system is scaled by the ratio

\mathscr{R}=\frac{\kappa}{E_{\rm rest}^{2}}=\frac{\eta\pi k_{B}T_{\rm int}\Lambda}{m^{2}c^{4}}.

(17.2)

This ratio depends on the dimensionless coupling between the external and internal DoFs,
\eta
, as well as the internal temperature, which can be of order
T_{\rm int}\sim 10^{4}
K for complex molecules
[
77
]
. The graviton cutoff
\Lambda
is related to the typical size of the geodesic deviation,
L_{0}
, as pointed out in Chapter
4
. We take
\Lambda=\hbar c/L_{0}
and refer to
L_{0}
as the "detector size". Finally, the ratio also depends on the mass of the composite particle,
m
. As we discussed in Chapter
3
, quantum spatial superposition has been observed for complex molecules with masses up to
m\sim 10^{-22}
kg
[
69
,
62
,
124
]
. We can rewrite the ratio (
17.2
) as

\mathscr{R}=\eta\frac{L(m,T_{\rm int})}{L_{0}},

(17.3)

where
L(m,T_{\rm int})
is a typical length scale determined by the mass of the system and its internal temperature. For instance, if we take
T_{\rm int}\sim 10^{4}
K and
m\sim 10^{-22}
kg, we find
L(m,T_{\rm int})\sim L_{\rm P}
, the Planck length. Therefore, although the ratio
\mathscr{R}
can be increased by increasing the coupling between external and internal DoFs, and/or decreasing the detector size, it is clear that, for typical systems, one can expect to have
\mathscr{R}\ll 1
. Nevertheless, the relevance of such contributions needs to be analyzed by looking at the behaviors of the functions listed in Table
1
.

17.1
Short-time limit

Let us begin by considering short times,
t\ll\hbar/\Lambda=L_{0}/c
. In this limit, we can use the expansions shown in Table
1
and write the decoherence function as

\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{v})}(t)=\frac{8\Xi^{2}v^{2}m^{2}}{15\pi E_{\rm P}^{2}}\quantity[\frac{1}{6}\qty(\frac{\Lambda}{\hbar})^2-\frac{GM_N}{R_N^3}]\quantity(\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar})^{3}\quantity(\frac{1}{8}\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar}+\frac{1}{3}\frac{\kappa}{E_{\rm rest}^2}).

(17.4)

First, let us note that there seems to be a competition between the squared frequencies
\quantity(\Lambda/\hbar)^{2}
and
GM_{N}/R_{N}^{3}
, and decoherence occurs only if the former is greater than the latter. Typically, this is the case, as one can see from Table
2
, where we estimate some values of both squared frequencies for different detector sizes and different sources of Newtonian gravitational potential. However, it seems to be possible, at least in principle, to have a situation in which the reversed scenario holds by increasing the detector size as well as the density of the Newtonian source. Of course, one must be careful not to violate any of our assumptions in doing so, such as the Newtonian approximation, which prevents us from increasing the density of the source to an arbitrarily large value. For instance, we show the result of the tidal squared frequency for neutron stars for informational purposes, but let us not forget that we are working in a perturbative regime and dropping higher order terms in the tidal tensor. We will return to this discussion at the end of the chapter.

Let us rewrite the decoherence function as

\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{v})}(t)=\frac{4}{45\pi}\delta\Omega\quantity(\frac{\Xi}{L_0})^{2}\quantity(\frac{v}{c})^{2}\quantity(\frac{m}{M_{\rm P}})^{2}\quantity(\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar})^{3}\quantity(\frac{1}{8}\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar}+\frac{1}{3}\frac{\kappa}{E_{\rm rest}^2}),

(17.5)

where
M_{\textrm{P}}
is the Planck mass, and we define

\delta\Omega\equiv 1-6\quantity(\frac{\hbar}{\Lambda})^{2}\frac{GM_{N}}{R_{N}^{3}}.

(17.6)

Note that
\delta\Omega\leq 1
and typically one has
\delta\Omega\simeq 1
.

The decoherence function depends on many parameters, with the ratio
\Xi/L_{0}
among them. Recall that, while
L_{0}
represents a typical geodesic deviation with respect to the much more massive mass
M
at the center of our reference frame,
\Xi
basically denotes the size of the superposition. For simplicity, we can consider from now on that one of the paths is close enough to the reference mass
M
such that we can take
\Xi\sim L_{0}
.

Now, let us note from Eq. (
17.5
) that, as long as
t\gg(8/3)\eta t(m,T_{\rm int})
, with
t(m,T_{\rm int})=L(m,T_{\rm int})/c
denoting some typical time scale determined by the properties of the system, the contribution from the interplay between the gravitons and the internal DoFs becomes negligible. For instance, for the typical values of the mass and internal temperature we discussed before,
t(m,T_{\rm int})\sim t_{\rm P}
, the Planck time.

In summary, in the regime in which

\frac{8}{3}\eta\frac{L(m,T_{\rm int})}{c}\ll t\ll\frac{L_{0}}{c},

(17.7)

only the interaction with the graviton bath dominates, and the decoherence function becomes

\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{v})}(t)=\frac{1}{90\pi}\delta\Omega\quantity(\frac{v}{c})^{2}\quantity(\frac{m}{M_{\rm P}})^{2}\quantity(\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar})^{4}.

(17.8)

We can compute the decoherence time
t_{\rm dec}
, defined by
\Gamma(t_{\rm dec})=1
. One finds
39
39

39

A word on notation: let
t_{\rm dec}
denote decoherence times computed in the short-time limit, while
\tau_{\rm dec}
denotes decoherence times computed in the long-time limit.

t^{(\textrm{v})}_{\rm dec}=\frac{\hbar}{\Lambda}\sqrt{\sqrt{\frac{90\pi}{\delta\Omega}}\frac{c}{v}\frac{M_{\rm P}}{m}}.

(17.9)

Consistency demands that
t^{(\textrm{v})}_{\rm dec}\ll\hbar/\Lambda
, and, as a consequence, graviton-induced decoherence occurs for systems that satisfy

mv\gg\sqrt{\frac{90\pi}{\delta\Omega}}M_{\rm P}c,

(17.10)

which agrees with the conclusions obtained by Kanno et al.
[
90
]
(Section
11
). For
\delta\Omega\simeq 1
this means that we must have
mv\gg 110\,\textrm{kg}\cdot\textrm{m}/\textrm{s}
.

Eq. (
17.10
) shows that the observation of graviton-induced decoherence requires the preparation of spatial quantum superpositions of macroscopic masses. Even if we were to extrapolate our results to the ultra-relativistic limit,
v\simeq c
, the decoherence condition would require masses satisfying
m\gg 3.7\times 10^{-7}
kg.

In conclusion, the short-time limit is dominated solely by the graviton bath, and decoherence generally does not occur for microscopic, typical experimentally accessible masses. One can hope that the scenario improves for long times. However, if we consider only the pure graviton contribution, one can see from Table
1
that
f_{\rm v}^{(I)}
tends to a constant value as
t\to\infty
and the decoherence function saturates at

\Gamma^{(\textrm{v})}_{1,\rm sat}=\frac{16}{5\pi}\delta\Omega\quantity(\frac{v}{c})^{2}\quantity(\frac{m}{M_{\rm P}})^{2},

(17.11)

increasing no further. Therefore, the graviton bath alone cannot decohere spatial superpositions of a microscopic mass. The situation changes in the long-time limit when we consider the graviton interplay with the other contributions (internal DoFs and the Newtonian potential), since the other functions exhibit no such behavior as
t\to\infty
. Let us then return our attention to Eq. (
17
) and consider the long-time limit.

17.2
Long-time limit

We can see from Table
1
that, for
x\gg 1
, the functions
f_{\rm v}^{(II)}(x)
and
f_{\rm v}^{(IV)}(x)
, which include the internal DoFs contributions, scale as
x^{3}
, while
f_{\rm v}^{(I)}(x)
remains constant and
f_{\rm v}^{(III)}(x)
grows in a much slower logarithmic rate. This means that, regardless of how small the ratio
\mathscr{R}=\kappa/E_{\rm rest}^{2}
is, there will always be a time, no matter how long, when the G+I contributions will dominate. Since these contributions will continue to increase without bound, decoherence will eventually occur.

In the long-time limit, using definition (
17.6
) and taking
\Xi\sim L_{0}
, Eq. (
17
) becomes

\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{v})}(t)=\frac{4}{135}\delta\Omega\quantity(\frac{v}{c})^{2}\quantity(\frac{\eta k_BT_{\rm int}\Lambda}{E_{\rm P}^2})\quantity(\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar})^{3}.

(17.12)

Interestingly, we see that the decoherence function (
17.12
) does not seem to contain any explicit dependence on the mass of the composite particle. This does not mean, however, that this function does not depend at all on the mass, since the long-time limit was established according to the ratio
\kappa/E_{\rm rest}^{2}
, which
does
depend on it. Also, there could be some explicit dependence on
m
through the dimensionless coupling constant
\eta
.

From Eq. (
17.12
), we can compute the decoherence time, for which
\Gamma(\tau_{\rm dec})=1
. One finds

\displaystyle\tau_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{v})}

\displaystyle=\quantity[\frac{135}{4}\frac{1}{\delta\Omega}\qty(\frac{c}{v})^2\frac{\hbar^4c^5}{Gk_B}\frac{1}{\eta T_{\rm int}\Lambda^4}]^{1/3}

\displaystyle=\quantity[\frac{135}{4}\frac{1}{\delta\Omega}\qty(\frac{c}{v})^2\frac{cL_0^4}{Gk_B\eta T_{\rm int}}]^{1/3}

(17.13)

This is still a typically long time. For instance, consider a strong coupling scenario and take
\eta\sim 1
. Also, consider
T_{\rm int}\sim 10^{4}
K, a typical molecular speed of the order
v\sim 10^{-6}c
,
L_{0}\sim 10^{-9}
m, and
\delta\Omega\sim 1
. Then one obtains
\tau_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{v})}\sim 10^{5}
s (about
1.2
days). Nevertheless, this shows that decoherence eventually does happen when we consider the interplay between the gravitons and the internal DoFs of the system, as opposed to the case where only the graviton bath contributes directly.

This analysis was performed by considering gravitons initially in the vacuum state. However, other possible states can significantly decrease the decoherence time, as we will see now.

18
Decoherence for initial thermal, coherent, and squeezed states

We can now repeat the same analysis for the other initial states for which the decoherence function is given by Eq. (
16.1
). The functions
f_{A}
were all listed in Section
16
, and some of their relevant properties are shown in Tables
3
,
4
, and
5
(see also Figures
9
,
10
, and
11
). The procedure is basically the same, so we will simply list the results.

.

Just as we did for the case of the initial vacuum state, we begin by considering the short-time limit. For
t\ll\hbar/\Lambda_{A}
, we can use the expansions for the functions
f_{A}(x)
around
x=0
that are shown in Tables
3
,
4
, and
5
. Once again, the purely graviton bath contribution dominates in this regime, and one finds the decoherence times to be given by

t_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{t})}=\quantity[1+\frac{32}{21}\delta\Omega_{\rm t}\frac{(\pi k_B T_{\rm g})^6}{\Lambda^6}]^{-1/4}t_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{v})},

(18.1a)

t_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{c})}=\quantity(1+\alpha^2)^{-1/4}t_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{v})},

(18.1b)

t_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{s})}=e^{-r/2}t_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{v})},

(18.1c)

where

\delta\Omega_{\rm t}\equiv 1+6\quantity(\qty(\frac{\hbar}{\Lambda})^2-\frac{7}{20}\qty(\frac{\hbar}{\pi k_BT_{\rm g}})^2)\frac{GM_{N}}{R_{N}^{3}},

(18.2)

and with
t_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{v})}
given by Eq. (
17.9
). Note that there is a decrease in decoherence time when considering such states, with
t_{\rm dec}
even exhibiting an exponential decay for the squeezed state.

Next, we turn to the long-time limit. Similar to the case of the initial vacuum state, one can see from Tables
3
and
4
that, for the thermal and coherent states, the contributions involving the internal DoFs keep increasing for
\Lambda_{A}t/\hbar\gg 1
, while the others either saturate or exhibit a slower logarithmic behavior. Then, using the expressions from the tables, in the long-time limit, we find the decoherence times to be given by

\tau_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{t})}=\quantity[1+\frac{32}{21}\delta\Omega_{\rm t}\frac{(\pi k_B T_{\rm g})^6}{\Lambda^6}]^{-1/3}\tau_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{v})},

(18.3a)

\tau_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{c})}=\quantity(1+\frac{\alpha^2}{2})^{-1/3}\tau_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{v})},

(18.3b)

with
\tau_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{v})}
given by Eq. (
17.2
).

However, the situation is quite different for the initial squeezed state. The internal DoFs contributions behave as sine functions, and therefore they do not keep increasing. Due to the typical small values of the ratio
\mathscr{R}=\kappa/E_{\rm rest}^{2}
, this means that the contributions coming from the graviton bath alone also dominate in the long-time limit
40
40

40

It is important to remark that we are referring only to the additional contribution to the decoherence function for the squeezed state. One should keep in mind that, for all states, this is added to the vacuum term, for which the G+I contributions dominate for long times, as shown in Section
17
.
. Now,
f_{\rm s}^{(I)}(x)
saturates at a constant value. Furthermore, although there is a logarithmic increase in
f_{\rm s}^{(III)}(x)
, this is still much smaller than the vacuum behavior, which goes as
\sim x^{3}
as we saw in Section
17
. Therefore, the long-time limit simply reads

\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{s})}(t)=(\cosh 2r)\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{v})}(t),

(18.4)

with
\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{v})}(t)
given by Eq. (
17.12
). At last, the decoherence time is

\tau_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{s})}=\quantity(\cosh 2r)^{-1/3}\tau_{\rm dec}^{(\textrm{v})}.

(18.5)

We see that, also in the long-time limit, there is an exponential decay of the decoherence time with respect to the vacuum state. In Ref.
[
73
]
, the authors argue that relic gravitons should now be in squeezed states and also estimate a squeeze parameter of order
r\sim 10^{2}
, for which
\quantity(\cosh 2r)^{-1/3}\sim 10^{-29}
, a significant reduction in the graviton-induced decoherence time.

19
The possibility of gravitational recoherence

Before we close this chapter, let us address one concern raised in Section
17
. We saw in Eq. (
17.11
) that when we consider only the G contribution to the decoherence function for the vacuum state, it saturates and changes no further. The situation is a little different when we include the Newtonian potential, since the G+N contribution does not saturate to a fixed value but keeps increasing with time. So, let us now consider a particle with no internal DoFs (or simply frozen ones). Then, the decoherence function for the vacuum state reduces to

\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{v})}(t)=\frac{16\Xi^{2}v^{2}m^{2}}{5\pi E_{\rm P}^{2}}\left\{\quantity(\frac{\Lambda}{\hbar})^{2}f_{\rm v}^{(I)}\quantity(\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar})-\frac{GM_{N}}{R_{N}^{3}}f_{\rm v}^{(III)}\quantity(\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar})\right\}.

(19.1)

We already saw that, in the short-time limit, this reduces to Eq. (
17.8
). But now there are no internal DoFs to dominate in the long-time regime, and one may be concerned about the fact that the G contribution remains constant while the G+N contribution, which has a negative sign, keeps increasing with time. From Table
1
we see that
f_{\rm v}^{(I)}(x)\sim 1
and
f_{\rm v}^{(III)}(x)\sim 8\ln x
for
x\gg 1
, and the decoherence function becomes

\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{v})}(t)\simeq\frac{16}{5\pi}\quantity(\frac{v}{c})^{2}\quantity(\frac{m}{M_{\rm P}})^{2}\quantity[1-8\qty(\frac{\hbar}{\Lambda})^2\frac{GM_N}{R_N^3}\ln\qty(\frac{\Lambda t}{\hbar})].

(19.2)

This means that, when there are no internal DoFs to guarantee gravitational decoherence, not only does the function stop increasing with time, but it also starts to decrease (although very slowly). In light of this behavior, one may raise the concern that the "decoherence" function could ever become negative. In principle, this can happen, and we can compute the time it takes for that to occur. A quick analysis shows that
\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{v})}(t)<0
for

t\gtrsim\frac{\hbar}{\Lambda}\exp\quantity[\frac{1}{8}\qty(\frac{\Lambda}{\hbar})^2\frac{R_N^3}{GM_N}].

(19.3)

Now, even for a superposition size on the order of kilometers,
L_{0}\sim 10^{3}
m, we find
\quantity(\frac{\Lambda}{\hbar})^{2}\frac{R_{N}^{3}}{GM_{N}}\sim 10^{16}
near Earth. Take the exponential of that number, and we are clearly talking about an infinitely long time before such recoherence can happen.

For the coherent and squeezed state, the situation is quite similar since the G+N contributions in those cases also grow as
\ln(\Lambda t/\hbar)
for
t\gg\hbar/\Lambda
. For the thermal state, however, Table
3
shows that
f_{\rm t}^{(III)}(x)\sim 4x
, which grows faster than the logarithm. The thermal decoherence function, in the absence of internal DoFs and in the long-time limit, reads

\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{t})}(t)\simeq\frac{16}{5\pi}\quantity(\frac{v}{c})^{2}\quantity(\frac{m}{M_{\rm P}})^{2}\quantity[1+\qty(\frac{\pi k_B T_{\rm g}}{\Lambda})^2\qty(1-\frac{4\hbar}{\pi k_B T_{\rm g}}\frac{GM_N}{R_N^3}t)].

(19.4)

In that case, the condition
\Gamma_{1}^{(\textrm{t})}(t)<0
is achieved by

t\gtrsim\frac{\pi k_{B}T_{\rm g}}{4\hbar}\frac{R_{N}^{3}}{GM_{N}}\quantity[1+\qty(\frac{\Lambda}{\pi k_B T_{\rm g}})^2].

(19.5)

For
T_{\rm g}\sim 1
K, we have
\frac{\pi k_{B}T_{\rm g}}{4\hbar}\frac{R_{N}^{3}}{GM_{N}}\sim 10^{17}
s (near Earth), which is about the age of the universe.

In conclusion, the presence of the Newtonian potential seems to slightly slow down the decoherence mechanism, which can eventually lead to the recoherence of the system. Although possible in principle, this is not expected to happen for typical parameters. Even if it were so, we saw in the previous sections that decoherence will inevitably prevail when we include the interaction with the system’s own internal structure.

Nevertheless, we should mention that recoherence mechanisms can appear in non-Markovian models for open quantum systems, such as when one describes quantum fields in cosmological backgrounds (see Ref.
[
44
]
). The connection between memory (non-Markovian) effects and recoherence is explored in Ref.
[
95
]
.

Chapter 6
Graviton-induced entropy production

Let us consider once again an open system coupled with a bath of gravitons, only now we include an external agent acting upon it according to some force protocol
f(t)
in a given time interval. Due to the coupling with the environment, whose final state is not of interest, the dynamics of the system evolves according to the combined action of the deterministic force
f(t)
and a stochastic force term encompassing the influence of the environment, as discussed in Section
8
on more general grounds. Now, this coarse-grained description of the environment inevitably leads to dissipation. We then turn to the question of how much work is dissipated when we try to move a system through a bath of gravitons, which we attempt to answer by verifying how the fluctuation theorem applies to the problem at hand (see Appendix
10
for a brief review of the fluctuation theorem, or, more precisely, the Crooks fluctuation theorem and Jarzynski’s equality).

The fluctuation theorem was established for a quantum system under the effects of relativistic time dilation in Ref.
[
15
]
, and it was later generalized to include the effects of
classical
spacetime curvature
[
16
]
and to apply to a quantum field
[
46
]
. While these references treated spacetime as a classical entity, here we aim to apply the fluctuation theorem to a system under the influence of
quantum
spacetime fluctuations.

One of the main conceptual difficulties faced in establishing a quantum fluctuation theorem is the definition of work. While there seems to be general agreement in defining work via the two-time measurement scheme
[
157
]
for closed systems, the proper definition for open quantum systems is still under debate. Here, we follow the proposal of Ref.
[
155
]
to define work as it is done in classical systems by making sense of the notion of trajectories in quantum mechanics. In order to do so, we shall turn to the
decoherent
(or
consistent
)
histories
formalism, which we discussed in Section
9
. As we saw in that section, the solutions of the Langevin equation describe trajectories of the system, provided they are followed with an accuracy of an order
\sim N^{-1}
, with
N
being the noise kernel of the environment. The idea is then to use these solutions to define the work done on the open system.

Since we are going to need the expressions for the solutions to the Langevin equation, we begin by discussing them in Section
20
. Then, in Section
21
, we use these solutions to define work and establish the fluctuation theorem in order to quantify the entropy production.

20
The Langevin equation with graviton noise

We begin by going back to Eq. (
15
), which describes the external degrees of freedom density matrix of a quantum system coupled with both gravitational and its own internal degrees of freedom. As we have discussed in Chapter
4
, the term containing the internal DoFs noise kernel may also be rewritten in terms of a Gaussian stochastic variable, just as we have done for the gravitational noise kernel. Indeed, Eq. (
15
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm ext}(\xi,\xi^{\prime},t)=\int\differential\xi(0)\differential\xi^{\prime}(0)\,\rho_{\rm ext}(\xi(0),\xi^{\prime}(0),0)\int\mathcal{D}\xi\mathcal{D}\xi^{\prime}\,e^{\frac{i}{2}m_{0}\delta^{ij}\int\differential t\,\quantity(\dot{\xi}_i\dot{\xi}_j-\dot{\xi}_i'\dot{\xi}_j')}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int\mathcal{D}\mathcal{N}\,\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}]\mathscr{P}[\mathcal{N}_{\rm int}]\,e^{-im\int\differential t\,\mathcal{N}^{ij}\quantity(\xi_i\xi_j-\xi_i'\xi_j')}e^{-\frac{i}{2}\int\differential t\,\mathcal{N}_{\rm int}\quantity(\dot{\xi}_i\dot{\xi}_j-\dot{\xi}_i'\dot{\xi}_j')}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times e^{i\int\differential t\,\mathcal{N}_{\rm int}\mathcal{N}^{ij}\quantity(\xi_i\xi_j-\xi_i'\xi_j')},

(20.1)

where
\mathscr{P}
denotes the Gaussian probability density and
\mathcal{N}
(
\mathcal{N}_{\rm int}
) is the stochastic variable describing the interaction with the gravitons (internal degrees of freedom).

The system is then described by a stochastic effective action given by

\displaystyle S_{\rm SEA}[\xi,\xi^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=S_{\rm p}[\xi]-S_{\rm p}[\xi^{\prime}]-\frac{1}{2}\int\differential t\,\mathcal{N}_{\rm int}\quantity(\dot{\xi}_i\dot{\xi}_j-\dot{\xi}_i'\dot{\xi}_j')

\displaystyle-\int\differential t\,\quantity(m-\mathcal{N}_{\rm int})\mathcal{N}^{ij}\quantity(\xi_i\xi_j-\xi_i'\xi_j'),

(20.2)

with

S_{\rm p}[\xi]=\int\differential t\,\quantity[\frac{1}{2}m\,\delta_{ij}\dot{\xi}^i\dot{\xi}^j-V(\xi)],

(20.3)

and we included a potential function
V(\xi)
to account for the possibility of an external agent acting on the system.

The equation of motion is obtained by setting
\delta S_{\rm SEA}=0
as usual, resulting in

\quantity[m-\mathcal{N}_{\rm int}(t)]\ddot{\xi}_{i}(t)+\partialderivative{V}{\xi^i}-\dot{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm int}(t)\dot{\xi}_{i}(t)+2\quantity[m-\mathcal{N}_{\rm int}(t)]\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t)\xi^{j}(t)=0.

(20.4)

Analytically solving this second-order differential equation for the geodesic deviation
\xi(t)
is no easy task. Since we are going to need an explicit form for the solution, we will drop the internal DoFs contribution and analyze the effects only due to the gravitons for simplicity. Additionally, we choose to work on a perturbative regime for
\xi_{i}(t)
. Then, by taking
\mathcal{N}_{\rm int}\to 0
one is left with
41
41

41

We changed the sign of the noise term for convenience, which is allowed since this is simply a change of variables in the path integral.

m\ddot{\xi}_{i}(t)=f_{i}(t)+2m\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t)\xi_{0}^{j}(t),

(20.5)

where
f_{i}
is defined such that
V(\xi)=-f_{i}(t)\xi^{i}(t)
and
\xi_{0}^{i}
is the solution to
m\ddot{\xi}_{0}^{i}=f^{i}
.

Eq. (
20.5
) is the Langevin equation for the time evolution of a system which is affected by a stochastic force term that comes from the interaction with the bath of gravitons. The effects of such stochastic noise are also analyzed in Refs.
[
120
,
121
,
122
,
90
,
38
,
39
,
36
]
.

The solution to the Langevin equation for the initial conditions
\xi^{i}(0)=\dot{\xi}^{i}(0)=0
is given by

\xi_{i}(t)=\frac{1}{m}\int_{0}^{t}\differential t^{\prime}\,\quantity(t-t')f_{i}(t^{\prime})+2\int_{0}^{t}\differential t^{\prime}\,\quantity(t-t')\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t^{\prime})\xi_{0}^{j}(t^{\prime}).

(20.6)

This can be seen as the classical limit for the geodesic separation between two test masses, where the stochastic behavior comes from the quantum fluctuations of the gravitational field. However, as we discussed in the beginning of the chapter, for paths with accuracy determined by the noise kernel, we can use the solution to the Langevin equation to provide a definition of work for the
quantum
system in the same way that is done for classical systems, which in turn is heavily dependent on the notion of a trajectory in space. This will allow us to establish the fluctuation theorem for the quantum system and we shall see how dissipation arises when an external agent tries to move the system through a bath of gravitons.

21
Dissipated work and entropy

In order to discuss the validity of the fluctuation theorem, we proceed as in Ref.
[
155
]
. By fluctuation theorem we mean Jarzynski’s equality, which is the integral form of the Crooks fluctuation theorem
[
84
,
48
,
78
,
86
,
85
,
30
]
(see Appendix
10
). For closed quantum systems, the derivation of the theorem relies on the hypothesis of initial thermal state of the entire system. However, our analysis of a quantum particle interacting with a bath of gravitons is built under the assumption of initial product state, which is obviously not a thermal one. In order to circumvent this issue, we take the initial time to be
t_{0}=-\infty
. At this time instant, we assume that the total state of the system is described by a tensor product,
\hat{\rho}(-\infty)=\hat{\rho}_{\rm sys}(-\infty)\otimes\hat{\rho}_{\rm grav}(-\infty)
, where
\hat{\rho}_{\rm grav}
is a thermal state of gravitons. We then allow the system to evolve according to the total action with
f^{i}(t)=f^{i}(0)
for
t<0
so that we obtain a total thermal state at
t=0
. Then, the driving force starts changing according to some arbitrary protocol until a given time instant
t=\tau
. The work performed on the system by the external agent in the interval
\quantity[0,\tau]
is then defined by

W\equiv-\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential t\,\dot{f}_{i}(t)\xi^{i}(t),

(21.1)

where
\xi^{i}(t)
is the solution to the Langevin equation given in Eq. (
20.6
). By using the explicit forms of the solutions, we can write

W=-\frac{1}{m}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\dot{f}^{i}(t)g(t-t^{\prime})f_{i}(t^{\prime})-2\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\dot{f}^{i}(t)g(t-t^{\prime})\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t^{\prime})\xi^{j}_{0}(t^{\prime}),

(21.2)

where
g(t-t^{\prime})=\quantity(t-t')\theta(t-t^{\prime})
.

We can see from Eq. (
21.2
) that the work performed on the system is linear in
\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t)
, which is a Gaussian random process. Therefore,
W
itself must also be a Gaussian random variable with its statistics specified by the first two moments
\expectationvalue{W}
and
\sigma_{W}^{2}=\expectationvalue{W^2}-\expectationvalue{W}^{2}
. We can then write the work probability density function

\mathscr{P}(W)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_{W}^{2}}}e^{-\quantity(W-\expval{W})^{2}/2\sigma_{W}^{2}}.

(21.3)

Throughout this chapter, the brackets denote the stochastic average with a Gaussian probability density.

Before we proceed, it is worth emphasizing that the average with
\mathscr{P}(W)
is the same stochastic average with
\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{N})
due to the linear dependence of
W
on
\mathcal{N}_{ij}(t)
. In usual formulations of the classical fluctuation theorem, such stochastic force is not considered, and the probabilistic aspect of the work done on the system comes from the initial thermal state assumption. Each sampling from the initial state gives rise to a trajectory, and the average is performed over an ensemble of such realizations. For the quantum fluctuation theorem, the inherent quantum uncertainty of the initial state contributes a further probabilistic aspect. However, we chose to follow an equivalent initial state preparation method based on a product initial state for the total system. This choice replaces the system’s dependence on the initial state with the properties of noise statistics and, consequently, one is left with only one probabilistic element instead of two
[
155
]
.

The moments of the work distribution can be computed as follows. First, since
\expectationvalue{\mathcal{N}^{ij}(t)}=\frac{1}{2}\Phi_{ij}
, with
\Phi_{ij}
being the tidal tensor
42
42

42

Recall that we changed the sign of the stochastic noise variable.
, we have

\expectationvalue{W}=-\frac{1}{m}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\dot{f}^{i}(t)g(t-t^{\prime})f_{i}(t^{\prime})-\Phi_{ij}\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\dot{f}^{i}(t)g(t-t^{\prime})\xi^{j}_{0}(t^{\prime}).

(21.4)

Additionally, by using
43
43

43

We dropped the index g since, in this section, there is to be no confusion with the noise kernel coming from any other environment.

\expectationvalue{\mathcal{N}^{ij}(t)\mathcal{N}^{kl}(t')}=N^{ijkl}(t,t^{\prime})
, which is the noise kernel of gravitons, we also find

\sigma_{W}^{2}=\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential t\differential t^{\prime}\,\dot{f}^{i}(t)\sigma_{ij}(t,t^{\prime})\dot{f}^{j}(t^{\prime}),

(21.5)

where

\sigma_{ij}(t,t^{\prime})=4\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential t_{1}\differential t_{2}\,g(t-t_{1})g(t^{\prime}-t_{2})N_{ijkl}(t_{1},t_{2})\xi^{k}_{0}(t_{1})\xi^{l}_{0}(t_{2}).

(21.6)

Note that, while
\expectationvalue{W}
is independent of the noise kernel of gravitons, the second moment
\sigma_{W}^{2}
is not.

With the expressions for the first and second moments of work, the probability distribution is completely specified. One can now compute

\expectationvalue{e^{-\beta W}}=\int\mathcal{D}W\,\mathscr{P}(W)e^{-\beta W},

(21.7)

where
\beta
is the inverse temperature of the bath of gravitons. Since Jarzynski’s equality holds for the entire system, as argued in the beginning of this section, we must have

\expectationvalue{e^{-\beta W}}=e^{-\beta\Delta F},

(21.8)

where
\Delta F=F(\tau)-F(0)
is the free energy difference between time instants
t=0
and
t=\tau
, calculated quantum mechanically for the total system.

A direct calculation turns Eq. (
21.7
) into

\expectationvalue{e^{-\beta W}}=e^{-\beta\quantity(\expval{W}-\beta\sigma_W^2/2)},

(21.9)

which, upon comparison with Eq. (
21.8
), allows us to identify the free energy difference of the total system as

\Delta F=\expectationvalue{W}-\beta\sigma_{W}^{2}/2.

(21.10)

At last, we identify the dissipated work
W_{\rm diss}
with

\displaystyle W_{\rm diss}

\displaystyle=\expectationvalue{W}-\Delta F=\beta\sigma_{W}^{2}/2

\displaystyle=2\beta\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential t_{1}\cdots\differential t_{4}\,\dot{f}^{i}(t_{1})\dot{f}^{j}(t_{2})g(t_{1}-t_{3})g(t_{2}-t_{4})N_{ijkl}(t_{3},t_{4})\xi_{0}^{k}(t_{3})\xi_{0}^{l}(t_{4}).

(21.11)

For movement along the
z-
direction, we computed the noise kernel
N(t,t^{\prime})=N_{3333}(t,t^{\prime})
in Appendix
9
(naturally, in this case we are interested in the initial thermal state for the gravitons).

Finally, we conclude that there is an entropy production in the system given by

\expectationvalue{\Sigma}=\beta W_{\rm diss}=\frac{\beta^{2}\sigma_{W}^{2}}{2}.

(21.12)

Note that when there are no gravitons, or when
f=0
, we have
\sigma_{W}^{2}=0
and, consequently, there is no production of entropy. Additionally, we remark that the thermodynamical interpretation of this entropy is tied to the interpretation of the parameter
\beta
as a proper temperature, as discussed in Section
10
. And, lastly, we point out that, since Eq. (
21.12
) arises due to the fluctuating spacetime, one expects this entropy production to be a universal and unavoidable feature of driven (classical and quantum) systems.

Conclusions and future work

The quantum fluctuations of spacetime, however weak, cannot be avoided since spacetime is the background in which matter propagates and interacts. Here, we quantified the consequences of such realization for the decoherence of spatial superpositions and entropy production. We began by considering a composite quantum system in a spacetime described by a classical static Newtonian potential and the quantum gravitational radiation degrees of freedom. Such a spacetime also induces interaction between the center-of-mass variable and the internal degrees of freedom of the composite system. Since we were interested in studying spatial superpositions of the center-of-mass variable, we integrated out both the gravitational radiation degrees of freedom and the internal variables of the system itself. The description of this open quantum system coupled with two mutually interacting environments was accomplished within the influence functional approach, which allowed us to obtain the decoherence function for the center-of-mass variable in a superposition of two classically distinguishable paths.

We found that, although the interaction with gravitons alone cannot decohere spatial superpositions of microscopic systems (in agreement with previous results in the literature
[
90
]
), the interplay between the gravitons and the system’s internal structure will inevitably lead to decoherence, even if it happens for typically long times. Such decoherence times were found to depend on the initial state of the gravitons. In fact, for gravitons initially in thermal, coherent, and squeezed states, the decoherence time exhibits a decrease with respect to the value found by considering an initial vacuum state. This decrease can be significant, especially for an initial squeezed state, which is expected to describe relic gravitons
[
73
]
.

Apart from the enhancement of gravitational decoherence arising from a suitable choice of the initial graviton state, we emphasize the role of the interplay between gravity and the internal degrees of freedom of the system in leading to decoherence, even in situations where it was not expected to occur at all. One can think of the system’s internal structure acting as an environment that, due to the universal aspect of gravity, works as a mediator and amplifier of the effects of the quantum spacetime fluctuations. This opens the possibility of considering more general scenarios in which a system is in simultaneous interaction with both a gravitational and a non-gravitational environment. Since these will inevitably interact with each other, we are left to wonder what the effects of such interplay on the system are. Whether non-gravitational environments can be used to mediate and amplify gravitational decoherence, as the internal structure of the system can, is a subject of future investigation.

In this work, we also analyzed the entropy production coming from the driving of a quantum system through a bath of gravitons. This was done via the decoherent histories approach to quantum mechanics, which allowed us to give meaning to the concept of trajectory in space and to provide a suitable definition of work done on the open quantum system in complete analogy with classical mechanics. With this definition at hand, the fluctuation theorem was established in the form of Jarzynski’s equality, from which the entropy production was estimated. We remark on the universal character of this entropy production, which arises from the quantum degrees of freedom of the universal gravitational radiation.

Although we considered the entropy produced when the system is driven by an external agent through the graviton bath, it would be interesting to investigate the case in which the driving agent is spacetime itself. For instance, one could analyze the work done by the stochastic force that comes from the gravitational fluctuations. However, such a work distribution is not expected to be Gaussian, which would render the analysis more complex.

In general, it would be interesting to see how these results apply to relativistic particles and even quantum fields. For instance, one could study how graviton-induced decoherence affects wave-packet dispersion in neutrino propagation (and, in the spirit of the previous discussion, how other non-gravitational environments, like matter effects, could amplify such decoherence and modify the neutrino oscillation probability formula). Finally, it would also be interesting to investigate how different spacetimes could affect our results. Here, we have expanded the gravitational field around a metric in the Newtonian limit, but the same can be done for any other classical solution to Einstein’s equation, such as the Schwarzschild solution or the Robertson-Walker expanding universe. Whether such curved backgrounds shall have a significant impact on the results of graviton-induced decoherence and entropy production remains to be seen.

Part III
Appendices

Chapter 7
Differential geometry

The main goal of this appendix is to provide a brief review of the mathematics behind the general theory of relativity, namely differential geometry. This is by no means an extensive or complete presentation of the subject, with many mathematical concepts and demonstrations left out. Our approach is heavily based on mathematical physics textbooks such as
[
114
,
135
]
, as well as some very good books on general relativity
[
35
,
159
,
161
,
152
]
. For a more mathematically rigorous approach the reader is referred to Ref.
[
130
]
.

Appendix 7.A
Differentiable manifolds

We begin with what is probably the most fundamental concept in differential geometry, the one of a differentiable manifold (sometimes we just write manifold, leaving the word "differentiable" implicit). The basic intuitive idea of a manifold is that of a space that may or may not be curved while looking flat in small enough regions. The entire manifold is then constructed by smoothly sewing together those regions. Now, in order to give this idea a precise mathematical meaning, we are going to need some preliminary definitions.

The first basic definition we are going to need is that of a topological space. A
topological space

(X,\mathcal{T})
consists of a set
X
together with a collection
\mathcal{T}
of subsets of
X
satisfying the following three properties:

1.

If
O_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{T}
for all
\alpha
, then

\bigcup_{\alpha}O_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{T}.

(7.A.1)

2.

If
O_{1},\dots,O_{n}\in\mathcal{T}
(
n
is a finite number), then

\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}O_{i}\in\mathcal{T}.

(7.A.2)

3.

The entire set
X
and the empty set
\emptyset
are in
\mathcal{T}
.

The collection
\mathcal{T}
is referred to as a
topology
on
X
, and subsets of
X
which are listed in the collection
\mathcal{T}
are called
open sets
.

Another indispensable idea is the notion of a metric space. A
metric

d:X\times X\to\mathbb{R}
is a function that satisfies the conditions:

•

d(x,y)=d(y,x)
,

•

d(x,y)\geq 0
, where the equality holds if and only if
x=y
, and

•

d(x,y)+d(y,z)\geq d(x,z)
,

for all
x,y,z\in X
. If
X
is endowed with a metric
d
,
X
is made into a topological space whose open sets are given by open discs centered at each point
x
,

O_{\epsilon}(x)=\quantity{y\in X|d(x,y)<\epsilon},

(7.A.3)

and all their possible unions. The topology
\mathcal{T}
thus defined is called the
metric topology
determined by
d
. The topological space
(X,\mathcal{T})
is called a
metric space
.

If
(X,\mathcal{T})
and
(Y,\mathcal{J})
are topological spaces, a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
is said to be
continuous
if the inverse image,
f^{-1}(O)=\quantity{x\in X|f(x)\in O}
, of every open set
O
in
Y
is an open set in
X
. If
(X,\mathcal{T})
and
(Y,\mathcal{J})
are topological spaces, a map
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a
homeomorphism
if it is continuous and has an inverse
f^{-1}:Y\rightarrow X
which is also continuous. If there is a homeomorphism between
X
and
Y
,
X
is said to be
homeomorphic
to
Y
and vice versa.

We are now ready to give a precise definition of differentiable manifolds. A set
M
is an
n-
dimensional
differentiable manifold
if

•

M
, together with a collection of its subsets, is a topological space;

•

M
is provided with a family of pairs
\quantity{(O_i,\phi_i)}
such that
\quantity{O_i}
is a family of open sets which covers
M
, that is,
\bigcup_{i}O_{i}=M
and
\phi_{i}
is a homeomorphism from
O_{i}
onto an open subset
U_{i}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(Figure
12
);

•

given
O_{i}
and
O_{j}
such that
O_{i}\cap O_{j}\neq\emptyset
for
i\neq j
, the map
\psi_{ij}=\phi_{i}\circ\phi_{j}^{-1}:\phi_{j}(O_{i}\cap O_{j})\mapsto\phi_{i}(O_{i}\cap O_{j})
is infinitely differentiable.

Figure 12:
Homeomorphisms
\phi_{i}
and
\phi_{j}
map open sets
O_{i}
and
O_{j}
on the manifold
M
into open sets
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
on Euclidean spaces.

The pair
(O_{i},\phi_{i})
is called a
chart
or a
coordinate system
, while the whole set
\quantity{(O_i,\phi_i)}
constitutes an
atlas
. The subset
O_{i}
is called the
coordinate neighborhood
and
\phi_{i}
the
coordinate function
. Given a point
p\in M
, the homeomorphism
\phi_{i}
is represented by the set of coordinates
\quantity{x^{\mu}(p)}=\quantity{x^0(p),\dots,x^{n-1}(p)}
.

A transformation that leaves invariant the differential structure described above is called a diffeomorphism. To be more precise, let
f:M\to M^{\prime}
be a homeomorphism and
\phi
and
\psi
coordinate functions, where the dimensions of
M
and
M^{\prime}
are
n
and
n^{\prime}
, respectively (Figure
13
). If
\psi\circ f\circ\phi^{-1}
is invertible, and both
y=\psi\circ f\circ\phi^{-1}(x)
and
x=\phi\circ f^{-1}\circ\psi^{-1}(y)
are
C^{\infty}
(infinitely differentiable), the map
f
is said to be a
diffeomorphism
and
M
and
M^{\prime}
are said to be
diffeomorphic
, which we denote as
M\equiv M^{\prime}
. It follows that if
M\equiv M^{\prime}
then
n=n^{\prime}
. In fact, two diffeomorphic manifolds are regarded as being the same manifold.

Figure 13:
The map
f:M\to M^{\prime}
has a coordinate representation given by
\psi\circ f\circ\phi^{-1}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n^{\prime}}
.

Since in physics the notion of a vector, and moreover a tensor, is a very useful one, it will be interesting to define vector spaces on a manifold. In order to do so, we need to define a very special class of maps called
curves
. An open curve on an
n-
dimensional manifold
M
is the map
c:(a,b)\to M
, where
(a,b)
is an open interval in
\mathbb{R}
such that
a<0<b
(Figure
14
). On a coordinate system
(O,\phi)
, a curve
c(t)
has the coordinate representation
x=\phi\circ c:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Figure 14:
A curve
c
in
M
, parametrized by
t
, and its coordinate representation
\phi\circ c
.

At last, we define a
function
on
M
as a smooth map from
M
to
\mathbb{R}
. On a coordinate system
(O,\phi)
, we can build the coordinate representation of
f
as
f\circ\phi^{-1}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, which is a real-valued function of
n
variables. The set of smooth functions on
M
is denoted by
\mathcal{F}(M)
.

We are now ready to introduce the concept of tensors in a manifold.

Appendix 7.B
Tensors

Let us begin with the more usual kind of tensor, namely a vector. In order to give a precise definition, let us consider a curve
c:(a,b)\to M
and a function
f:M\to\mathbb{R}
, where
(a,b)
is an open interval in
\mathbb{R}
containing the point
t=0
, with
t
being an arbitrary parametrization of the curve (Figure
15
). By choosing a local coordinate function
\phi
and using the chain rule, the rate of change of the function
f
at
t=0
along the curve
c
is given by

\displaystyle\evaluated{\dv{f(c(t))}{t}}_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\evaluated{\dv{t}(f\circ c)}_{t=0}=\evaluated{\dv{t}\qty[(f\circ\phi^{-1})\circ(\phi\circ c)]}_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\evaluated{\pdv{(f\circ\phi^{-1})}{x^\mu}\dv{(\phi\circ c)^\mu}{t}}_{t=0}=\evaluated{\dv{x^\mu(c(t))}{t}}_{t=0}\partial_{\mu}f,

(7.B.1)

with

\partial_{\mu}f=\partialderivative{f}{x^\mu}\equiv\partialderivative{(f\circ\phi^{-1})}{x^\mu}.

(7.B.2)

This means that the rate
\evaluated{(\dv*{f}{t})}_{t=0}
is obtained by the application of the differential operator

X=X^{\mu}\quantity(\pdv{x^\mu})\,\,\,\,\,\,\textrm{with}\,\,\,\,\,\,X^{\mu}=\evaluated{\dv{x^\mu(c(t))}{t}}_{t=0}

(7.B.3)

to the map
f
, that is

\evaluated{\dv{f(c(t))}{t}}_{t=0}=X^{\mu}\quantity(\pdv{f}{x^\mu})\equiv X(f).

(7.B.4)

We then define
X
as the
tangent vector
to
M
at the point
c(0)
along the direction given by the curve
c(t)
, as shown in Figure
15
.

Figure 15:
A curve
c
along with a function
f
and a local coordinate system
\phi
define the tangent vector
X
at a point of the manifold
M
in the direction determined by
c(t)
.

In summary, each curve through
p
defines an operator on
\mathcal{F}
, namely the tangent vector, which maps
f\mapsto\derivative*{f}{t}
at
p
. Basically, we are defining vectors at each point of
M
as directional derivatives on that point. Therefore, a tangent vector
X
at a point
p\in M
is a map
X:\mathcal{F}\to\mathbb{R}
which is linear and obeys the Leibniz rule,

•

X(af+bg)=aX(f)+bX(g)
,
\forall\,f,g\in\mathcal{F}
,
\forall\,a,b\in\mathbb{R}
;

•

X(fg)=g(p)X(f)+f(p)X(g)
,
\forall\,f,g\in\mathcal{F}
.

If
X_{1}=\derivative*{t_1}
and
X_{2}=\derivative*{t_2}
represent derivatives along two curves
x^{\mu}(t_{1})
and
x^{\nu}(t_{2})
through
p
, one can define a new operator
Y=aX_{1}+bX_{2}
,
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, which is manifestly linear and can be straightforwardly shown to satisfy the Leibniz rule. Thus, the set
T_{p}M
of all the tangent vectors of
p\in M
form a vector space, called the
tangent space
of
M
at
p
, whose dimension is the one of
M
. From Eq. (
7.B.3
) it is clear that
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{\mu}\equiv\partial_{\mu}
is a basis vector. The basis
\quantity{\vu{e}_\mu}
is called the
coordinate basis
.

We can now see how the vectors components change when we change coordinates. Let
p\in O_{i}\cap O_{j}
and
\phi_{i}(p)
and
\phi_{j}(p)
be the two coordinate functions represented by the set of coordinates
x^{\mu}
and
x^{\mu^{\prime}}
, respectively. Since the basis vectors in the coordinate system
x^{\mu}
are
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}
, the basis vectors in the new coordinate system
x^{\mu^{\prime}}
are given by

\partial_{\mu^{\prime}}=\partialderivative{x^\mu}{x^{\mu'}}\partial_{\mu}.

(7.B.5)

The vector
X=X^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
itself must be unchanged by a change of basis, so that

X^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}=X^{\mu^{\prime}}\partial_{\mu^{\prime}}=X^{\mu^{\prime}}\partialderivative{x^\mu}{x^{\mu'}}\partial_{\mu},

(7.B.6)

and hence, since the matrix
\partialderivative*{x^{\mu'}}{x^\mu}
is the inverse of the matrix
\partialderivative*{x^{\mu}}{x^{\mu'}}
,

X^{\mu^{\prime}}=\partialderivative{x^{\mu'}}{x^\mu}X^{\mu}.

(7.B.7)

The rule (
7.B.7
) for transforming components is what we call the
vector transformation law
.

If a vector is smoothly defined on each point of the manifold, we refer to it as a
vector field
. In other words,
X
is a vector field if
X[f]\in\mathcal{F}(M)
for any
f\in\mathcal{F}(M)
.

Since
T_{p}M
is a vector space, there exists a dual vector space to
T_{p}M
whose elements are linear functions from
T_{p}M
to
\mathbb{R}
. The dual space is called the
cotangent space
at
p
, denoted by
T_{p}^{*}M
. An element
\omega:T_{p}M\to\mathbb{R}
of
T_{p}^{*}M
is called a
dual vector
or a
cotangent vector
. The differential
\differential f
of a function
f\in\mathcal{F}(M)
is an example of a dual vector, with its action on
V\in T_{p}M
being defined by

\langle\differential f,V\rangle\equiv V(f)=V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f\in\mathbb{R}.

(7.B.8)

In terms of the coordinate
x=\phi(p)
,
\differential f
is expressed as
\differential f=\quantity(\pdv*{f}{x^\mu})\differential x^{\mu}
, where
\phi
is a coordinate function. One can naturally regard
\quantity{\dd x^\mu}
as a basis of
T_{p}^{*}M
, which is, in fact, a dual basis, since

\left\langle\differential x^{\nu},\partialderivative{x^\mu}\right\rangle=\partialderivative{x^\nu}{x^\mu}=\delta^{\nu}_{\mu}.

(7.B.9)

An arbitrary dual vector
\omega
is then written as

\omega=\omega_{\mu}\differential x^{\mu},

(7.B.10)

where the
\omega_{\mu}
are the components of
\omega
.

The notion of a dual vector allows us to define the inner product. For instance, take a vector
V=V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
and a dual vector
\omega=\omega_{\mu}\differential x^{\mu}
. The
inner-product

\langle\,,\rangle:T_{p}^{*}M\times T_{p}M\to\mathbb{R}
is obtained by

\langle\omega,V\rangle=\omega_{\mu}V^{\nu}\left\langle\differential x^{\mu},\partialderivative{x^\nu}\right\rangle=\omega_{\mu}V^{\nu}\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}=\omega_{\mu}V^{\mu}\equiv\omega(V).

(7.B.11)

Next, let
p\in O_{i}\cap O_{j}
and
\phi_{i}(p)
and
\phi_{j}(p)
be the two coordinate functions represented by the set of coordinates
x^{\mu}
and
x^{\mu^{\prime}}
, respectively. We thus have

\omega=\omega_{\mu}\differential x^{\mu}=\omega_{\mu^{\prime}}\differential x^{\mu^{\prime}}.

(7.B.12)

From the fact that
\differential x^{\mu^{\prime}}=\quantity(\pdv*{x^{\mu'}}{x^\mu})\differential x^{\mu}
we can write down the transformation law for the components of the dual vector
\omega
as

\omega_{\mu^{\prime}}=\partialderivative{x^{\mu}}{x^{\mu'}}\omega_{\mu}.

(7.B.13)

At last, a
tensor
of type
(q,r)
is a multilinear object which maps
q
elements of
T_{p}^{*}M
and
r
elements of
T_{p}M
to a real number,

T:\quantity[\times^qT_p^*M]\quantity[\times^rT_pM]\to\mathbb{R},

(7.B.14)

where the symbol
\times^{q}T_{p}^{*}M
means the Cartesian product of the space
T_{p}^{*}M

q
times, with a similar definition for the vector space
T_{p}M
. The set of all tensors of type
(q,r)
at
p\in M
is denoted by
T^{q}_{r,p}(M)
. The components of the tensor
T\in T^{q}_{r,p}(M)
in a coordinate basis can be obtained by acting the tensor on basis dual vectors and vectors,

{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}=T(\differential x^{\mu_{1}},\dots,\differential x^{\mu_{q}},\partial_{\nu_{1}},\dots,\partial_{\nu_{r}}).

(7.B.15)

This is equivalent to the expansion

T={T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}\partial_{\mu_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\partial_{\mu_{q}}\otimes\differential x^{\nu_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\differential x^{\nu_{r}},

(7.B.16)

where
\otimes
denotes the tensor product. Now let
V_{i}=V_{i}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
(
1\leq i\leq r
) and
\omega_{i}=\omega_{i\mu}\differential x^{\mu}
(
1\leq i\leq q
). The action of
T
on them results in the number

T(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{q};V_{1},\dots,V_{r})={T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}\omega_{1\mu_{1}}\dots\omega_{q\mu_{q}}V_{1}^{\nu_{1}}\dots V_{r}^{\nu_{r}}.

(7.B.17)

If
p\in O_{i}\cap O_{j}
and
\phi_{i}(p)
and
\phi_{j}(p)
are the two coordinate functions represented by the set of coordinates
x^{\mu}
and
x^{\mu^{\prime}}
, respectively, the components of the tensor
T
change as

{T^{\mu_{1}^{\prime}\dots\mu_{q}^{\prime}}}_{\nu_{1}^{\prime}\dots\nu_{r}^{\prime}}=\partialderivative{x^{\mu_1'}}{x^{\mu_1}}\dots\partialderivative{x^{\mu_q'}}{x^{\mu_q}}\partialderivative{x^{\nu_1}}{x^{\nu_1'}}\dots\partialderivative{x^{\nu_r}}{x^{\nu_r'}}\,{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}},

(7.B.18)

which is the general transformation law for tensors.

We define a
tensor field
of type
(q,r)
by the smooth assignment of an element of
T^{q}_{r,p}(M)
to each point
p\in M
. The set of all tensor fields of type
(q,r)
on
M
is denoted by
T_{r}^{q}(M)
. For example,
T_{1}^{0}(M)
is the set of the dual vector fields.

Since the tensors themselves are objects that are independent of the coordinate system, it is useful (although not strictly mandatory) to express physics laws in terms of tensor equations. But the laws of physics usually involve derivatives, and, unfortunately, the partial derivative of a tensor is not, in general, a new tensor, as we can see by considering, for example, the partial derivative of a dual vector,
\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}
, and changing to a new coordinate system:

\displaystyle\partialderivative{x^{\mu'}}\omega_{\nu^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\partialderivative{x^\mu}{x^{\mu'}}\partialderivative{x^\mu}\quantity(\pdv{x^\nu}{x^{\nu'}}\omega_\nu)

\displaystyle=\partialderivative{x^\mu}{x^{\mu'}}\partialderivative{x^\nu}{x^{\nu'}}\quantity(\pdv{x^\mu}\omega_\nu)+\omega_{\nu}\partialderivative{x^\mu}{x^{\mu'}}\partialderivative{x^\mu}\quantity(\pdv{x^\nu}{x^{\nu'}}).

(7.B.19)

The second term should not be there if
\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}
were to transform as a
(0,2)
tensor. Therefore we need to introduce a coordinate independent derivative operator and, to do so, we first need to see how tensor fields are carried along by maps between manifolds.

Appendix 7.C
Maps between manifolds

Consider two manifolds
M
and
N
, with dimensions
m
and
n
, and coordinate systems
x^{\mu}
and
y^{\alpha}
, respectively. Consider the map
\phi:M\to N
and a function
f:N\to\mathbb{R}
. We define the
pullback
of
f
by
\phi
, denoted
\phi^{*}f
, by

\phi^{*}f=\quantity(f\circ\phi):M\to\mathbb{R}.

(7.C.1)

We think of
\phi^{*}
as "pulling back" the function
f
from
N
to
M
(Figure
16
).

Figure 16:
Pullback of a function
f
by a map
\phi:M\to N
.

If
V(p)
is a vector at a point
p
on
M
, we define the
pushforward
vector
\phi_{*}V
at the point
\phi(p)
on
N
by giving its action on functions on
N
,

\quantity(\phi_*V)(f)=V(\phi^{*}f).

(7.C.2)

Basically, the action of
\phi_{*}V
on the function
f
is simply the action of
V
on
\phi^{*}f
.

Since a basis for vectors on
M
is given by the set of partial derivatives
\partial_{\mu}=\partialderivative*{x^\mu}
and a basis on
N
is given by the set of partial derivatives
\partial_{\alpha}=\partialderivative*{y^\alpha}
, we can relate the components of
V=V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
to those of
(\phi_{*}V)=(\phi_{*}V)^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}
by applying the pushed-forward vector to a test function and using the chain rule of partial derivatives,

(\phi_{*}V)^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}f=V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}(\phi^{*}f)=V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}(f\circ\phi)=V^{\mu}\partialderivative{y^\alpha}{x^\mu}\partial_{\alpha}f.

(7.C.3)

Thus the pushforward operation
\phi_{*}
can be seen as a matrix operator,
(\phi_{*}V)^{\alpha}={\quantity(\phi_*)^{\alpha}}_{\mu}V^{\mu}
, with the matrix being given by

{\quantity(\phi_*)^{\alpha}}_{\mu}=\partialderivative{y^\alpha}{x^\mu}.

(7.C.4)

The pullback
\phi^{*}\omega
of a dual vector
\omega
on
N
is defined by its action on a vector
V
on
M
: it is equal to the action of
\omega
on the pushforward of
V
,

(\phi^{*}\omega)(V)=\omega(\phi_{*}V).

(7.C.5)

From this expression, we have

(\phi^{*}\omega)(V)=\omega_{\alpha}(\phi_{*}V)^{\alpha}=\omega_{\alpha}{(\phi_{*})^{\alpha}}_{\mu}V^{\mu}={(\phi_{*})_{\mu}}^{\alpha}\omega_{\alpha}V^{\mu}.

(7.C.6)

where
{(\phi_{*})_{\mu}}^{\alpha}=\partialderivative*{y^\alpha}{x^\mu}
. It is the same matrix as the pushforward (
7.C.4
) but with a different index being contracted when the matrix acts to pull back dual vectors.

We can pull back tensors with an arbitrary number of lower indices. Since a
(0,r)
tensor is a linear map from the direct product of
r
vectors to
\mathbb{R}
, we define the pullback of such a tensor by the action of the original tensor on the pushed-forward vectors,

(\phi^{*}T)(V^{(1)},\dots,V^{(r)})=T(\phi_{*}V^{(1)},\dots,\phi_{*}V^{(r)}),

(7.C.7)

where
T_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{r}}
is a
(0,r)
tensor on
N
. We can similarly push forward any
(q,0)
tensor
S^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}
on
M
by acting it on pulled-back dual vectors,

(\phi_{*}S)(\omega^{(1)},\dots,\omega^{(q)})=S(\phi^{*}\omega^{(1)},\dots,\phi^{*}\omega^{(q)}).

(7.C.8)

For the matrix representation of the pullback of a
(0,r)
tensor we have

(\phi^{*}T)_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{r}}=\partialderivative{y^{\alpha_1}}{x^{\mu_1}}\dots\partialderivative{y^{\alpha_r}}{x^{\mu_r}}T_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{r}},

(7.C.9)

while for the pushforward of a
(q,0)
tensor,

(\phi_{*}S)^{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{q}}=\partialderivative{y^{\alpha_1}}{x^{\mu_1}}\dots\partialderivative{y^{\alpha_q}}{x^{\mu_q}}S^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}.

(7.C.10)

In general, tensors with both upper and lower indices can be neither pushed forward nor pulled back since the matrix
\partialderivative*{y^\alpha}{x^\mu}
might not be invertible. If it is invertible, however, then
\phi
defines a diffeomorphism between
M
and
N
, allowing us to define the pushforward and pullback of arbitrary tensors. Specifically, for a
(q,r)
tensor field
{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}
on
M
, we define the pushforward by

\displaystyle(\phi_{*}T)(\omega^{(1)},\dots,\omega^{(q)},V^{(1)},\dots,V^{(r)})
