\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=T(\phi^{*}\omega^{(1)},\dots,\phi^{*}\omega^{(q)},[\phi^{-1}]_{*}V^{(1)},\dots,[\phi^{-1}]_{*}V^{(r)}).

(7.C.11)

In components, this becomes

{(\phi_{*}T)^{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{q}}}_{\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{r}}=\partialderivative{y^{\alpha_1}}{x^{\mu_1}}\dots\partialderivative{y^{\alpha_q}}{x^{\mu_q}}\partialderivative{x^{\nu_1}}{y^{\beta_1}}\dots\partialderivative{x^{\nu_r}}{y^{\beta_r}}\,{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}.

(7.C.12)

Note the resemblance to the tensor transformation law between coordinate systems, Eq. (
7.B.18
). It is for this reason that we can think of a diffeomorphism as an "active" coordinate transformation.

A diffeomorphism also provides a way of comparing tensors at different points on a manifold, which we can use to define a way of computing derivatives of tensors.

Appendix 7.D
Lie derivatives

Given a diffeomorphism
\phi:M\to M
and a tensor field
{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}(x)
we can define the difference between the value of the tensor at some point
p
and its value at
\phi(p)
pulled back to
p
,
\phi^{*}[{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}(\phi(p))]
. Therefore we can define some kind of derivative operator on tensor fields, which would require a one-parameter family of diffeomorphisms,
\phi_{t}
. This family can be thought of as a smooth map
\mathbb{R}\times M\to M
, such that for each
t\in\mathbb{R}
we have a diffeomorphism
\phi_{t}
, satisfying

\phi_{s}\circ\phi_{t}=\phi_{s+t}.

(7.D.1)

Note that this condition implies that
\phi_{0}
is the identity map.

With the entire family
\phi_{t}
, a point
p
describes a curve in
M
such that these curves for all points cover the entire manifold. We can then define a vector field
V^{\mu}(x)
to be the set of tangent vectors to each of these curves at every point, evaluated at
t=0
. This entire line of thought can be reversed, however, and it will be useful to think of the one-parameter family of diffeomorphisms as being defined by the vector field itself. Explicitly, given an arbitrary
V^{\mu}(x)
, we define its
integral curves
to be those curves
x^{\mu}(t)
that solve

\derivative{x^\mu}{t}=V^{\mu}.

(7.D.2)

The vector field
V^{\mu}(x)
is referred to as the
generator
of the diffeomorphism.

For a given a vector field
V^{\mu}(x)
, then, we have a family of diffeomorphisms parametrized by
t
, and we can ask how fast a tensor changes along the integral curves. For each
t
we can define this change as the difference between the pullback of the tensor to
p
and its original value at
p
,

\Delta_{t}{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}(p)=\phi_{t}^{*}[{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}(\phi_{t}(p))]-{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}(p).

(7.D.3)

Note that both terms on the right-hand side are tensors at
p
. We then define the
Lie derivative
of the tensor along the vector field as

\mathcal{L}_{V}{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}=\lim_{t\to 0}\quantity(\frac{\Delta_t{T^{\mu_1\dots\mu_q}}_{\nu_1\dots\nu_r}}{t}).

(7.D.4)

The Lie derivative is a map from
(q,r)
tensor fields to
(q,r)
tensor fields, which is manifestly independent of coordinates. It is linear,

\mathcal{L}_{V}(aT+bS)=a\mathcal{L}_{V}T+b\mathcal{L}_{V}S,

(7.D.5a)

and obeys the Leibniz rule,

\mathcal{L}_{V}(T\otimes S)=(\mathcal{L}_{V}T)\otimes S+T\otimes(\mathcal{L}_{V}S),

(7.D.5b)

where
S
and
T
are tensors and
a
and
b
are constants. Also, the Lie derivative reduces to the ordinary directional derivative when acting on functions,

\mathcal{L}_{V}f=\derivative{f}{t}=\derivative{x^\mu}{t}\partial_{\mu}f=V^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}f=V(f).

(7.D.5c)

The Lie derivative of a vector field
U(x)
with respect to
V(x)
can be shown to be

\mathcal{L}_{V}U^{\mu}=\commutator{V}{U}^{\mu}=V^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}U^{\mu}-U^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}V^{\mu}.

(7.D.6)

The commutator is called the
Lie bracket
. Note that
\mathcal{L}_{V}U=-\mathcal{L}_{U}V
.

Finally, in terms of components in a given coordinate system, the Lie derivative of an arbitrary tensor field is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{V}{T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}

\displaystyle=V^{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}{T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}

\displaystyle-(\partial_{\lambda}V^{\mu_{1}}){T^{\lambda\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}-(\partial_{\lambda}V^{\mu_{2}}){T^{\mu_{1}\lambda\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}-\dots

\displaystyle+(\partial_{\nu_{1}}V^{\lambda}){T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\lambda\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}+(\partial_{\nu_{2}}V^{\lambda}){T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\lambda\dots\nu_{r}}+\dots\,.

(7.D.7)

Although not manifestly tensorial, this expression can be shown to be covariant (see Section
7.F
).

The Lie derivative is a successful result to our quest of obtaining a coordinate independent derivative operator. However, it can be argued that this operator lacks some generality since it depends on the specification of the vector field. It will then be useful to define another derivative operator, namely the covariant derivative. But before we do that, let us pause a moment to discuss an extremely important tensor in differential geometry, the metric tensor.

Appendix 7.E
The metric tensor

The metric tensor is the mathematical object that provides the notion of the length of a path in a manifold, which we also call the line element

\differential s^{2}=g_{\mu\nu}\differential x^{\mu}\otimes\differential x^{\nu}=g_{\mu\nu}\differential x^{\mu}\differential x^{\nu}.

(7.E.1)

We may also use the metric to denote the inner product of two vectors
V^{\mu}
and
W^{\nu}
as

\differential s^{2}(V,W)=g_{\mu\nu}V^{\mu}W^{\nu}=V_{\nu}W^{\nu}.

(7.E.2)

We refer to two vectors whose inner product vanishes as
orthogonal
, and the inner product of a vector with itself is called its
norm
.

The metric is a symmetric tensor, by which we mean that
g_{\mu\nu}=g_{\nu\mu}
. By imposing that the determinant of the metric does not vanish,
\det(g_{\mu\nu})\equiv g\neq 0
, we can properly define the inverse of the metric,
g^{\mu\nu}
, such that

g^{\mu\nu}g_{\nu\sigma}=g_{\lambda\sigma}g^{\lambda\mu}=\delta^{\mu}_{\sigma}.

(7.E.3)

The symmetry of
g_{\mu\nu}
implies that
g^{\mu\nu}
is also symmetric. We can use the inverse of the metric to denote the inner product of two dual vectors
\omega_{\mu}
and
\zeta_{\nu}
as

\differential s^{2}(\omega,\zeta)=g^{\mu\nu}\omega_{\mu}\zeta_{\nu}=\omega^{\nu}\zeta_{\nu}.

(7.E.4)

Eqs. (
7.E.2
) and (
7.E.4
) illustrate what we usually say that the metric is used to raise and lower indices.

The metric can be characterized by putting
g_{\mu\nu}
into its
canonical form
. In this form the metric components become

g_{\mu\nu}=\textrm{diag}\,\quantity(-1,-1,\dots,-1,+1,+1,\dots,+1,0,\dots,0).

(7.E.5)

The
signature
of the metric refer to the number of both positive and negative eigenvalues; we speak of "a metric with signature minus-plus-plus-plus" for Minkowski space, for example, for which

g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}=\textrm{diag}\,\quantity(-1,+1,+1,+1)

(7.E.6)

for the entire manifold (in Cartesian coordinates). If any of the eigenvalues are zero, the metric is degenerate, and the inverse metric will not exist; if the metric is continuous and nondegenerate, its signature will be the same at every point. If all of the signs are positive, the metric is called
Euclidean
or
Riemannian
(or just positive definite), while if there is a single minus it is called
Lorentzian
or
pseudo-Riemannian
, and any metric with some
+1
’s and some
-1
’s is called indefinite.

For a Lorentzian metric, the norm of a vector is not positive definite. We say
V^{\mu}
is
timelike
if
g_{\mu\nu}V^{\mu}V^{\nu}<0
,
lightlike
or
null
if
g_{\mu\nu}V^{\mu}V^{\nu}=0
and
spacelike
if
g_{\mu\nu}V^{\mu}V^{\nu}>0
.

At a single point
p
on the manifold, one may start with any set of components for
g_{\mu\nu}
, diagonalize the matrix and then rescale the basis vectors such that the metric components satisfy (
7.E.5
). Minkowski spacetime is just a four dimensional manifold with a Lorentzian metric such that this can be done globally for a single coordinate system, namely the Cartesian one. We say it is a flat manifold (or flat spacetime in a more physical context). This is not true for a general metric, however. At a single point one can always choose coordinates in which the metric tensor looks flat, but this has to be done in different coordinate systems for different points, in general. We say that such spacetime is curved. It will then be interesting to learn how we can extract the curvature information of the metric tensor. We start in the next section with the notion of covariant derivatives, which tells us how to parallel transport vectors along curves in a coordinate independent way that accounts for the curvature of the manifold.

Appendix 7.F
Covariant derivatives

We define the
covariant derivative

\nabla
as a map from
(q,r)
tensor fields to
(q,r+1)
tensor fields which has the following properties:

1.

(linearity)
\nabla(T+S)=\nabla T+\nabla S
;

2.

(Leibniz rule)
\nabla(T\otimes S)=(\nabla T)\otimes S+T\otimes(\nabla S)
;

3.

(commutativity with contractions)
\nabla_{\mu}({T^{\lambda}}_{\lambda\rho})={{\quantity(\nabla T)_{\mu}}^{\lambda}}_{\lambda\rho}
;

4.

(reduction to the partial derivatives on scalars)
\nabla_{\mu}\phi=\partial_{\mu}\phi
.

Here
\phi
is a scalar field and
T
and
S
are arbitrary rank tensor fields. For the covariant derivative of a vector
V^{\mu}
, the general expression that can be easily shown to satisfy all requirements above is

\nabla_{\mu}V^{\nu}=\partial_{\mu}V^{\nu}+\Gamma_{\mu\lambda}^{\nu}V^{\lambda},

(7.F.1)

where the set of
n
matrices
{(\Gamma_{\mu})^{\nu}}_{\lambda}
(one
n\times n
matrix for each
\mu
, where
n
is the dimension of the manifold) are known as the
connection coefficients
. The second term on the right-hand side of Eq. (
7.F.1
) can be thought as being a correction to the partial derivative of a vector due to the curvature of the manifold. We can determine the transformation properties of
\Gamma_{\mu\lambda}^{\nu}
by demanding that the left-hand side of Eq. (
7.F.1
) be a
(1,1)
tensor. In doing so, one finds that, in a new coordinate system,

\Gamma_{\mu^{\prime}\lambda^{\prime}}^{\nu^{\prime}}=\partialderivative{x^\mu}{x^{\mu'}}\partialderivative{x^{\nu'}}{x^\nu}\partialderivative{x^\lambda}{x^{\lambda'}}\Gamma_{\mu\lambda}^{\nu}-\partialderivative{x^\mu}{x^{\mu'}}\partialderivative{x^\lambda}{x^{\lambda'}}\partialderivative{x^{\nu'}}{x^\mu}{x^\lambda}.

(7.F.2)

This is clearly not the tensor transformation law, so the connection coefficients are not really tensors.

By a similar reasoning to that used for vectors, the covariant derivative of a dual vector
\omega_{\mu}
can also be expressed as a partial derivative plus some linear transformation. In order to find out what this linear transformation is, we apply the required properties of the covariant derivative of the scalar
\omega_{\mu}V^{\mu}
, which then tell us that the covariant derivative of
\omega_{\mu}
must be of the form

\nabla_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\omega_{\nu}-\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\omega_{\lambda}.

(7.F.3)

The generalization to the covariant derivative of a tensor of arbitrary rank is

\begin{split}\nabla_{\sigma}{T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}&=\partial_{\sigma}{T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+\Gamma_{\sigma\lambda}^{\mu_{1}}{T^{\lambda\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}+\Gamma_{\sigma\lambda}^{\mu_{2}}{T^{\mu_{1}\lambda\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}+\dots\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,-\Gamma_{\sigma\nu_{1}}^{\lambda}{T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\lambda\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}-\Gamma_{\sigma\nu_{2}}^{\lambda}{T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\lambda\dots\nu_{r}}-\dots\,.\end{split}

(7.F.4)

In order to define a covariant derivative we need to put a connection on our manifold, which is specified in some coordinate system by a set of coefficients
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
. This specification is generally not unique. In general relativity, however, we use a special kind of connection coefficients that emerge when we require two more properties:

•

(torsion-free)
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\Gamma_{\nu\mu}^{\lambda}
;

•

(metric compatibility)
\nabla_{\rho}g_{\mu\nu}=0
.

Given a metric
g_{\mu\nu}
, there exists a unique derivative operator compatible with it
[
159
]
, for which

\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\sigma}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\sigma\rho}\quantity(\partial_\mu g_{\nu\rho}+\partial_\nu g_{\rho\mu}-\partial_\rho g_{\mu\nu}).

(7.F.5)

The connection given by Eq. (
7.F.5
) is known as the
Christoffel connection
, and the associated connection coefficients are called
Christoffel symbols
.

More generally, let
\nabla^{(1)}_{\mu}
and
\nabla^{(2)}_{\mu}
be two distinct covariant derivatives that are compatible with the distinct metric tensors
g^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}
and
g^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}
, respectively. Then, the action of
\nabla^{(1)}_{\mu}
on an arbitrary tensor field in terms of
\nabla^{(2)}_{\mu}
and a tensor field
{C^{\sigma}}_{\mu\nu}
can be written as
[
159
]

\begin{split}\nabla^{(1)}_{\sigma}{T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}&=\nabla^{(2)}_{\sigma}{T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,+{C^{\mu_{1}}}_{\sigma\lambda}{T^{\lambda\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}+{C^{\mu_{2}}}_{\sigma\lambda}{T^{\mu_{1}\lambda\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}+\dots\\
&\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,-{C^{\lambda}}_{\sigma\nu_{1}}{T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\lambda\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}-{C^{\lambda}}_{\sigma\nu_{2}}{T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\lambda\dots\nu_{r}}-\dots\,.\end{split}

(7.F.6)

Metric compatibility implies that

{C^{\sigma}}_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}g^{\sigma\rho}_{(1)}\quantity(\nabla^{(2)}_\mu g^{(1)}_{\nu\rho}+\nabla^{(2)}_\nu g^{(1)}_{\rho\mu}-\nabla^{(2)}_\rho g^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}).

(7.F.7)

We can use the covariant derivative in order to obtain a manifestly covariant expression for the action of the Lie derivative. First, note that the Lie Bracket of vector fields
V^{\mu}(x)
and
U^{\mu}(x)
can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{V}U^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\commutator{V}{U}^{\mu}=V^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}U^{\mu}-U^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}V^{\mu}

\displaystyle=V^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}U^{\mu}-U^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}V^{\mu}.

(7.F.8)

This holds true for any torsion-free covariant derivative since the commutator involves an antisymmetrization which cancels the nontensorial piece of the partial derivative transformation law. We can then write the action of the Lie derivative on tensors, Eq. (
7.D
), in a manifestly covariant form,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{V}{T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}

\displaystyle=V^{\sigma}\nabla_{\sigma}{T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{\lambda}V^{\mu_{1}}){T^{\lambda\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}-(\nabla_{\lambda}V^{\mu_{2}}){T^{\mu_{1}\lambda\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}-\dots

\displaystyle+(\nabla_{\nu_{1}}V^{\lambda}){T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\lambda\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}+(\nabla_{\nu_{2}}V^{\lambda}){T^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\lambda\dots\nu_{r}}+\dots\,.

(7.F.9)

The Lie derivative of the metric tensor, for instance, reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{V}g_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=V^{\sigma}\nabla_{\sigma}g_{\mu\nu}+(\nabla_{\mu}V^{\lambda})g_{\lambda\nu}+(\nabla_{\nu}V^{\lambda})g_{\mu\lambda}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mu}V_{\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}V_{\mu}.

(7.F.10)

This expression is particularly useful for studying gauge invariance of linearized gravity.

With the proper definition of a derivative operator at hand, we are now ready to introduce the idea of parallel transport of tensors, which shall lead to the ultimate notion of curvature in a manifold.

Appendix 7.G
Parallel transport and geodesics

Let us start with the simplest non trivial example of a tensor field, namely the vector field. We may be tempted to compare and compute operations between vectors that are defined at different points, as we usually do in basic "flat-space physics". However, vectors that are defined at different points belong to different vector spaces. This is not a problem if spacetime is flat because we can simply transport vectors to the same point, so that they belong to the same tangent space, and then compare one with the other there. This is what we call a parallel transport, which basically means that we are moving the vector while keeping it "constant". Parallel transport can also be defined for curved manifolds, but the problem is that, in general, the end result of a tensor being parallel transported along a path will depend on our choice of the curve itself.

Given a curve
x^{\mu}(\lambda)
, the requirement of constancy of a tensor
{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}
along this curve in flat space reads

\derivative{\lambda}\,{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}=\derivative{x^\mu}{\lambda}\partialderivative{x^\mu}\,{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}=0.

(7.G.1)

In a generic manifold we need to replace this partial derivative by a covariant one. We then define the
directional covariant derivative
to be

\frac{\rm D}{\textrm{d}\lambda}=\derivative{x^\mu}{\lambda}\nabla_{\mu}.

(7.G.2)

This is a map, defined only along the path, from
(q,r)
tensors to
(q,r)
tensors. The
parallel transport
of the tensor
T
along the path
x^{\mu}(\lambda)
is defined via

{\quantity(\frac{\rm D}{\textrm{d}\lambda}T)^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}\equiv\derivative{x^\sigma}{\lambda}\nabla_{\sigma}{T^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}=0.

(7.G.3)

This is a tensor equation known as the
equation of parallel transport
. For a vector it takes the form

\derivative{\lambda}V^{\mu}+\Gamma_{\sigma\rho}^{\mu}\derivative{x^\sigma}{\lambda}V^{\rho}=0.

(7.G.4)

The idea of parallel transport gives us a way to interpret the covariant derivative of tensors: it quantifies the instantaneous rate of change of a tensor field in comparison to what the tensor would be if it were parallel transported
[
35
]
.

Parallel transport depends on the connection, since different connections define different covariant derivatives that lead to different ways on how to transport a tensor. Here we stick with metric-compatible connections, for which the metric is always parallel transported,

\frac{\rm D}{\textrm{d}\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}=\derivative{x^\sigma}{\lambda}\nabla_{\sigma}g_{\mu\nu}=0.

(7.G.5)

It follows that the inner product of two parallel-transported vectors is preserved and parallel transport with respect to a metric-compatible connection preserves the norm of vectors.

A path
x^{\mu}(\lambda)
that parallel-transports its own tangent vector is called a
geodesic
. Such parallel transport condition reads

\derivative[2]{x^\mu}{\lambda}+\Gamma_{\sigma\rho}^{\mu}\derivative{x^\sigma}{\lambda}\derivative{x^\rho}{\lambda}=0.

(7.G.6)

This is the
geodesic equation
. It reproduces the usual notion of straight lines if the connection coefficients are the Christoffel symbols in Euclidean space. In particular, for any given space, if the connections in Eq. (
7.G.6
) are the Christoffel connections, the geodesic is also the shortest distance between two points, by which we mean that it extremizes the length

l=\int\sqrt{g_{\mu\nu}\derivative{x^\mu}{\lambda}\derivative{x^\nu}{\lambda}}\,\differential\lambda

(7.G.7)

Since a curve is said to be timelike or null or spacelike at a point if its tangent vector is timelike or null or spacelike at that point, it follows from metric compatibility that geodesics preserve their own signature. For null curves the length is zero, while for timelike curves we change the sign in the square root above and use the term
proper time
,

\tau=\int\sqrt{-g_{\mu\nu}\derivative{x^\mu}{\lambda}\derivative{x^\nu}{\lambda}}\,\differential\lambda.

(7.G.8)

Sometimes it is useful to specify the curve parameter to be the proper time itself. Then, any transformation of the form
\tau\to\lambda=\alpha\tau+\beta
, for constants
\alpha
and
\beta
, leaves the geodesic equation invariant. Any parameter
\lambda
related to the proper time as specified above is called an
affine parameter
.

Appendix 7.H
The Riemann curvature tensor

We are now ready to introduce our measure of curvature. Let us recall that the covariant derivative of a tensor in a certain direction measures how much the tensor changes relative to what it would have been if it had been parallel transported along that direction. The commutator of two covariant derivatives then measures the difference between parallel transporting the tensor first one way and then the other, versus the opposite ordering (Figure
17
). This difference, which is expected to be non-existent in flat spaces, is thus related to the curvature of the manifold.

Figure 17:
The commutator of two covariant derivatives.

For a vector field
V^{\rho}
, we can compute

\commutator{\nabla_\mu}{\nabla_\nu}V^{\rho}=\nabla_{\mu}\quantity(\nabla_\nu V^\rho)-\nabla_{\nu}\quantity(\nabla_\mu V^\rho).

(7.H.1)

A sequence of algebraic manipulations then leads to

\commutator{\nabla_\mu}{\nabla_\nu}V^{\rho}={R^{\rho}}_{\sigma\mu\nu}V^{\sigma},

(7.H.2)

where we have defined the
Riemann tensor
as

{R^{\rho}}_{\sigma\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\Gamma_{\nu\sigma}^{\rho}-\partial_{\nu}\Gamma_{\mu\sigma}^{\rho}+\Gamma_{\mu\lambda}^{\rho}\Gamma_{\nu\sigma}^{\lambda}-\Gamma_{\nu\lambda}^{\rho}\Gamma_{\mu\sigma}^{\lambda}.

(7.H.3)

This can also be shown to be related to the change experienced by a vector when parallel transported around a closed loop
[
159
]
.

In general, the action of
\commutator{\nabla_\rho}{\nabla_\sigma}
can be computed on a tensor of arbitrary rank as

\displaystyle\commutator{\nabla_\rho}{\nabla_\sigma}{X^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}

\displaystyle={R^{\mu_{1}}}_{\lambda\rho\sigma}{X^{\lambda\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}+{R^{\mu_{2}}}_{\lambda\rho\sigma}{X^{\mu_{1}\lambda\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{r}}+\dots

\displaystyle-{R^{\lambda}}_{\nu_{1}\rho\sigma}{X^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\lambda\nu_{2}\dots\nu_{r}}-{R^{\lambda}}_{\nu_{2}\rho\sigma}{X^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{q}}}_{\nu_{1}\lambda\dots\nu_{r}}-\dots\,.

(7.H.4)

The Riemann tensor satisfies the following properties:

1.

R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}=-R_{\sigma\rho\mu\nu}
,

2.

R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}=-R_{\rho\sigma\nu\mu}
,

3.

R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
,

4.

R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}+R_{\rho\mu\nu\sigma}+R_{\rho\nu\sigma\mu}=0
,

where
R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}=g_{\rho\lambda}{R^{\lambda}}_{\sigma\mu\nu}
. In addition, it also obeys a very important differential identity known as
Bianchi identity
:

\nabla_{\lambda}R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}+\nabla_{\rho}R_{\sigma\lambda\mu\nu}+\nabla_{\sigma}R_{\lambda\rho\mu\nu}=0.

(7.H.5)

There are some other tensors that are useful in general relativity which are obtained from the Riemann tensor. For instance, we can take a contraction to form the
Ricci tensor
:

R_{\mu\nu}={R^{\lambda}}_{\mu\lambda\nu},

(7.H.6)

which is symmetric. The trace of the Ricci tensor is what we call the
Ricci scalar
or the
curvature scalar
:

R={R^{\mu}}_{\mu}=g^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}.

(7.H.7)

Now, by contracting twice on the Bianchi identity (
7.H.5
), we can write

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=g^{\nu\sigma}g^{\mu\lambda}\quantity(\nabla_\lambda R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}+\nabla_\rho R_{\sigma\lambda\mu\nu}+\nabla_\sigma R_{\lambda\rho\mu\nu})

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\mu}R_{\rho\mu}-\nabla_{\rho}R+\nabla^{\nu}R_{\rho\nu},

(7.H.8)

or

\nabla^{\mu}R_{\rho\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\rho}R.

(7.H.9)

This motivates us to define the
Einstein tensor
as

G_{\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}Rg_{\mu\nu},

(7.H.10)

so that the twice-contracted Bianchi identity (
7.H.9
) becomes equivalent to

\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=0.

(7.H.11)

This means that the Einstein tensor, fundamentally related to the curvature of the manifold, is conserved.

Appendix 7.I
Geodesic deviation

Let us now address another important manifestation of curvature, namely the violation of Euclid’s fifth postulate, which basically states that initially parallel lines remain parallel through the entire space. This is not true in a curved space and, since the generalization of a straight line is a geodesic, this violation is quantified by the geodesic deviation equation.

Consider a one-parameter family of geodesics,
\gamma_{s}(t)
, that is, for each
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
\gamma_{s}
is a geodesic parametrized by the parameter
t
. The collection of these curves defines a smooth two-dimensional surface on which the coordinates may be chosen to be
s
and
t
, provided we have chosen a family of geodesics that do not cross (Figure
18
). The entire surface is the set of points
x^{\mu}(s,t)\in M
. Let us then consider two vector fields: the tangent vectors to the geodesics,

T^{\mu}=\partialderivative{x^\mu}{t},

(7.I.1)

and the deviation vectors

S^{\mu}=\partialderivative{x^\mu}{s}.

(7.I.2)

Figure 18:
A set of geodesics
\gamma_{s}(t)
with tangent vectors
T^{\mu}
and deviation vectors
S^{\mu}
.

We define the relative velocity of geodesics by

V^{\mu}=T^{\rho}\nabla_{\rho}S^{\mu},

(7.I.3)

and the relative acceleration of geodesics by

A^{\mu}=T^{\rho}\nabla_{\rho}V^{\mu}.

(7.I.4)

Since
S
and
T
are basis vectors adapted to a coordinate system, their commutator vanishes
[
35
]
, which means that

S^{\rho}\nabla_{\rho}T^{\mu}=T^{\rho}\nabla_{\rho}S^{\mu}.

(7.I.5)

A direct computation then leads to

A^{\mu}=\frac{\textrm{D}^{2}}{\textrm{d}t^{2}}S^{\mu}={R^{\mu}}_{\nu\rho\sigma}T^{\nu}T^{\rho}S^{\sigma},

(7.I.6)

which is known as the
geodesic deviation equation
. It shows that the relative acceleration between two neighboring geodesics is proportional to the curvature.

Appendix 7.J
Locally inertial coordinates

Having discussed the curvature in a differentiable manifold, let us now show how to construct a set of coordinates in a small region that looks locally flat. In general relativity this is linked to the equivalence principle which, loosely speaking, concerns the impossibility of detecting the spacetime curvature by means of local experiments
[
35
]
. Here we show how to construct such reference frame at a point in the manifold. In the next section we show how we can do the same along an entire curve for small enough regions.

For a given point
p\in M
we can define a locally flat set of coordinates for a small region that contains
p
, which we call
locally inertial coordinates
. In order to construct such a coordinate system, let
k^{\mu}
be a tangent vector at
p
for which
\lambda(p)=0
,

k^{\mu}=\evaluated{\dv{x^\mu}{\lambda}}_{\lambda=0}.

(7.J.1)

This tangent vector then defines a geodesic passing through
p
. Since we are specifying the initial conditions,
x^{\mu}(\lambda=0)
and
k^{\mu}(\lambda=0)
, and the geodesic equation is a second-order differential equation, this geodesic is unique. Specifically, there will be a unique point in
M
for which
\lambda=1
. We then define the
exponential map
at
p
,
\textrm{exp}_{p}:T_{p}M\to M
, as

\exp_{p}(k)=x^{\nu}(\lambda=1),

(7.J.2)

where
x^{\nu}(\lambda)
solves the geodesic equation subject to the initial condition (
7.J.1
).

Since different geodesics emanating from a single point may eventually cross, the exponential map is not necessarily one-to-one. However, it is possible to show that there always exists a sufficiently small neighborhood of
p
on which the exponential map is one-to-one
[
47
]
. This fact allows us to use the exponential map to construct locally inertial coordinates.

We have already argued how, at any given point
p\in M
, we can find basis vectors
\quantity{\vu{e}_{\mu}}
for
T_{p}M
such that the components of the metric are those of the canonical form,

g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu},

(7.J.3)

at
p
. Our next task is to find a coordinate system
x^{\mu}
for which the basis vectors
\quantity{\vu{e}_{\mu}}
stand for a coordinate basis,
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}
, and such that
\partial_{\sigma}g_{\mu\nu}=0
. This is accomplished by considering a point
q\in M
sufficiently close to
p
and defining the coordinates
x^{\mu}(q)
to be the components of the tangent vector
k=k^{\mu}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{\mu}
that gets mapped to
q
by the exponential map
\exp_{p}
. These coordinates are known as
Riemann normal coordinates
at
p
.

Now a parametrized set of vectors of the form
\lambda k^{\mu}
, for some fixed vector
k^{\mu}
, gets mapped to points in a geodesic by the exponential map. Therefore, in Riemann normal coordinates, any geodesic through
p
may be expressed as

x^{\mu}(\lambda)=\lambda k^{\mu},

(7.J.4)

for which we have

\derivative[2]{x^{\mu}}{\lambda}=0

(7.J.5)

along any geodesic through
p
in this coordinate system. The geodesic equation (
7.G.6
) then implies

\Gamma_{\rho\sigma}^{\mu}(p)=0,

(7.J.6)

from which metric compatibility yields

\partial_{\sigma}g_{\mu\nu}=0

(7.J.7)

at
p
. This result, together with (
7.J.3
), constitute the desired properties of locally inertial coordinates.

Appendix 7.K
Fermi normal coordinates

Now that we have seen how to construct locally inertial coordinates in a small neighborhood of a single point, let us show that it is possible to introduce coordinates near any curve in a (pseudo-)Riemannian manifold in such a way that the metric is rectangular along the entire curve. These are called
Fermi normal coordinates
, and here we will be interested in the case where the curve is a geodesic
\gamma
. These coordinates will be constructed in order to satisfy the so-called Fermi conditions,

\evaluated{g_{\mu\nu}}_{\gamma}=\eta_{\mu\nu},

(7.K.1a)

\evaluated{\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^\sigma}_{\gamma}=0,

(7.K.1b)

along the given geodesic. First we will show how such construction is possible, and then we will proceed to write the components of the metric tensor in a neighborhood of the geodesic in Fermi normal coordinates. In this section we follow the development of Ref.
[
107
]
(see also refs.
[
74
,
110
,
166
]
).

7.K.1
Constructing Fermi normal coordinates

We begin by choosing an arbitrary point
p_{0}
in the spacetime manifold to be the origin of our coordinate system and an orthonormal set of vectors
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{0},\dots,\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}
at
p_{0}
to fix the coordinate axes. Now let
\gamma
be the unique timelike geodesic which starts at
p_{0}
with tangent vector
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{0}
at that point. We denote the proper time along it by
t
such that
p_{0}=\gamma(t=0)
. The orthonormal set at the origin is denoted by
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{0}(0),\dots,\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{3}(0)
. Now, we know that the tangent vector to a geodesic at any two points on it is related by parallel transport along
\gamma
. Therefore, the vector
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{0}(t)
will be tangent to the geodesic at any point
p=\gamma(t)
, and similarly the vectors
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}(0)
are parallel transported to get
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}(t)
at
p
. We assume for simplicity that
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{0}
is a timelike vector and that all the
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
’s are spacelike.

Now consider the point
q
, which is not necessarily a point of the geodesic curve
\gamma
, and whose Fermi normal coordinates are denoted by
x^{\mu}
. We construct, at the point
p=\gamma(x^{0}=t)
, the unit vector

\mathbf{v}(x^{0})=v^{i}\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}(x^{0}),\hskip 28.45274ptv^{i}=\frac{x^{i}}{s},

(7.K.2)

with
s=\sqrt{x^{j}x_{j}}
. We can construct a family of spacelike geodesics
\beta(x^{0},v^{i})
orthogonal to
\gamma
at
p
by varying
v^{i}
. Then, a unique geodesic intersects
\gamma
orthogonally at
p
, is tangent to the vector
\mathbf{v}
, and passes through the point
q
at a proper distance
s
,

q=\beta(x^{0},v^{i},s).

(7.K.3)

If the point
q
is represented by its coordinate values
x^{\mu^{\prime}}(q)
in some coordinate system in which the metric components
g_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}(x^{\sigma^{\prime}})
are known, Eq. (
7.K.3
) can be written in such coordinates as

x^{\mu^{\prime}}=x^{\mu^{\prime}}(x^{0},v^{i},s).

(7.K.4)

Since the geodesic equation is unchanged by a re-scaling of the affine parameter
\lambda
, it follows that

x^{\mu^{\prime}}(x^{0},v^{i},s\lambda)=x^{\mu^{\prime}}(x^{0},sv^{i},\lambda).

(7.K.5)

In particular, and recalling that
x^{i}=sv^{i}
, we find

x^{\mu^{\prime}}(x^{0},v^{i},s)=x^{\mu^{\prime}}(x^{0},sv^{i},1)=x^{\mu^{\prime}}(x^{0},x^{i},1)=x^{\mu^{\prime}}(x^{\mu}),

(7.K.6)

which gives the transformation law between the arbitrary coordinates
x^{\mu^{\prime}}
and Fermi normal coordinates
x^{\mu}
.

In the
x^{\mu^{\prime}}
coordinates the tangent vector to the geodesics
\beta(x^{0},v^{i},s)
is

v^{\mu^{\prime}}=\derivative{x^{\mu'}}{s},

(7.K.7)

which calculated along
\gamma
yields

\evaluated{v^{\mu'}}_{\gamma}=\evaluated{\dv{x^{\mu'}}{s}}_{\gamma}=\evaluated{\pdv{x^{\mu'}}{x^i}}_{s=0}\derivative{x^i}{s}=\evaluated{\pdv{x^{\mu'}}{x^i}}_{s=0}v^{i}.

(7.K.8)

A direct comparison between Eqs. (
7.K.2
) and (
7.K.8
) then gives

\quantity(\vu{e}_i)^{\mu^{\prime}}=\evaluated{\pdv{x^{\mu'}}{x^i}}_{\gamma}.

(7.K.9)

Additionally, since the tangent vector to
\gamma
at
p=\gamma(x^{0})
is
\partial_{0}
, we find that, along
\gamma
,

\quantity(\vu{e}_\mu)^{\mu^{\prime}}=\evaluated{\pdv{x^{\mu'}}{x^\mu}}_{\gamma},

(7.K.10)

or
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{\mu}(t)=\evaluated{\partial_\mu}_{\gamma}
.

The relation between the metric components in the Fermi normal coordinates
x^{\mu}
and the components of the metric in the arbitrary coordinates
x^{\mu^{\prime}}
is given by

g_{\mu\nu}=\partialderivative{x^{\mu'}}{x^\mu}\partialderivative{x^{\nu'}}{x^\nu}g_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}.

(7.K.11)

Evaluating it on the geodesic
\gamma
and using Eq. (
7.K.10
), we find

\displaystyle\evaluated{g_{\mu\nu}}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\evaluated{\pdv{x^{\mu'}}{x^\mu}}_{\gamma}\evaluated{\pdv{x^{\nu'}}{x^\nu}}_{\gamma}g_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}=\quantity(\vu{e}_\mu)^{\mu^{\prime}}\quantity(\vu{e}_\nu)^{\nu^{\prime}}g_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}=\quantity(\vu{e}_\mu)^{\mu^{\prime}}\quantity(\vu{e}_\nu)_{\mu^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\mu\nu},

(7.K.12)

since the vectors
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{\mu}
are orthogonal and we chose
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{0}
(
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{i}
) to be timelike (spacelike). Therefore Fermi’s first condition (
7.K.1a
) is satisfied along the geodesic
\gamma
.

Next, recall that the curve
x^{0}=t
,
x^{i}=v^{i}s
satisfies the geodesic equation

\derivative[2]{x^\mu}{s}+\Gamma_{\sigma\rho}^{\mu}\derivative{x^\sigma}{s}\derivative{x^\rho}{s}=0,

(7.K.13)

which implies that

\Gamma_{ij}^{\mu}v^{i}v^{j}=0,

(7.K.14)

where
\Gamma_{ij}^{\mu}
depends on
t
and
x^{i}=v^{i}s
. But along
\gamma
,
s=0
and the dependence on
v^{i}
vanishes. Then, in order to keep Eq. (
7.K.14
) satisfied, we must have

\evaluated{\Gamma_{ij}^\mu}_{\gamma}=0.

(7.K.15)

Now for the other symbols, we recall that the vector
\hat{\mathbf{e}}_{0}(t)
, with components in Fermi normal coordinates given by
\quantity(\vu{e}_0)^{\mu}=\delta_{0}^{\mu}
according to Eq. (
7.K.10
), must satisfy the equation of parallel transport along
\gamma
,

\derivative{t}\quantity(\vu{e}_0)^{\mu}+\Gamma_{\sigma\rho}^{\mu}\derivative{x^\sigma}{t}\quantity(\vu{e}_0)^{\rho}=0\implies\Gamma_{\sigma\rho}^{\mu}\derivative{x^\sigma}{t}\delta_{0}^{\rho}=0,

(7.K.16)

which then implies

\evaluated{\Gamma_{\sigma 0}^\mu}_{\gamma}=\evaluated{\Gamma_{0\sigma}^\mu}_{\gamma}=0.

(7.K.17)

Together, Eqs. (
7.K.15
) and (
7.K.17
) result in Fermi’s second condition,

\evaluated{\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^\sigma}_{\gamma}=0.

(7.K.18)

Therefore, given a geodesic, it is possible to introduce Fermi normal coordinates
x^{\mu}=(t,x^{i})
near it so that the Christoffel symbols vanish along it, with
t
being the geodesic proper time. Also, along
\gamma
,
x^{i}=0
.

7.K.2
Metric components

We are now interested in obtaining an expression for the components of the metric tensor in a neighborhood of the geodesic
\gamma
, written as a power series expansion up to second order in
x^{i}
.

Since
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\sigma}=0
holds for all
x^{0}
at
x^{i}=0
, we may differentiate it with respect to
x^{0}
to obtain

\evaluated{\partial_0\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^\sigma}_{\gamma}=0.

(7.K.19)

From the definition of the Christoffel symbols, this implies

\evaluated{\partial_0\partial_\sigma g_{\mu\nu}}_{\gamma}=0.

(7.K.20)

It follows from Fermi’s conditions (
7.K.1
) that the zeroth order term will be the Minkowski metric while the linear terms vanish. Then, using Eq. (
7.K.20
) we can write

g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\evaluated{\partial_i\partial_jg_{\mu\nu}}_{\gamma}x^{i}x^{j}+O(x^{3}).

(7.K.21)

The Riemann curvature tensor reads

\displaystyle\evaluated{R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\evaluated{g_{{\rho}{\lambda}}\qty(\partial_{{\mu}}\Gamma_{{\nu}{\sigma}}^{{\lambda}}-\partial_{{\nu}}\Gamma_{{\mu}{\sigma}}^{{\lambda}})}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\evaluated{\frac{1}{2}\qty(\partial_{{\mu}}\partial_{{\sigma}}g_{{\rho}{\nu}}-\partial_{{\mu}}\partial_{{\rho}}g_{{\nu}{\sigma}}-\partial_{{\nu}}\partial_{{\sigma}}g_{{\rho}{\mu}}+\partial_{{\nu}}\partial_{{\rho}}g_{{\mu}{\sigma}})}_{\gamma}.

(7.K.22)

From this equation we immediately find

\evaluated{R_{i0j0}}_{\gamma}=-\frac{1}{2}\evaluated{\partial_i\partial_jg_{00}}_{\gamma}.

(7.K.23)

Also, it follows from Eq. (
7.K.19
) that

\evaluated{{R^\rho}_{\sigma\mu 0}}_{\gamma}=\evaluated{\partial_\mu\Gamma_{\sigma 0}^\rho}_{\gamma}.

(7.K.24)

In order to obtain the other second derivatives of the metric, we will need to use the geodesic deviation equation,

\frac{\textrm{D}^{2}}{\textrm{d}s^{2}}n^{\mu}={R^{\mu}}_{\nu\rho\sigma}\derivative{x^\nu}{s}\derivative{x^\rho}{s}n^{\sigma},

(7.K.25)

where we are now denoting
n^{\mu}
as the deviation vector. This equation can be rewritten as

\derivative[2]{s}n^{\mu}+2\Gamma_{\nu\sigma}^{\mu}\derivative{x^\nu}{s}\derivative{s}n^{\sigma}+\quantity(\partial_\nu\Gamma_{\rho\sigma}^\mu-\Gamma_{\lambda\sigma}^\mu\Gamma_{\nu\rho}^\lambda+\Gamma_{\nu\lambda}^\mu\Gamma_{\rho\sigma}^\lambda-{R^\mu}_{\nu\rho\sigma})\derivative{x^\nu}{s}\derivative{x^\rho}{s}n^{\sigma}=0.

(7.K.26)

Recall that the family of geodesics
\beta(x^{0},v^{i},s)
is described in Fermi normal coordinates by
x^{0}=t
,
x^{i}=v^{i}s
, and thus the deviation vectors are

n_{(i)}^{\mu}=\partialderivative{x^\mu}{v^i}=s\delta_{i}^{\mu},

(7.K.27)

such that
\derivative{s}n_{(i)}^{\mu}=\delta_{i}^{\mu}
and
\derivative[2]{s}n_{(i)}^{\mu}=0
. For that case, the geodesic deviation equation (
7.K.26
) yields

2\Gamma_{ji}^{\mu}v^{j}+s\quantity(\partial_j\Gamma_{ki}^\mu-\Gamma_{\lambda i}^\mu\Gamma_{jk}^\lambda+\Gamma_{j\lambda}^\mu\Gamma_{ki}^\lambda-{R^\mu}_{jki})v^{j}v^{k}=0.

(7.K.28)

In the first term we can expand the Christoffel symbols as

\Gamma_{ji}^{\mu}=\evaluated{\Gamma_{ji}^\mu}_{\gamma}+s\evaluated{\partial_k\Gamma_{ji}^\mu}_{\gamma}v^{k}+O(s^{2}),

(7.K.29)

such that dividing the equation by
s
and then taking the limit
s\to 0
yields

\evaluated{\qty(\partial_j\Gamma_{ki}^\mu+2\partial_k\Gamma_{ji}^\mu-{R^\mu}_{jki})}_{\gamma}v^{j}v^{k}=0,

(7.K.30)

or

\evaluated{\qty(\partial_k\Gamma_{ij}^\mu+\partial_j\Gamma_{ik}^\mu)}_{\gamma}=\evaluated{\frac{1}{3}\qty({R^\mu}_{jki}+{R^\mu}_{ikj})}_{\gamma}.

(7.K.31a)

We may also write the expressions obtained from the one above by cyclic permutations,

\evaluated{\qty(\partial_i\Gamma_{jk}^\mu+\partial_k\Gamma_{ji}^\mu)}_{\gamma}=\evaluated{\frac{1}{3}\qty({R^\mu}_{kij}+{R^\mu}_{jik})}_{\gamma}.

(7.K.31b)

and

\evaluated{\qty(\partial_j\Gamma_{ki}^\mu+\partial_i\Gamma_{kj}^\mu)}_{\gamma}=\evaluated{\frac{1}{3}\qty({R^\mu}_{ijk}+{R^\mu}_{kji})}_{\gamma}.

(7.K.31c)

Adding Eq. (
7.K.31b
) to Eq. (
7.K.31a
) and subtracting Eq. (
7.K.31c
) from it finally yields

\evaluated{\partial_k\Gamma_{ij}^\mu}_{\gamma}=\evaluated{\frac{1}{3}\qty({R^\mu}_{jki}+{R^\mu}_{ikj})}_{\gamma}.

(7.K.32)

Now, for the second derivatives of the metric, we write the metric compatibility condition
\nabla_{l}g_{\mu\nu}=0
as

\partial_{l}g_{\mu\nu}-\Gamma_{l\mu}^{\lambda}g_{\lambda\nu}-\Gamma_{l\nu}^{\lambda}g_{\mu\lambda}=0,

(7.K.33)

differentiate it with respect to
x^{k}
and compute it along
\gamma
to obtain

\evaluated{\partial_k\partial_lg_{\mu\nu}}_{\gamma}=\evaluated{\partial_k\Gamma_{l\mu}^\lambda}_{\gamma}\eta_{\lambda\nu}+\evaluated{\partial_k\Gamma_{l\nu}^\lambda}_{\gamma}\eta_{\mu\lambda}.

(7.K.34)

Then, using Eqs. (
7.K.24
) and (
7.K.32
) gives

\displaystyle\evaluated{\partial_k\partial_lg_{0i}}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\evaluated{\partial_k\Gamma_{l0}^\lambda}_{\gamma}\eta_{\lambda i}+\evaluated{\partial_k\Gamma_{li}^\lambda}_{\gamma}\eta_{0\lambda}

\displaystyle=\evaluated{R_{ilk0}}_{\gamma}+\evaluated{\frac{1}{3}\qty(R_{0ikl}+R_{0lki})}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\evaluated{\frac{1}{3}\qty(3R_{0kli}-R_{0kli}-R_{0lik}+R_{0lki})}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\evaluated{\frac{2}{3}\qty(R_{0kli}+R_{0lki})}_{\gamma},

(7.K.35)

where we used the properties of the Riemann tensor. Similarly we use Eq. (
7.K.32
) to obtain

\displaystyle\evaluated{\partial_k\partial_lg_{ij}}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\evaluated{\partial_k\Gamma_{li}^\lambda}_{\gamma}\eta_{\lambda j}+\evaluated{\partial_k\Gamma_{lj}^\lambda}_{\gamma}\eta_{i\lambda}

\displaystyle=-\evaluated{\frac{1}{3}\qty(R_{ikjl}+R_{iljk})}_{\gamma}.

(7.K.36)

Using the results from Eqs. (
7.K.23
), (
7.K.2
) and (
7.K.2
) in the metric expansion (
7.K.21
), we finally obtain

\displaystyle g_{00}

\displaystyle=-1-\evaluated{R_{i0j0}}_{\gamma}x^{i}x^{j}+O(x^{3}),

(7.K.37a)

\displaystyle g_{0i}

\displaystyle=-\evaluated{\frac{2}{3}R_{0jik}}_{\gamma}x^{j}x^{k}+O(x^{3}),

(7.K.37b)

\displaystyle g_{ij}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}-\evaluated{\frac{1}{3}R_{ikjl}}_{\gamma}x^{k}x^{l}+O(x^{3}).

(7.K.37c)

These are the desired expressions for the metric components written in Fermi normal coordinates
x^{\mu}
up to second order.

Now, before we end this section, let us quickly discuss the concept of the "small enough region" over which this metric expansion is valid. Eqs. (
7.K.37
) make it clear that the expansion is only valid for small values of
x^{i}
, but the natural question that arises is: small compared to
what
?

To answer this, let us note that the metric tensor is dimensionless and, since the Riemann tensor involves second derivatives of
g_{\mu\nu}
, it has the dimension of one over length-squared. We can then introduce the concept of a typical variation scale of the metric, denoted
R_{0}
, such that
R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}\sim O(1/R_{0}^{2})
. One can make this definition precise as, for instance, defining it in terms of the full contraction of the Riemann tensor,
R_{0}^{-4}\propto R^{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}R_{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}
, but such a precise definition is not necessary for our purposes. The important point is that
R_{0}
measures the scale over which the metric changes appreciably. In this sense, the metric expansion (
7.K.37
) is really an expansion over powers of the ratio
x/R_{0}
. This means that Fermi normal coordinates remain valid as long as
x\ll R_{0}
, i.e. it is the curvature itself that determines how "small enough" such region is. For stronger curvatures the smaller
R_{0}
is, and consequently the smaller are the values of
x^{i}
for which Eqs. (
7.K.37
) remain valid.

Chapter 8
Graviton scattering by a Newtonian potential

In this appendix we explicitly compute the differential cross section for a graviton being scattered by a Newtonian source.

Appendix 8.A
The
S-
matrix

In Chapter
1
we showed that the action describing the interaction between gravitational radiation and a classical Newtonian potential is given by Eq. (
4.3
). Here we shall only work with the metric perturbation field in the TT gauge, Eqs. (
4.30
), and so we will denote it by
h_{\mu\nu}
instead of the explicit notation
h^{\rm TT}_{\mu\nu}
since there is no room for confusion. Also, we will rescale the fields
h_{\mu\nu}\to\kappa_{\rm g}h_{\mu\nu}
, with
\kappa_{\rm g}=\sqrt{32\pi}
, so that they are canonically normalized. Hence, the action reads

S_{\rm EH}=\frac{1}{2}\int\differential^{4}x\,\quantity(h_{ij}\Box h^{ij}+2\phi\,h_{ij}\delta_{\mu\nu}\partial^\mu\partial^\nu h^{ij}),

(8.A.1)

with
\phi(\mathbf{x})
denoting the time-independent gravitational potential generated by a Newtonian source of mass
M_{N}
. Our goal now is to obtain the probability for a graviton to be scattered by an angle
\theta
.

As discussed in Chapter
1
, the (canonical) quantization of gravitational radiation is done by writing the field operator

\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}(x)=\int\frac{\differential^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\omega_{p}}}\sum_{s}\quantity[\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^s(p)\Hat{a}_{p,s}e^{ipx}+{\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^s}^*(p)\Hat{a}^\dagger_{p,s}e^{-ipx}],

(8.A.2)

where
p^{\mu}=\quantity(\omega_p,\vb{p})
and
\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s}
denotes the polarization tensors. The annihilation and creation operators satisfy the commutation relations

\commutator{\hat{a}_{p,s}}{\hat{a}_{p',s'}^\dagger}=(2\pi)^{3}\delta_{ss^{\prime}}\delta^{3}(\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{p}^{\prime}).

(8.A.3)

Now, consider an initial single graviton state
\ket{i}
at
t=-\infty
, with momentum
p_{1}
and polarization
s_{1}
, sufficiently far from the source of the gravitational potential
\phi
. After the interaction, one ends up with a final single graviton state
\ket{f}
at
t=+\infty
, with momentum
p_{2}
and polarization
s_{2}
. Suppose that the potential
\phi
drops to zero sufficiently fast at infinity, so that we can consider the interaction dynamics to happen in some finite time interval and the theory to be free at
t\to\pm\infty
. The transition probability has the form

P_{\rm transition}=\frac{\absolutevalue{\braket{f}{i}}^{2}}{\innerproduct{f}{f}\innerproduct{i}{i}},

(8.A.4)

where the overlap
\innerproduct{f}{i}
is a
S-
matrix element with

\displaystyle\ket{i}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\omega_{p_{1}}}\hat{a}_{p_{1},s_{1}}^{\dagger}(-\infty)\ket{\Omega},

(8.A.5a)

\displaystyle\ket{f}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\omega_{p_{2}}}\hat{a}_{p_{2},s_{2}}^{\dagger}(+\infty)\ket{\Omega},

(8.A.5b)

and
\ket{\Omega}
is the vacuum state of the interacting theory. Explicitly, we have

\innerproduct{f}{i}=\sqrt{2\omega_{p_{1}}}\sqrt{2\omega_{p_{2}}}\matrixelement{\Omega}{\Hat{a}_{p_2,s_2}(+\infty)\Hat{a}_{p_1,s_1}^\dagger(-\infty)}{\Omega}.

(8.A.6)

It is interesting to notice that the operators inside brackets are in time order, so we can write this expression in a more convenient way as

\displaystyle\innerproduct{f}{i}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\omega_{p_{1}}}\sqrt{2\omega_{p_{2}}}

\displaystyle\times\matrixelement{\Omega}{\textrm{T}\qty{\qty[\hat{a}_{p_2,s_2}(+\infty)-\hat{a}_{p_2,s_2}(-\infty)]\qty[\hat{a}_{p_1,s_1}^\dagger(-\infty)-\hat{a}_{p_1,s_1}^\dagger(+\infty)]}}{\Omega},

(8.A.7)

where T stands for the usual time ordering operation. This is essentially the same expression we had since time ordering sends the unwanted
\hat{a}_{p_{2},s_{2}}(-\infty)
to the left, where it annihilates
\ket{\Omega}
, and the unwanted
\hat{a}_{p_{1},s_{1}}^{\dagger}(+\infty)
to the right, where it annihilates
\bra{\Omega}
. This new expression will prove to be useful since we can relate it to the field operators as follows.

Assuming that the field dies off at
\mathbf{x}=\pm\infty
, we can write

\displaystyle i\int\differential^{4}x\,e^{-ipx}\Box\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}(x)

\displaystyle=i\int\differential^{4}x\,e^{-ipx}\quantity(-\partial_t^2+\laplacian)\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}(x)

\displaystyle=-i\int\differential^{4}x\,e^{-ipx}\quantity(\partial_t^2+\omega_p^2)\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}(x),

(8.A.8)

where
\omega_{p}^{2}=\mathbf{p}^{2}
for a massless particle such as the graviton. Now, note that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\quantity[e^{-ipx}\qty(i\partial_t+\omega_p)\Hat{h}_{\mu\nu}(x)]

\displaystyle=\quantity[i\omega_pe^{-ipx}\qty(i\partial_t+\omega_p)+e^{-ipx}\qty(i\partial_t^2+\omega_p\partial_t)]\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}(x)

\displaystyle=ie^{-ipx}\quantity(\partial_t^2+\omega_p^2)\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}(x),

(8.A.9)

and, therefore,

\displaystyle i\int\differential^{4}x\,e^{-ipx}\Box\hat{h}_{\mu\nu}(x)=-\int\differential^{4}x\,\partial_{t}\quantity[e^{-ipx}\qty(i\partial_t+\omega_p)\Hat{h}_{\mu\nu}(x)]

\displaystyle=-\evaluated{e^{i\omega_pt}\int\dd^3x\,e^{-i\vb{p}\vdot\vb{x}}\qty(i\partial_t+\omega_p)\Hat{h}_{\mu\nu}(x)}_{t=-\infty}^{t=+\infty}

\displaystyle=-e^{i\omega_{p}t}\int\differential^{3}x\,e^{-i\mathbf{p}\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}\int\frac{\differential^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\sum_{r}\left[\quantity(\frac{\omega_k+\omega_p}{\sqrt{2\omega_k}})\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{r}(\mathbf{k})\hat{a}_{k,r}(t)e^{ik\cdot x}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\evaluated{\left. +\qty(\frac{-\omega_k+\omega_p}{\sqrt{2\omega_k}}){\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^r}^*(\vb{k})\Hat{a}^\dagger_{k,r}(t)e^{-ik\cdot x}\right] }_{t=-\infty}^{t=+\infty}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{2\omega_{p}}\sum_{r}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{r}(\mathbf{p})\quantity[\Hat{a}_{p,r}(+\infty)-\Hat{a}_{p,r}(-\infty)].

(8.A.10)

Here we used the fact that
\evaluated{\partial_t\Hat{a}_{k,r}(t)}_{t=-\infty}^{t=+\infty}=0
since the theory is assumed to be free at
t\to\pm\infty
. Lastly, if we contract both sides of the equation above with
\epsilon^{\mu\nu}_{s}(p)
and use the polarization tensors orthonormality condition (
2.31
), we arrive at

\sqrt{2\omega_{p}}\quantity[\Hat{a}_{p,s}(+\infty)-\Hat{a}_{p,s}(-\infty)]=-\frac{i}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s}(\mathbf{p})\int\differential^{4}x\,e^{-ipx}\Box\hat{h}^{\mu\nu}(x).

(8.A.11)

Using Eq. (
8.A.11
) in (
8.A
) leads to

\displaystyle\innerproduct{f}{i}=\quantity[-\frac{i}{2}{\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_1}}^*(\vb{p}_1)\int\dd^4x_1\,e^{ip_1x_1}\Box_1]\quantity[-\frac{i}{2}{\epsilon_{\rho\sigma}^{s_2}}(\vb{p}_2)\int\dd^4x_2\,e^{-ip_2x_2}\Box_2]

\displaystyle\times\matrixelement{\Omega}{\textrm{T}\qty{\Hat{h}^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)}}{\Omega},

(8.A.12)

where
\Box_{j}
denotes the d’Alembertian with respect to
44
44

44

Here the index
j
is not labeling components of a three dimensional vector, but simply different integration variables.

x_{j}
. This is the
LSZ reduction formula
for gravitons, which relates
S-
matrix elements (left-hand side) to the Green functions involving the quantum fields (right-hand side)
[
149
]
. Hence, in order to compute the transition probability, we are going to need the time-ordered two-point function.

Appendix 8.B
The time-ordered two-point function

8.B.1
The free graviton propagator

We will begin with the simplest case, namely the time-ordered two-point function of the free theory, also called the
Feynman propagator
for gravitons. It is defined by

D^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(x_{1}-x_{2})=\matrixelement{0}{\textrm{T}\qty{\Hat{h}_0^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}_0^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)}}{0},

(8.B.1)

where
\hat{h}_{0}^{\mu\nu}(x)
is the free field operator and
\ket{0}
represents the vacuum of the free theory. From the plane-wave expansion of the field operator we find

\displaystyle\matrixelement{0}{\Hat{h}_0^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}_0^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)}{0}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\differential^{3}p_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{\differential^{3}p_{2}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\omega_{p_{1}}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\omega_{p_{2}}}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\epsilon^{\mu\nu}_{s_{1}}(\mathbf{p}_{1}){\epsilon^{\rho\sigma}_{s_{2}}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{2})\matrixelement{0}{\Hat{a}_{p_1,s_1}\Hat{a}_{p_2,s_2}^\dagger}{0}e^{i(p_{1}x_{1}-p_{2}x_{2})},

(8.B.2)

or, by using the commutation relations for the creation and annihilation operators,

\matrixelement{0}{\Hat{h}_0^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}_0^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)}{0}=\int\frac{\differential^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2\omega_{p}}A^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\mathbf{p})e^{ip(x_{1}-x_{2})},

(8.B.3)

where

A^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\mathbf{p})\equiv\sum_{s}\epsilon^{\mu\nu}_{s}(\mathbf{p}){\epsilon^{\rho\sigma}_{s}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}).

(8.B.4)

For the propagator, we have

\displaystyle D^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(x_{1}-x_{2})=\matrixelement{0}{\textrm{T}\qty{\Hat{h}_0^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}_0^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)}}{0}

\displaystyle=\matrixelement{0}{\Hat{h}_0^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}_0^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)}{0}\theta(t_{1}-t_{2})+\matrixelement{0}{\Hat{h}_0^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)\Hat{h}_0^{\mu\nu}(x_1)}{0}\theta(t_{2}-t_{1})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\differential^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2\omega_{p}}\quantity[A^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\vb{p})e^{-i\omega_p\tau}\theta(\tau)+A^{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(-\vb{p})e^{i\omega_p\tau}\theta(-\tau)]e^{i\mathbf{p}\dotproduct(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2})},

(8.B.5)

where
\tau\equiv t_{1}-t_{2}
and
\theta(t)
is the Heaviside step function. Now,
\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(-\mathbf{p})=\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
and thus

A^{\rho\sigma\mu\nu}(-\mathbf{p})=A^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\mathbf{p}).

(8.B.6)

We can also use the result

e^{-i\omega_{p}\tau}\theta(\tau)+e^{i\omega_{p}\tau}\theta(-\tau)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{-2\omega_{p}}{2\pi i}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\differential\omega}{\omega^{2}-\omega_{p}^{2}+i\varepsilon}e^{i\omega\tau}

(8.B.7)

to finally arrive at

D^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(x_{1}-x_{2})=\int\frac{\differential^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}A^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\mathbf{p})\frac{-i}{p^{2}+i\varepsilon}e^{-ip(x_{1}-x_{2})}.

(8.B.8)

8.B.2
Interacting theory graviton propagator

In quantum field theory, we can relate vacuum expectation values of time-ordered products in the interacting theory to those in the free theory by
[
149
,
129
]

\matrixelement{\Omega}{\textrm{T}\qty{\Hat{h}^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)}}{\Omega}=\frac{\matrixelement{0}{\textrm{T}\qty{\Hat{h}_0^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}_0^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)e^{i\int\dd^4x\,\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}[\Hat{h}_0]}}}{0}}{\matrixelement{0}{\textrm{T}\qty{e^{i\int\dd^4x\,\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}[\Hat{h}_0]}}}{0}},

(8.B.9)

where the interaction is described by the Lagrangian density
\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}
. In our case,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}[h(x)]

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu\nu}\phi(\mathbf{x})h_{ij}(x)\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}h^{ij}(x)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\mu\nu}\phi(\mathbf{x})\eta_{\alpha\gamma}\eta_{\beta\delta}h^{\alpha\beta}(x)\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}h^{\gamma\delta}(x),

(8.B.10)

which we wrote in a more convenient way.

Now, let us denote the free graviton propagator by
D_{12}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\equiv D^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(x_{1}-x_{2})
. Also, let
\int_{x}\equiv\int\differential^{4}x
and
\delta^{4}_{xy}\equiv\delta^{4}(x-y)
. Then we can write

\displaystyle\matrixelement{0}{\textrm{T}\qty{\Hat{h}_0^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}_0^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)e^{i\int\dd^4x\,\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}[\Hat{h}_0]}}}{0}=D_{12}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle-i\eta_{\alpha\gamma}\eta_{\beta\delta}\int_{x}\int_{y}\phi(\mathbf{x})\delta^{4}_{xy}\delta^{\mu_{y}\nu_{y}}\partial_{\mu_{y}}\partial_{\nu_{y}}\matrixelement{0}{\textrm{T}\qty{\Hat{h}_0^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}_0^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)\Hat{h}_0^{\alpha\beta}(x)\Hat{h}_0^{\gamma\delta}(y)}}{0},

(8.B.11)

up to first order in
\phi
. We may then use Wick’s theorem to rewrite the time-ordered four-point function so as to obtain
[
149
,
151
,
129
]

\displaystyle\matrixelement{0}{\textrm{T}\qty{\Hat{h}_0^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}_0^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)e^{i\int\dd^4x\,\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}[\Hat{h}_0]}}}{0}=D_{12}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle-i\eta_{\alpha\gamma}\eta_{\beta\delta}\int_{x}\int_{y}\phi(\mathbf{x})\delta^{4}_{xy}\delta^{\mu_{y}\nu_{y}}\partial_{\mu_{y}}\partial_{\nu_{y}}\quantity(D_{12}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}D_{xy}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}+D_{1x}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}D_{2y}^{\rho\sigma\gamma\delta}+D_{1y}^{\mu\nu\gamma\delta}D_{2x}^{\rho\sigma\alpha\beta}).

(8.B.12)

Similarly,

\matrixelement{0}{\textrm{T}\qty{e^{i\int\dd^4x\,\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}[\Hat{h}_0]}}}{0}=1-i\eta_{\alpha\gamma}\eta_{\beta\delta}\int_{x}\int_{y}\phi(\mathbf{x})\delta^{4}_{xy}\delta^{\mu_{y}\nu_{y}}\partial_{\mu_{y}}\partial_{\nu_{y}}D_{xy}^{\alpha\beta\gamma\delta}.

(8.B.13)

The first term on the second line of Eq. (
8.B.2
), proportional to
D_{12}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
, corresponds to a Feynman diagram that includes what are usually called bubbles. However, as we can see from Eq. (
8.B.13
), and from the fact that
\frac{1}{1+kx}=1-kx+O(k^{2})
, the bubble diagram gets canceled out, leaving us with

\displaystyle\matrixelement{\Omega}{\textrm{T}\qty{\Hat{h}^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)}}{\Omega}=D_{12}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle-2i\eta_{\alpha\gamma}\eta_{\beta\delta}\int_{x}\int_{y}\phi(\mathbf{x})\delta^{4}_{xy}\delta^{\mu_{y}\nu_{y}}\partial_{\mu_{y}}\partial_{\nu_{y}}D_{1x}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}D_{2y}^{\rho\sigma\gamma\delta}.

(8.B.14)

Using the expression for the free graviton propagator, Eq. (
8.B.8
), we arrive at
45
45

45

We are now leaving the
i\varepsilon
prescription implicit.

\displaystyle\matrixelement{\Omega}{\textrm{T}\qty{\Hat{h}^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)}}{\Omega}=\int\frac{\differential^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}A^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\mathbf{p})\frac{-i}{p^{2}}e^{-ip(x_{1}-x_{2})}

\displaystyle-2i\int\frac{\differential^{4}p}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{\differential^{4}p^{\prime}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\tilde{\phi}(-p-p^{\prime})B^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{p}^{\prime})\frac{\omega^{\prime 2}+\mathbf{p}^{\prime 2}}{p^{2}p^{\prime 2}}e^{-ipx_{1}}e^{-ip^{\prime}x_{2}},

(8.B.15)

where

B^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{p}^{\prime})\equiv\eta_{\alpha\gamma}\eta_{\beta\delta}A^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}(\mathbf{p})A^{\rho\sigma\gamma\delta}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})

(8.B.16)

and
\tilde{\phi}(q)
is the four-dimensional Fourier transform of the Newtonian potential.

Appendix 8.C
The differential cross section

With the expression for the time-ordered two-point function at hand, let us now return to the
S-
matrix elements via the reduction formula, Eq. (
8.A
). Explicitly,

\displaystyle\innerproduct{f}{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{4}{\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_{1}}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1}){\epsilon_{\rho\sigma}^{s_{2}}}(\mathbf{p}_{2})\int\differential^{4}x_{1}\differential^{4}x_{2}\,e^{ip_{1}x_{1}}e^{-ip_{2}x_{2}}\Box_{1}\Box_{2}

\displaystyle\times\quantity(i\mel{\Omega}{\textrm{T}\qty{\Hat{h}^{\mu\nu}(x_1)\Hat{h}^{\rho\sigma}(x_2)}}{\Omega})

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{4}{\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_{1}}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1}){\epsilon_{\rho\sigma}^{s_{2}}}(\mathbf{p}_{2})p_{1}^{2}A^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\mathbf{p}_{1})(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(p_{1}-p_{2})

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{4}{\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_{1}}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1}){\epsilon_{\rho\sigma}^{s_{2}}}(\mathbf{p}_{2})2\tilde{\phi}(p_{2}-p_{1})B^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\mathbf{p}_{1},-\mathbf{p}_{2})(\omega_{2}^{2}+\mathbf{p}_{2}^{2}).

(8.C.1)

Then, by using the normalization condition (
2.31
), direct calculations give

{\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_{1}}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}){\epsilon_{\rho\sigma}^{s_{2}}}(\mathbf{p})A^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\mathbf{p})=4\delta_{s_{1},s_{2}},

(8.C.2a)

and

{\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_{1}}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1}){\epsilon_{\rho\sigma}^{s_{2}}}(\mathbf{p}_{2})B^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}(\mathbf{p}_{1},-\mathbf{p}_{2})=4\epsilon_{s_{1}}^{\mu\nu}(\mathbf{p}_{1})\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_{2}}(\mathbf{p}_{2}).

(8.C.2b)

Since the graviton is massless, we have
p_{1}^{2}=p_{2}^{2}=0
, and hence

\absolutevalue{\braket{f}{i}}^{2}=\absolutevalue{4\Tilde{\phi}(p_2-p_1)\epsilon_{s_1}^{\mu\nu}(\vb{p}_1)\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_2}(\vb{p}_2)}^{2}\omega_{2}^{4}.

(8.C.3)

Additionally, we know that

\displaystyle\innerproduct{i}{i}

\displaystyle=2\omega_{1}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{3}(0)=2\omega_{1}V,

(8.C.4a)

\displaystyle\innerproduct{f}{f}

\displaystyle=2\omega_{2}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{3}(0)=2\omega_{2}V,

(8.C.4b)

where we are denoting
V=(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{3}(0)
, a (formally infinite) regularization volume. Therefore, the transition probability becomes

P_{\rm transition}=\frac{4}{V^{2}}\absolutevalue{\Tilde{\phi}(p_2-p_1)\epsilon_{s_1}^{\mu\nu}(\vb{p}_1)\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_2}(\vb{p}_2)}^{2}\frac{\omega_{2}^{3}}{\omega_{1}}.

(8.C.5)

Now take the Newtonian potential to be given by
\phi(r)=-M_{N}/r
. Then

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}(p_{2}-p_{1})

\displaystyle=-M_{N}\int\differential^{4}x\,\frac{1}{r}e^{i(p_{1}-p_{2})x}

\displaystyle=-M_{N}\int\differential t\,e^{-it(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})}\int\differential^{3}x\,\frac{1}{r}e^{i(\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2})\dotproduct\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=-4\pi M_{N}\frac{1}{(\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2})^{2}}(2\pi)\delta(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}),

(8.C.6)

and

P_{\rm transition}=\frac{128\pi^{3}TM_{N}^{2}}{V^{2}}\absolutevalue{\frac{1}{(\vb{p}_1-\vb{p}_2)^2}\epsilon_{s_1}^{\mu\nu}(\vb{p}_1)\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_2}(\vb{p}_2)}^{2}\frac{\omega_{2}^{3}}{\omega_{1}}\delta(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2}),

(8.C.7)

with
T=(2\pi)\delta(0)
(formally infinite) being the total time for the scattering process.

The differential cross section is defined by

\differential\sigma=\frac{1}{T}\frac{1}{\Phi}\differential P,

(8.C.8)

where
\Phi=1/V
is the incoming flux,
\differential P
is the quantum mechanical differential probability,

\differential P=P_{\rm transition}\differential\Pi,

(8.C.9)

and
\differential\Pi
is the region of final momenta at which we are looking. For one outgoing particle with momentum
\mathbf{p}_{2}
, we have

\differential\Pi=\frac{V}{(2\pi)^{3}}\differential^{3}p_{2},

(8.C.10)

defined such that
\int\differential\Pi=1
. Since
\differential^{3}p_{2}=\absolutevalue{\vb{p}_2}^{2}\differential\absolutevalue{\vb{p}_2}\differential\Omega
, we write

\derivative{\sigma}{\Omega}=\frac{V}{T}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\absolutevalue{\vb{p}_2}\,\frac{V}{(2\pi)^{3}}\absolutevalue{\vb{p}_2}^{2}P_{\rm transition}(\absolutevalue{\vb{p}_2}).

(8.C.11)

By plugging Eq. (
8.C.7
) into (
8.C.11
) we find

\displaystyle\derivative{\sigma}{\Omega}

\displaystyle=16M_{N}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\omega_{2}\frac{1}{(\omega_{1}^{2}+\omega_{2}^{2}-2\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\cos\theta)^{2}}\frac{\omega_{2}^{5}}{\omega_{1}}\delta(\omega_{1}-\omega_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\frac{1}{8}\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\absolutevalue{\epsilon_{s_1}^{\mu\nu}(\vb{p}_1)\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_2}(\vb{p}_2)}^{2},

(8.C.12)

where
\theta
is the angle between
\mathbf{p}_{1}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}
and we averaged over the polarizations in order to obtain the unpolarized differential cross section
46
46

46

Note that, due to our normalization convention,
\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\absolutevalue{\epsilon_{s_1}^{\mu\nu}(\vb{p})\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_2}(\vb{p})}^{2}=8
.
. We can compute the sum over polarizations as follows.

Letting
u
and
w
denote the spatial polarization unit vectors orthogonal to the direction of propagation,
\mathbf{p}/\absolutevalue{\vb{p}}
, the polarization tensors can be written as

\displaystyle\epsilon_{ij}^{+}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=u_{i}u_{j}-w_{i}w_{j},

(8.C.13a)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{ij}^{\crossproduct}(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=u_{i}w_{j}+w_{i}u_{j}.

(8.C.13b)

Then, we explicitly find

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ij}_{+}(\mathbf{p}_{1})\epsilon_{ij}^{+}(\mathbf{p}_{2})

\displaystyle=(u_{1}\dotproduct u_{2})^{2}+(w_{1}\dotproduct w_{2})^{2}-(u_{1}\dotproduct w_{2})^{2}-(w_{1}\dotproduct u_{2})^{2},

(8.C.14a)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ij}_{\crossproduct}(\mathbf{p}_{1})\epsilon_{ij}^{\crossproduct}(\mathbf{p}_{2})

\displaystyle=2(u_{1}\dotproduct u_{2})(w_{1}\dotproduct w_{2})+2(u_{1}\dotproduct w_{2})(w_{1}\dotproduct u_{2}),

(8.C.14b)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ij}_{\crossproduct}(\mathbf{p}_{1})\epsilon_{ij}^{+}(\mathbf{p}_{2})

\displaystyle=2(u_{1}\dotproduct u_{2})(w_{1}\dotproduct u_{2})-2(u_{1}\dotproduct w_{2})(w_{1}\dotproduct w_{2}),

(8.C.14c)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{ij}_{+}(\mathbf{p}_{1})\epsilon_{ij}^{\crossproduct}(\mathbf{p}_{2})

\displaystyle=2(u_{1}\dotproduct u_{2})(u_{1}\dotproduct w_{2})-2(w_{1}\dotproduct u_{2})(w_{1}\dotproduct w_{2}),

(8.C.14d)

where
u_{1}
,
w_{1}
(
u_{2}
,
w_{2}
) are the unit vectors orthogonal to
\mathbf{p}_{1}
(
\mathbf{p}_{2}
).

Figure 19:
Vectors
\mathbf{p}_{1}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}
and their corresponding spatial polarization orthogonal unit vectors.

Choosing the systems
\quantity(u_1,w_1,\frac{\vb{p}_1}{\abs{\vb{p}_1}})
and
\quantity(u_2,w_2,\frac{\vb{p}_2}{\abs{\vb{p}_2}})
as the ones represented in Figure
19
, so that the angle between
\mathbf{p}_{1}
and
\mathbf{p}_{2}
is
\theta
, we find

\displaystyle u_{1}\dotproduct u_{2}

\displaystyle=\cos\theta,

(8.C.15a)

\displaystyle w_{1}\dotproduct w_{2}

\displaystyle=1,

(8.C.15b)

\displaystyle u_{1}\dotproduct w_{2}=w_{1}\dotproduct u_{2}

\displaystyle=0,

(8.C.15c)

and thus, after some amount of trigonometric manipulations,

\displaystyle\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}}\absolutevalue{\epsilon_{s_1}^{\mu\nu}(\vb{p}_1)\epsilon_{\mu\nu}^{s_2}(\vb{p}_2)}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1+\cos^{2}\theta)^{2}+4\cos^{2}\theta

\displaystyle=8\quantity(\cos^8\frac{\theta}{2}+\sin^8\frac{\theta}{2}).

(8.C.16)

Using this result in Eq. (
8.C
) and computing the integral with the delta function finally leads to the differential cross section

\derivative{\sigma}{\Omega}=\frac{M_{N}^{2}}{\sin^{4}\frac{\theta}{2}}\quantity(\cos^8\frac{\theta}{2}+\sin^8\frac{\theta}{2}).

(8.C.17)

We can restore factors of
G
and
c
by noticing that the differential cross section has dimensions of area and letting
\derivative*{\sigma}{\Omega}\to(1/L_{\rm P}^{2})\derivative*{\sigma}{\Omega}
and
M_{N}\to M_{N}/M_{\rm P}
, with the Planck length and mass written in terms of the universal constants. The result is

\derivative{\sigma}{\Omega}=\frac{G^{2}M_{N}^{2}}{c^{4}\sin^{4}\frac{\theta}{2}}\quantity(\cos^8\frac{\theta}{2}+\sin^8\frac{\theta}{2}).

(8.C.18)

This differential cross section matches the one computed for scattering of
classical
gravitational waves by a Newtonian potential
[
164
]
.

Chapter 9
Computing the noise kernels

Appendix 9.A
Gravitational noise kernel

In this section, we compute the noise kernel encoding the influence of the quantized gravitational field, defined by Eq. (
14.13b
), for different initial states. In what follows we shall consider an unpolarized and isotropic gravitational wave background such that the position operators are independent of the polarizations and of the direction of
\mathbf{k}
, that is,
\hat{q}_{s}(t,\mathbf{k})=\hat{q}_{\omega}(t)
,
\omega=\absolutevalue{\vb{k}}
. Within this assumption, we may write the gravitational noise kernel as

\displaystyle N_{\rm g}^{ijkl}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{32}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\omega\,\omega^{2}\left[A_{\Omega}^{ijkl}\derivative[2]{t}\derivative[2]{{t'}}G_{\omega}(t,t^{\prime})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+A_{\Omega}^{ijnl}{\Phi_{n}}^{k}\derivative[2]{t}G_{\omega}(t,t^{\prime})+A_{\Omega}^{njkl}{\Phi_{n}}^{i}\derivative[2]{{t'}}G_{\omega}(t,t^{\prime})\right],

(9.A.1)

where

G_{\omega}(t,t^{\prime})=\expectationvalue{\acomm{\hat{q}_\omega(t)}{\hat{q}_\omega(t')}}_{\rm g}

(9.A.2)

is the Hadamard function, and the tensor structure is encoded in the angular integral given by

A_{\Omega}^{ijkl}\equiv\int\differential\Omega\sum_{s}\epsilon_{s}^{ij}(\mathbf{k})\epsilon_{s}^{kl}(\mathbf{k}).

(9.A.3)

Let us begin by explicitly evaluating the angular integral, since it does not depend on the initial graviton state. First, write the polarization tensors as in Eq. (
2.29
),

\begin{split}\epsilon_{ij}^{+}&=\hat{\epsilon}_{i}^{(1)}\hat{\epsilon}_{j}^{(1)}-\hat{\epsilon}_{i}^{(2)}\hat{\epsilon}_{j}^{(2)}\\
\epsilon_{ij}^{\crossproduct}&=\hat{\epsilon}_{i}^{(1)}\hat{\epsilon}_{j}^{(2)}+\hat{\epsilon}_{i}^{(2)}\hat{\epsilon}_{j}^{(1)},\end{split}

(9.A.4)

where the spatial polarization unit vectors
\hat{\epsilon}^{(1)}
and
\hat{\epsilon}^{(2)}
are orthogonal to the direction of propagation
\hat{k}=\mathbf{k}/\absolutevalue{\vb{k}}
. They satisfy

\hat{\epsilon}_{i}^{(1)}\hat{\epsilon}_{j}^{(1)}+\hat{\epsilon}_{i}^{(2)}\hat{\epsilon}_{j}^{(2)}=\delta_{ij}-\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}\equiv P_{ij}.

(9.A.5)

Then, a straightforward calculation yields

\sum_{s}\epsilon_{s}^{ij}\epsilon_{s}^{kl}=P^{ik}P^{jl}+P^{il}P^{jk}-P^{ij}P^{kl}.

(9.A.6)

We can use the following integration over solid angles in three spatial dimensions:

\int\differential\Omega\,\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}=\frac{4\pi}{3}\delta_{ij},

(9.A.7a)

\int\differential\Omega\,\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}\hat{k}_{k}\hat{k}_{l}=\frac{4\pi}{15}\quantity(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}),

(9.A.7b)

to obtain

\int\differential\Omega\,P_{ij}P_{kl}=\frac{8\pi}{5}\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\frac{4\pi}{15}\quantity(\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}).

(9.A.8)

At last, we find

A_{\Omega}^{ijkl}=\frac{8\pi}{15}\quantity[3\qty(\delta^{ik}\delta^{jl}+\delta^{il}\delta^{jk})-2\delta^{ij}\delta^{kl}].

(9.A.9)

In Chapter
5
we argue that only the component
N_{\rm g}^{3333}(t,t^{\prime})\equiv N_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})
will be of interest for the specific decoherence functions we work with, and so we shall focus on it from now on for simplicity. From Eq. (
9.A.9
) we have
A_{\Omega}^{3333}=32\pi/15
, and thus

N_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{\pi}{15}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\omega\,\omega^{2}\quantity{\dv[2]{t}\dv[2]{{t'}}G_\omega(t,t')+\Phi_{zz}\qty[\dv[2]{t}G_\omega(t,t')+\dv[2]{{t'}}G_\omega(t,t')]}.

(9.A.10)

Here we lowered the index of the tidal tensor and dropped higher order terms in
\phi
as usual.

Next, let us compute the Hadamard function. Since the free Hamiltonian for each mode of the gravitational field is that of a harmonic oscillator, Eq. (
13.21
), we may write the position operators in the Heisenberg picture as

\hat{q}_{\omega}(t)=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2m_{\rm g}\omega}}\quantity(\hat{a}_\omega e^{-i\omega t}+\hat{a}_\omega^\dagger e^{i\omega t}),

(9.A.11)

where the
\hat{a}
’s (
\hat{a}^{\dagger}
’s) are annihilation (creation) operators satisfying the usual commutation relations. A direct calculation then yields

G_{\omega}(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{2}{m_{\rm g}\omega^{2}}\langle\hat{H}_{\omega}\rangle_{\rm g}\cos\omega(t-t^{\prime})+\frac{1}{m_{\rm g}\omega}\quantity[\langle\hat{a}_\omega^2\rangle_{\rm g}\,e^{-i\omega(t+t')}+\langle\qty(\hat{a}^\dagger_\omega)^2\rangle_{\rm g}\,e^{i\omega(t+t')}],

(9.A.12)

where
\hat{H}_{\omega}
is the free Hamiltonian operator of the harmonic oscillator with frequency
\omega
. We may now compute the noise kernel for different initial states of the gravitational field.

9.A.1
Vacuum state

If the initial state is the vacuum state, we have
\langle\hat{a}_{\omega}^{2}\rangle_{\rm g}=\langle\quantity(\hat{a}^\dagger_\omega)^{2}\rangle_{\rm g}=0
and
\langle\hat{H}_{\omega}\rangle_{\rm g}=\omega/2
, and thus

G_{\omega}^{\rm(vac)}(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{m_{\rm g}\omega}\cos{\omega(t-t')}.

(9.A.13)

Plugging this Hadamard function into Eq. (
9.A.10
) leads to

\displaystyle N_{\rm g}^{\rm(vac)}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{15\pi}\quantity[\int_0^\Lambda\dd\omega\,\omega^5\cos{\omega(t-t')}-2\Phi_{zz}\int_0^\Lambda\dd\omega\,\omega^3\cos{\omega(t-t')}]

\displaystyle=\frac{2\Lambda^{4}}{15\pi}\quantity{\Lambda^2F_5\qty[\Lambda(t-t')]-2\Phi_{zz}F_3\qty[\Lambda(t-t')]},

(9.A.14)

where
\Lambda
is the graviton energy cutoff (see Section
13
) and we used
m_{\rm g}=\pi^{2}/2
. Also, we defined

F_{n}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{x^{n+1}}\int_{0}^{x}\differential y\,y^{n}\cos y.

(9.A.15)

Explicitly,

F_{5}(x)=\frac{1}{x^{6}}\left[\quantity(5x^4-60x^2+120)\cos x+x\quantity(x^4-20x^2+120)\sin x-120\right],

(9.A.16a)

and

F_{3}(x)=\frac{1}{x^{4}}\quantity[\qty(3x^2-6)\cos x+\qty(x^3-6x)\sin x+6].

(9.A.16b)

Now, from Eq. (
15
) we see that there are contributions to the external DoFs density matrix that are proportional to the product of the gravitational and the internal DoFs noise kernels. In Section
9.B
we will explicitly compute the latter, modeling it as a white noise. Therefore we will be interested in the coincidence limit
t^{\prime}\to t
for the gravitational noise kernel. From

\lim_{x\to 0}F_{n}(x)=\frac{1}{n+1},

(9.A.17)

it follows that

N^{\rm(vac)}_{\rm g}(t)=\lim_{t^{\prime}\to t}N^{\rm(vac)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{\Lambda^{4}}{15\pi}\quantity(\frac{\Lambda^2}{3}-\Phi_{zz}).

(9.A.18)

9.A.2
Thermal state

Let us now consider the gravitons to be initially in a thermal state with temperature
T_{\textrm{g}}=\beta_{\textrm{g}}^{-1}
(which, as we discussed in Section
10
, is a parameter that is not necessarily tied to a precise thermodynamical definition). In that case we have
\langle\hat{a}_{\omega}^{2}\rangle_{\rm g}=\langle\quantity(\hat{a}^\dagger_\omega)^{2}\rangle_{\rm g}=0
once again, and

\langle\hat{H}_{\omega}\rangle_{\rm g}=\omega\quantity(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{e^{\omega\beta_{\rm g}}-1}).

(9.A.19)

Plugging into Eq. (
9.A.12
) leads to

G_{\omega}^{\rm(th)}(t,t^{\prime})=G_{\omega}^{\rm(vac)}(t,t^{\prime})+\frac{2}{m_{\rm g}\omega}\frac{1}{e^{\omega/T_{\rm g}}-1}\cos{\omega(t-t')}.

(9.A.20)

The noise kernel is found to be

\displaystyle N^{\rm(th)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=N^{\rm(vac)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{15\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\omega\quantity[\frac{\omega^5}{e^{\omega/T_{\rm g}}-1}\cos\omega(t-t')-2\Phi_{zz}\frac{\omega^3}{e^{\omega/T_{\rm g}}-1}\cos\omega(t-t')]

\displaystyle=N^{\rm(vac)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\frac{8(\pi T_{\rm g})^{4}}{5\pi}\quantity{10(\pi T_{\rm g})^2F_1^{(\rm th)}\qty[\pi T_{\rm g}(t-t')]-\Phi_{zz}F_2^{(\rm th)}\qty[\pi T_{\rm g}(t-t')]},

(9.A.21)

with

F_{1}^{(\rm th)}(x)=\frac{1}{x^{6}}-\frac{1}{15\sinh^{6}x}\quantity(2\cosh^4x+11\cosh^2x+2),

(9.A.22a)

and

F_{2}^{(\rm th)}(x)=\frac{1}{3\sinh^{4}x}\quantity(2\cosh^2x+1)-\frac{1}{x^{4}}.

(9.A.22b)

Also, using
\lim_{x\to 0}F_{1}^{(\rm th)}(x)=2/945
and
\lim_{x\to 0}F_{2}^{(\rm th)}(x)=1/45
, we find

N^{\rm(th)}_{\rm g}(t)=\lim_{t^{\prime}\to t}N^{\rm(th)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})=N^{\rm(vac)}_{\rm g}(t)+\frac{8(\pi T_{\rm g})^{4}}{45\pi}\quantity(\frac{4}{21}\pi^2T_{\rm g}^2-\frac{1}{5}\Phi_{zz}).

(9.A.23)

9.A.3
Coherent state

In quantum optics, the coherent states are those whose electric field variation approaches that of a classical electromagnetic wave. They are important because a single-mode laser usually generates a coherent state excitation
[
104
]
. In complete analogy, one may say that a coherent graviton state is the quantum-mechanical state whose properties most closely resemble those of a classical gravitational wave.

For a quantum harmonic oscillator, the coherent state is defined by

\ket{\alpha_\omega}=\hat{\mathscr{D}}(\alpha_{\omega})\ket{0},

(9.A.24)

where
\hat{\mathscr{D}}(\alpha_{\omega})
is called the
coherent-state displacement operator
, defined as
[
104
]

\mathscr{D}(\alpha_{\omega})=\exp(\alpha_\omega\hat{a}_\omega^\dagger-\alpha_\omega^*\hat{a}_\omega).

(9.A.25)

Here we take the displacement parameter to be independent of
\omega
,
\alpha_{\omega}=\alpha
, for simplicity. The displacement operator satisfies a number of properties,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathscr{D}}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\hat{\mathscr{D}}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=1,

(9.A.26a)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathscr{D}}^{\dagger}(\alpha)\hat{a}_{\omega}^{n}\hat{\mathscr{D}}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=(\hat{a}_{\omega}+\alpha)^{n},

(9.A.26b)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathscr{D}}^{\dagger}(\alpha)(\hat{a}_{\omega}^{\dagger})^{n}\hat{\mathscr{D}}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=(\hat{a}_{\omega}^{\dagger}+\alpha^{*})^{n},

(9.A.26c)

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, from which we find

\displaystyle\langle\hat{a}_{\omega}^{2}\rangle_{\rm g}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2},

(9.A.27a)

\displaystyle\langle(\hat{a}_{\omega}^{\dagger})^{2}\rangle_{\rm g}

\displaystyle=(\alpha^{*})^{2},

(9.A.27b)

\displaystyle\langle\hat{H}_{\omega}\rangle_{\rm g}

\displaystyle=\omega\quantity(\abs{\alpha}^2+\frac{1}{2}),

(9.A.27c)

where we also used the fact that the coherent state is an eigenstate of the annihilation operator,
\hat{a}_{\omega}\ket{\alpha}=\alpha\ket{\alpha}
.

For simplicity, let us further assume that the displacement parameter is real. In that case, we find

G_{\omega}^{\rm(coh)}(t,t^{\prime})=G_{\omega}^{\rm(vac)}(t,t^{\prime})+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{m_{\rm g}\omega}\cos(\omega t)\cos(\omega t').

(9.A.28)

For the noise kernel, one has

\displaystyle N^{\rm(coh)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=N^{\rm(vac)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})+\frac{2\alpha^{2}}{15\pi}\left[\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\differential\omega\,\omega^{5}\cos(\omega t)\cos(\omega t')\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\left.-2\Phi_{zz}\int_{0}^{\Lambda}\differential\omega\,\omega^{3}\cos(\omega t)\cos(\omega t')\right]

\displaystyle=N^{\rm(vac)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})+\frac{\alpha^{2}\Lambda^{4}}{15\pi}\left\{\Lambda^{2}\quantity[F_5\qty(\Lambda(t+t'))+F_5\qty(\Lambda(t-t'))]\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\left.-2\Phi_{zz}\quantity[F_3\qty(\Lambda(t+t'))+F_3\qty(\Lambda(t-t'))]\right\},

(9.A.29)

where the functions
F_{n}(x)
are defined in Eq. (
9.A.15
). By using Eq. (
9.A.17
), we also find

\displaystyle N^{\rm(coh)}_{\rm g}(t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t^{\prime}\to t}N^{\rm(coh)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=N^{\rm(vac)}_{\rm g}(t)+\frac{\alpha^{2}\Lambda^{4}}{15\pi}\left\{\Lambda^{2}\quantity[F_5\qty(2\Lambda t)+\frac{1}{6}]-2\Phi_{zz}\quantity[F_3\qty(2\Lambda t)+\frac{1}{4}]\right\}.

(9.A.30)

9.A.4
Squeezed state

Returning once again to the analogy with quantum optics, a squeezed state is the one for which the electric field has a quantum uncertainty smaller than the minimal uncertainty of a coherent state for some phases. For a weak quantized gravitational field, it has been argued that gravitons created from quantum fluctuations in the course of cosmological evolution should now be in strongly squeezed states
[
73
]
.

For a quantum harmonic oscillator, the squeezed state is defined by

\ket{\zeta_\omega}=\hat{\mathscr{S}}(\zeta_{\omega})\ket{0},

(9.A.31)

where
\hat{\mathscr{S}}(\zeta_{\omega})
is called the
squeeze operator
, defined as
[
104
]

\hat{\mathscr{S}}(\zeta_{\omega})=\exp[\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{\omega}^{*}\hat{a}_{\omega}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{\omega}(\hat{a}_{\omega}^{\dagger})^{2}],

(9.A.32)

and
\zeta_{\omega}
is the
complex squeeze parameter

\zeta_{\omega}=r_{\omega}e^{i\varphi_{\omega}}.

(9.A.33)

Here we take the squeeze parameter to be independent of
\omega
,
\zeta_{\omega}=\zeta
. The squeeze operator satisfies a number of properties,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathscr{S}}^{\dagger}(\zeta)\hat{\mathscr{S}}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=1,

(9.A.34a)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathscr{S}}^{\dagger}(\zeta)\hat{a}_{\omega}^{n}\hat{\mathscr{S}}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=(\hat{a}\cosh r-\hat{a}^{\dagger}e^{i\varphi}\sinh r)^{n},

(9.A.34b)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathscr{S}}^{\dagger}(\zeta)(\hat{a}_{\omega}^{\dagger})^{n}\hat{\mathscr{S}}(\zeta)

\displaystyle=(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\cosh r-\hat{a}\,e^{-i\varphi}\sinh r)^{n},

(9.A.34c)

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, from which we find

\displaystyle\langle\hat{a}_{\omega}^{2}\rangle_{\rm g}

\displaystyle=-e^{i\varphi}\sinh r\cosh r,

(9.A.35a)

\displaystyle\langle(\hat{a}_{\omega}^{\dagger})^{2}\rangle_{\rm g}

\displaystyle=-e^{-i\varphi}\sinh r\cosh r,

(9.A.35b)

\displaystyle\langle\hat{H}_{\omega}\rangle_{\rm g}

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega}{2}\cosh 2r,

(9.A.35c)

so that

G_{\omega}^{\rm(sq)}(t,t^{\prime})=(\cosh{2r})G_{\omega}^{\rm(vac)}(t,t^{\prime})-\frac{\sinh{2r}}{m_{\rm g}\omega}\cos[\omega(t+t^{\prime})-\varphi].

(9.A.36)

Assuming the squeeze parameter to be real for simplicity from now on (
\varphi=0
), the noise kernel becomes

\displaystyle N^{\rm(sq)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\cosh 2r)N^{\rm(vac)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{15\pi}\sinh 2r\quantity[\int_0^\Lambda\omega^5\cos\omega(t+t')-2\Phi_{zz}\int_0^\Lambda\omega^3\cos\omega(t+t')]

\displaystyle=(\cosh 2r)N^{\rm(vac)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})

\displaystyle-\frac{2\Lambda^{4}}{15\pi}\sinh 2r\quantity{\Lambda^2F_5\qty[\Lambda(t+t')]-2\Phi_{zz}F_3\qty[\Lambda(t+t')]}.

(9.A.37)

Also,

\displaystyle N^{\rm(sq)}_{\rm g}(t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t^{\prime}\to t}N^{\rm(sq)}_{\rm g}(t,t^{\prime})
