\displaystyle=(\cosh 2r)N^{\rm(vac)}_{\rm g}(t)-\frac{2\Lambda^{4}}{15\pi}\sinh 2r\quantity[\Lambda^2F_5\qty(2\Lambda t)-2\Phi_{zz}F_3\qty(2\Lambda t)].

(9.A.38)

Appendix 9.B
Internal DoFs noise kernel

In this section, we obtain an explicit expression for the internal degrees of freedom noise kernel, defined in Eq. (
15.17b
). Recall that the free Lagrangian for each position operator
\hat{\varrho}_{\alpha}
is that of a harmonic oscillator. Furthermore, we take the initial internal state to be a thermal one with temperature
T_{\rm int}=\beta_{\rm int}^{-1}
. In that case, the noise kernel is precisely the one we obtained in Eq. (
7.27b
), namely

N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\varpi\,J(\varpi)\coth\quantity(\frac{\varpi\beta_{\rm int}}{2})\cos\varpi(t-t^{\prime}),

(9.B.1)

where

J(\varpi)=\sum_{\alpha}\frac{\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}}{\mu_{\alpha}\varpi_{\alpha}}\delta(\varpi-\varpi_{\alpha})

(9.B.2)

is the spectral density.

If we consider the simple case of the internal DoFs being described by an Ohmic bath, we have

J(\varpi)=\eta\varpi

(9.B.3)

for some effective coupling constant
\eta
. For an Ohmic bath, the noise kernel reads

N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\eta\int_{0}^{\infty}\differential\varpi\,\varpi\coth\quantity(\frac{\varpi\beta}{2})\cos\varpi(t-t^{\prime}).

(9.B.4)

At high temperatures,
T_{\rm int}=\beta_{\rm int}^{-1}\gg\absolutevalue{t-t'}
, the integral is dominated by low frequencies (and one does need to introduce a frequency cutoff in such case), and we find

N_{\rm int}(t,t^{\prime})=\eta\pi T_{\rm int}\delta(t-t^{\prime}),

(9.B.5)

namely a white noise.

Chapter 10
The fluctuation theorem

The term
fluctuation theorems
stands for relations between probability distributions of fluctuating non-equilibrium quantities and some kind of equilibrium information of starting and ending points of a given thermodynamical process. In this appendix we present the fluctuation theorem following the approach due to Gavin E. Crooks
[
48
]
, which in turn leads to the Jarzynski equality
[
84
]
. We begin by presenting its classical version before the quantum one. The reader is referred to Ref.
[
30
]
for a review and a brief historical description of the field.

Appendix 10.A
Classical fluctuation theorem

The fluctuation theorem follows from two basic assumptions: (i) the initial description of the system by a thermal state and (ii) the time reversal invariance of the equations of microscopic motion. Before presenting the theorem itself, we shall begin by making this last assumption more precise, which we call
microreversibility
. We will assume Hamiltonian dynamics, although the results remain valid for a more general Markovian stochastic evolution
[
86
,
94
]
.

10.A.1
Microreversibility of driven classical systems

Consider a classical system described by a Hamiltonian

H(\mathbf{z},f)=H_{0}(\mathbf{z})-f(t)X(\mathbf{z}),

(10.A.1)

where
H_{0}(\mathbf{z})
is referred to as the unperturbed Hamiltonian,
f(t)
is a time-dependent external force and
X(\mathbf{z})
is its conjugate coordinate. Also, we are denoting the points in phase space by
\mathbf{z}=\quantity(\vb{q},\vb{p})
, where
\mathbf{q}
represents the generalized coordinates
(q_{1},\dots,q_{n})
of a system with
n
degrees of freedom while
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})
stands for their conjugated momenta. We assume that the force acts within a temporal interval
\quantity[0,\tau]
and that its instantaneous values
f(t)
are specified by a given force protocol.

The solution of Hamilton’s equations of motion assigns to each initial point
\mathbf{z}(0)
in phase space a point
\mathbf{z}(t)
at a later time
t\in[0,\tau]
, which is specified by the values of the force protocol in all times prior to
t
, that is

\mathbf{z}(t)=\varphi_{t,0}[\mathbf{z}(0);f],

(10.A.2)

where
\varphi
represents the Hamiltonian flow in phase space
[
30
]
.

In order to write the microreversibility condition we need to describe the time reversed process, which is a trajectory that runs
forward
in time such that the signs of momenta are reverted. We first assume that the unperturbed Hamiltonian
H_{0}
is time reversal invariant. Additionally, we also assume the conjugate coordinate
X
to transform under time reversal with a definite parity
\epsilon_{X}=\pm 1
. Now, while the flow (
10.A.2
) describes how the system evolves from
\mathbf{z}_{0}
under the forward protocol
f(t)
, we define the reverse flow

\varphi_{\tau-t}[\epsilon\mathbf{z}(\tau);\epsilon_{X}\tilde{f}]

(10.A.3)

as the one describing how the system evolves from the time-reversed final state
\epsilon\mathbf{z}(\tau)
, where
\epsilon\mathbf{z}=\epsilon\quantity(\vb{q},\vb{p})=\quantity(\vb{q},-\vb{p})
, under the reverse protocol
\tilde{f}(t)=f(\tau-t)
.

The principle of microreversibility states that the trajectory followed in the forward process, when time-reversed, must coincide with the trajectory followed in the reverse process. Therefore, applying the time-reversal operation to the forward trajectory leads to
[
30
,
154
]

\epsilon\varphi_{\tau-t,0}[\epsilon\mathbf{z}(\tau);\epsilon_{X}\tilde{f}]=\varphi_{t,0}[\mathbf{z}(0);f].

(10.A.4)

10.A.2
Crooks fluctuation theorem

When moving from
\mathbf{z}(0)
at
t=0
to
\mathbf{z}(\tau)
at
t=\tau
, the work done on the system is given by
47
47

47

This definition is referred to as
inclusive work
in the literature, as opposed to the
exclusive work
defined by
W_{0}=H_{0}(\mathbf{z}(\tau))-H_{0}(\mathbf{z}(0))
. To see how the two approaches relate to each other the reader is referred to refs.
[
78
,
86
]
.

W[\mathbf{z}(0);f]=H(\mathbf{z}(\tau),f(\tau))-H(\mathbf{z}(0),f(0)).

(10.A.5)

Let us note that

\displaystyle\derivative{t}H(\mathbf{z},f)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\quantity(\pdv{H}{q_i}\dot{q}_i+\pdv{H}{p_i}\dot{p}_i)+\partialderivative{H}{f}\dot{f}=\dot{f}(t)\partialderivative{H(\vb{z},f)}{f(t)}

\displaystyle=-\dot{f}(t)X(\mathbf{z}(t)),

(10.A.6)

where we used Hamilton’s equation of motion
[
70
]

\dot{q}_{i}=\partialderivative{H}{p_i},\hskip 28.45274pt\dot{p}_{i}=-\partialderivative{H}{q_i}.

(10.A.7)

Eq. (
10.A.5
) can then be written as

W[\mathbf{z}(0);f]=\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential t\,\dot{f}(t)\partialderivative{H(\vb{z},f)}{f(t)}=-\int_{0}^{\tau}\differential t\,\dot{f}(t)X(\mathbf{z}(t)).

(10.A.8)

At
t=0
we assume the system to be in thermal equilibrium at inverse temperature
\beta
described by a canonical distribution of the form

\rho(\mathbf{z}(0))=\frac{e^{-\beta H(\mathbf{z}(0),f(0))}}{Z_{0}},

(10.A.9)

where

Z_{0}=\int\differential\mathbf{z}(0)\,e^{-\beta H(\mathbf{z}(0),f(0))}

(10.A.10)

is the classical partition function.

The initial conditions are randomly sampled from the distribution (
10.A.9
), each of which leads to a trajectory in phase space from
t=0
to
t=\tau
. Since the definition of work (
10.A.8
) depends on the trajectory, it acquires a probabilistic aspect when considering the ensemble of processes with initial preparation (
10.A.9
). The complete information concerning the statistics is then contained in the work probability density function

P[W;f]=\int\differential\mathbf{z}(0)\rho(\mathbf{z}(0))\delta\quantity[W-H(\vb{z}(\tau),f(\tau))+H(\vb{z}(0),f(0))].

(10.A.11)

As usual, we define the characteristic function of work as the Fourier transform of the work probability density function,

\Phi[u;f]=\int\differential W\,e^{iuW}P[W;f],

(10.A.12a)

with

P[W;f]=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\differential u\,e^{-iuW}\Phi[u;f].

(10.A.12b)

By using Eqs. (
10.A.11
), (
10.A.9
) and (
10.A.5
) the characteristic function becomes

\displaystyle\Phi[u;f]

\displaystyle=\int\differential\mathbf{z}(0)\,e^{iu\quantity[H(\vb{z}(\tau),f(\tau))-H(\vb{z}(0),f(0))]}\frac{e^{-\beta H(\mathbf{z}(0),f(0))}}{Z_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z_{0}}\int\differential\mathbf{z}(0)\,e^{iuW[\mathbf{z}(0);f]}e^{-\beta H(\mathbf{z}(\tau),f(\tau))}e^{\beta W[\mathbf{z}(0);f]}

\displaystyle=\frac{Z_{\tau}}{Z_{0}}\int\differential\mathbf{z}(0)\,e^{iuW\quantity[\varphi_{\tau,0}[\vb{z}(0);f]]}e^{\beta W\quantity[\varphi_{\tau,0}[\vb{z}(0);f]]}\rho(\mathbf{z}(\tau)),

(10.A.13)

where
Z_{\tau}=\int\differential\mathbf{z}(\tau)\,e^{-\beta H(\mathbf{z}(\tau),f(\tau))}
. In the expression above,
W\quantity[\varphi_{\tau,0}[\vb{z}(0);f]]
denotes the work done on the system from
t=0
to
t=\tau
in the forward process. By using the microreversibility condition (
10.A.4
) and changing variables
\mathbf{z}(0)\to\mathbf{z}(\tau)
(for which the Jacobian is equal to unity since time evolution in classical mechanics is a canonical transformation), we find

\displaystyle Z_{0}\Phi[u;f]

\displaystyle=Z_{\tau}\int\differential\mathbf{z}(\tau)\,e^{iuW\quantity[\epsilon\varphi_{\tau-t,0}[\epsilon\vb{z}(\tau);\epsilon_X\Tilde{f}]]}e^{\beta W\quantity[\epsilon\varphi_{\tau-t,0}[\epsilon\vb{z}(\tau);\epsilon_X\Tilde{f}]]}\rho(\mathbf{z}(\tau))

\displaystyle=Z_{\tau}\int\differential\mathbf{z}(\tau)\,e^{i(u-i\beta)\quantity[H(\vb{z}(0),f(0))-H(\vb{z}(\tau),f(\tau))]}\rho(\mathbf{z}(\tau)),

(10.A.14)

where
W\quantity[\epsilon\varphi_{\tau-t,0}[\epsilon\vb{z}(\tau);\epsilon_X\Tilde{f}]]=H(\mathbf{z}(0),f(0))-H(\mathbf{z}(\tau),f(\tau))
is the work done on the reverse process starting at
t=\tau
with initial thermal distribution
\rho(\mathbf{z}(\tau))
. By introducing a delta function to recover the characteristic function of work, we find

Z_{0}\Phi[u;f]=Z_{\tau}\Phi[-u+i\beta;\epsilon_{X}\tilde{f}].

(10.A.15)

Applying the inverse Fourier transform on both sides yields

Z_{0}P[W;f]=Z_{\tau}e^{\beta W}P[-W;\epsilon_{X}\tilde{f}],

(10.A.16)

or

\frac{P[W;f]}{P[-W;\epsilon_{X}\tilde{f}]}=\frac{Z_{\tau}}{Z_{0}}e^{\beta W}.

(10.A.17)

We can rewrite this by recalling that the Helmholtz free energy is obtained from the partition function as
F(t)=-\frac{1}{\beta}\ln Z_{t}

[
123
]
and thus

\frac{Z_{\tau}}{Z_{0}}=\frac{e^{-\beta F(\tau)}}{e^{-\beta F(0)}}=e^{-\beta\Delta F},

(10.A.18)

where
\Delta F=F(\tau)-F(0)
is the free energy difference between initial and final points. Finally we may write

\frac{P[W;f]}{P[-W;\epsilon_{X}\tilde{f}]}=e^{\beta(W-\Delta F)}.

(10.A.19)

This is the Crooks fluctuation theorem, which establishes that the probability of observing the forward process is exponentially larger than the probability of observing the reverse one. The exponent
W_{\rm diss}=W-\Delta F
, called
dissipated work
, quantifies such statistical irreversibility.

10.A.3
Jarzynski equality

We can write Crooks fluctuation theorem (
10.A.19
) as

P[W;f]e^{-\beta W}=P[-W;\epsilon_{X}\tilde{f}]e^{-\beta\Delta F}

(10.A.20)

and integrate both sides over
W
. This leads to the Jarzynski equality

\expectationvalue{e^{-\beta W}}=e^{-\beta\Delta F},

(10.A.21)

where the brackets denote average with respect to the work probability density function.

The Jarzynski equality (
10.A.21
) states that the equilibrium free energy difference may be inferred by measurements of non-equilibrium work in many realizations of the system trajectory according to the force protocol
f(t)
. This result has many practical applications in the fields of physics, biology, chemistry and so on
[
85
,
30
]
. It also allows us to describe the statistical nature of the second law of thermodynamics by using Jensen’s inequality
48
48

48

Let
X
be a real integrable random variable and let
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a convex function such that
Y=g(X)
is also integrable. Then Jensen’s inequality states that
E\quantity[g(X)]\geq g\quantity(E[X])
, where
E[X]
denotes the expectation value of
X
.
,

e^{-\beta\Delta F}=\expectationvalue{e^{-\beta W}}\geq e^{-\beta\expectationvalue{W}}\implies e^{-\beta(\expectationvalue{W}-\Delta F)}\leq 1.

(10.A.22)

This implies that, on average, the entropy production is always positive

\expectationvalue{\Sigma}=\beta(\expectationvalue{W}-\Delta F)\geq 0.

(10.A.23)

Also, as we can see from Eq. (
10.A.19
),
\expectationvalue{\Sigma}=0
holds only for reversible processes.

Appendix 10.B
Quantum fluctuation theorem

On establishing the quantum version of the fluctuation theorem, as expressed by Crooks relation and Jarzynski equality, one is faced with an immediate question: how to define work done on quantum systems? Work describes a specific process instead of characterizing the state of the system. Thus it cannot be represented by a Hermitian operator whose eigenvalues can be determined in a single projective measurement
[
157
,
30
]
. For open quantum systems the proper definition is still under debate, but consensus seems to have been reached on how to define work done on a closed quantum system according to the two-time measurements scheme. In this approach, we measure the energy of the system twice, first at
t=0
and then at
t=\tau
, the end point of the force protocol. The difference of the outcomes of these two measurements is the work performed on the system in a particular realization. This inevitably yields a second probabilistic aspect to the work distribution, which comes from quantum uncertainty.

Before we make these ideas more precise, we follow the approach of the previous section and turn now to the question of microreversibility of driven
quantum
systems.

10.B.1
Quantum microreversibility

Let
\hat{U}_{f}(t,0)
be the unitary time evolution operator of the quantum system driven by the force protocol
f(t)
from
t=0
to
t=\tau
. In order to write the quantum microreversibility condition, we split the time interval in discrete steps and express the time evolution operator as a time ordered product,

\hat{U}_{\tilde{f}}(\tau-t,0)=\lim_{N\to\infty}e^{-i\hat{H}(\tilde{f}(\tau-N\varepsilon))\varepsilon}\dots e^{-i\hat{H}(\tilde{f}(\varepsilon))\varepsilon}e^{-i\hat{H}(\tilde{f}(0))\varepsilon},

(10.B.1)

where
\varepsilon=t/N
denotes the time step and
\hat{H}(f(t))
is the Hamiltonian operator. By recalling that
\tilde{f}(t)=f(\tau-t)
we may write

\hat{U}_{\tilde{f}}(\tau-t,0)=\lim_{N\to\infty}e^{-i\hat{H}(f(N\varepsilon))\varepsilon}\dots e^{-i\hat{H}(f(\tau-\varepsilon))\varepsilon}e^{-i\hat{H}(f(\tau))\varepsilon}.

(10.B.2)

Next let us introduce the time reversal operator
\hat{\Theta}
and assume that, at any time
t
, the Hamiltonian is invariant under time reversal,

\commutator{\hat{H}(f(t))}{\hat{\Theta}}=0.

(10.B.3)

Then,

\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{\tilde{f}}(\tau-t,0)\hat{\Theta}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}e^{-i\hat{H}(f(N\varepsilon))\varepsilon}\hat{\Theta}\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}\dots\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}e^{-i\hat{H}(f(\tau))\varepsilon}\hat{\Theta},

(10.B.4)

where we made
N-1
insertions of the identity
1=\hat{\Theta}\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}
. Since
\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}e^{-i\hat{H}(f(t))\varepsilon}\hat{\Theta}=e^{i\hat{H}(f(t))\varepsilon}
for real
\varepsilon

[
144
]
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{\tilde{f}}(\tau-t,0)\hat{\Theta}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}e^{i\hat{H}(f(N\varepsilon))\varepsilon}\dots e^{i\hat{H}(f(\tau))\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\quantity[e^{-i\hat{H}(f(\tau))\varepsilon}\dots e^{-i\hat{H}(f(N\varepsilon))\varepsilon}]^{\dagger}=\hat{U}_{f}^{\dagger}(\tau,t)

(10.B.5)

and the microreversibility condition reads

\hat{U}_{f}(t,\tau)=\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{\tilde{f}}(\tau-t,0)\hat{\Theta}.

(10.B.6)

Now, note that Eq. (
10.B.6
) can be rewritten as

\hat{U}_{f}(t,0)=\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{\tilde{f}}(\tau-t,0)\hat{\Theta}\hat{U}_{f}(\tau,0),

(10.B.7)

where we used
\hat{U}_{f}(t,\tau)=\hat{U}_{f}(t,0)\hat{U}_{f}(0,\tau)=\hat{U}_{f}(t,0)\hat{U}_{f}^{\dagger}(\tau,0)
. Next, let us apply this operator to the pure initial state
\ket{\psi_0}
,

\ket{\psi_t}=\hat{U}_{f}(t,0)\ket{\psi_0}=\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{\tilde{f}}(\tau-t,0)\hat{\Theta}\hat{U}_{f}(\tau,0)\ket{\psi_0}=\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{\tilde{f}}(\tau-t,0)\hat{\Theta}\ket{\psi_\tau},

(10.B.8)

where
\ket{\psi_\tau}=\hat{U}_{f}(\tau,0)\ket{\psi_0}
is the final state. Multiplying by
\hat{\Theta}
from the left leads to

\hat{\Theta}\ket{\psi_t}=\hat{U}_{\tilde{f}}(\tau-t,0)\hat{\Theta}\ket{\psi_\tau}.

(10.B.9)

This allows us to interpret the quantum microreversibility in terms of states: under the evolution generated by the reverse protocol
\tilde{f}
, the time reversed final state
\hat{\Theta}\ket{\psi_\tau}
evolves from
0
to
\tau-t
to the state
\hat{\Theta}\ket{\psi_t}

[
30
]
.

10.B.2
Work probability density function

Consider a system whose time evolution is generated by the Hamiltonian operator
\hat{H}(f(t))
initially described by the thermal density matrix

\hat{\rho}(0)=\frac{e^{-\beta\hat{H}(f(0))}}{Z_{0}},

(10.B.10)

where
Z_{0}=\textrm{Tr}\,e^{-\beta\hat{H}(f(0))}
is the partition function and Tr denotes trace over the Hilbert space of the system. The Hamiltonian eigenvalue equation can be written as

\hat{H}(f(t))\ket{\psi_{n,\alpha}(t)}=E_{n}(t)\ket{\psi_{n,\alpha}(t)},

(10.B.11)

where
n
labels the quantum numbers that specify the energy eigenvalues while
\alpha
labels all further quantum numbers.

Let us now describe the two-time measurement scheme from which we will define the work done on the system. At
t=0
we measure the energy of the system, which yields the eigenvalue
E_{n}(0)
with probability

p_{n}(0)=\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_n^0\hat{\rho}(0)].

(10.B.12)

Here,
\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{0}=\sum_{\alpha}\ket{\psi_{n,\alpha}(0)}\bra{{\psi_{n,\alpha}(0)}}
is the projector onto the eigenspace spanned by the eigenvectors associated with the eigenvalue
E_{n}(0)
. Once the energy measurement is performed, with result
E_{n}(0)
, the system is described by the state

\hat{\rho}_{n}=\frac{\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{0}\hat{\rho}(0)\hat{\Pi}_{n}^{0}}{p_{n}(0)}.

(10.B.13)

Since we are considering the system to be closed, the time evolution is unitary,

\hat{\rho}_{n}(t;f)=\hat{U}_{f}(t,0)\hat{\rho}_{n}\hat{U}_{f}^{\dagger}(t,0).

(10.B.14)

At time
t=\tau
a second energy measurement yields the eigenvalue
E_{m}(\tau)
with probability

p_{m|n}^{\tau}[f]=\textrm{Tr}\quantity[\hat{\Pi}_m^\tau\hat{\rho}_n(\tau;f)].

(10.B.15)

The work done on the system is then simply the difference between the energy eigenvalues,
w=E_{m}(\tau)-E_{n}(0)
. The probability distribution function of work reads

P[w;f]=\sum_{m,n}\delta\quantity[w-E_m(\tau)+E_n(0)]p_{m|n}^{\tau}[f]p_{n}^{0}.

(10.B.16)

10.B.3
Quantum versions of the Crooks fluctuation relation and Jarzynski equality

From the probability distribution function of work (
10.B.16
) we write the characteristic function as
[
156
]

\displaystyle\Phi[u;f]

\displaystyle=\int\differential w\,e^{iuw}P[w;f]

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,n}e^{iuE_{m}(\tau)}e^{-iuE_{n}(0)}\textrm{Tr}\quantity{\hat{\Pi}_m^\tau\hat{U}_f(\tau,0)\hat{\rho}_n\hat{U}_f^\dagger(\tau,0)}p_{n}^{0}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity{e^{iu\hat{H}(f(\tau))}\sum_m\hat{\Pi}_m^\tau\hat{U}_f(\tau,0)e^{-iu\hat{H}(f(0))}\sum_n\hat{\rho}_n p_n^0\hat{U}_f^\dagger(\tau,0)}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity{\hat{U}_f^\dagger(\tau,0)e^{iu\hat{H}(f(\tau))}\hat{U}_f(\tau,0)e^{-iu\hat{H}(f(0))}\hat{\rho}(0)},

(10.B.17)

where we used the fact that
\sum_{m}\hat{\Pi}_{m}^{\tau}=1
and
\sum_{n}\hat{\rho}_{n}p_{n}^{0}=\hat{\rho}(0)
, which holds since the initial density matrix commutes with the Hamiltonian at
t=0
.

By using the explicit form of the initial density matrix, Eq. (
10.B.10
), we can write

Z_{0}\Phi[u;f]=\textrm{Tr}\quantity{\hat{U}_f^\dagger(\tau,0)e^{iu\hat{H}(f(\tau))}\hat{U}_f(\tau,0)e^{-iu\hat{H}(f(0))}e^{-\beta\hat{H}(f(0))}}.

(10.B.18)

Setting
t=0
in the quantum microreversibility condition (
10.B.6
) leads to

\hat{U}_{f}(0,\tau)=\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{\tilde{f}}(\tau,0)\hat{\Theta}\implies\hat{U}_{f}^{\dagger}(\tau,0)=\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{\tilde{f}}(\tau,0)\hat{\Theta},

\implies\hat{U}_{f}(\tau,0)=\hat{\Theta}^{\dagger}\hat{U}_{\tilde{f}}^{\dagger}(\tau,0)\hat{\Theta}.

(10.B.19)

Using this expression back in Eq. (
10.B.18
) results in

\displaystyle Z_{0}\Phi[u;f]

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity{\hat{\Theta}^\dagger\hat{U}_{\Tilde{f}}(\tau,0)\hat{\Theta} e^{iu\hat{H}(f(\tau))}\hat{\Theta}^\dagger\hat{U}_{\Tilde{f}}^\dagger(\tau,0)\hat{\Theta} e^{-iu\hat{H}(f(0))}e^{-\beta\hat{H}(f(0))}}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity{\hat{\Theta}^\dagger\hat{U}_{\Tilde{f}}(\tau,0)e^{-iu^*\hat{H}(f(\tau))}\hat{U}_{\Tilde{f}}^\dagger(\tau,0)e^{iu^*\hat{H}(f(0))}e^{-\beta\hat{H}(f(0))}\hat{\Theta}}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity{e^{-\beta\hat{H}(f(0))}e^{-iu\hat{H}(f(0))}\hat{U}_{\Tilde{f}}(\tau,0)e^{iu\hat{H}(f(\tau))}\hat{U}_{\Tilde{f}}^\dagger(\tau,0)}

\displaystyle=\textrm{Tr}\quantity{\hat{U}_{\Tilde{f}}^\dagger(\tau,0)e^{i(-u+i\beta)\hat{H}(f(0))}\hat{U}_{\Tilde{f}}(\tau,0)e^{-i(-u+i\beta)\hat{H}(f(\tau))}e^{-\beta\hat{H}(f(\tau))}}

\displaystyle=Z_{\tau}\Phi[-u+i\beta;\tilde{f}],

(10.B.20)

where we used the anti-linearity
49
49

49

For any trace class operator
\hat{A}
the anti-linearity of
\hat{\Theta}
implies
\textrm{Tr}\quantity(\hat{\Theta}^\dagger\hat{A}\hat{\Theta})=\textrm{Tr}\hat{A}^{\dagger}
.
of
\Theta
. This result is precisely the same identity we obtained for the classical case, Eq. (
10.A.15
). If we follow the same steps as before we are led to the quantum version of the Crooks fluctuation theorem,

\frac{P[w;f]}{P[-w;\tilde{f}]}=e^{\beta(w-\Delta F)},

(10.B.21)

from which we also obtain the quantum Jarzynski equality

\expectationvalue{e^{-\beta w}}=e^{-\beta\Delta F}.

(10.B.22)
